# Architecture Decisions

Running log of decisions made during the project, why they were made, and what broke.
Each entry has a date and a status (standing / revised / abandoned).

---

## D-001 — ModernBERT-base as encoder baseline
**Date:** Phase 5  
**Status:** Planned

**Decision:** Use `answerdotai/ModernBERT-base` (150M params, 8192 context) as the encoder classification baseline.

**Why:** 8192 context window handles longer trajectories than standard BERT. Fast to train (~1 hour on T4). Sets the accuracy floor cheaply.

**Risk:** 8192 tokens still won't fit full multi-turn trajectories with complete tool schemas. State compression is required. See D-003.

---

## D-002 — Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct as decoder model
**Date:** Phase 6  
**Status:** Planned

**Decision:** Fine-tune `Qwen/Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct` with LoRA (rank 16–64 on attention layers).

**Why:** Strong pretrained priors on tool-calling and code from Qwen2.5 training. 1.5B fits on Colab T4 with LoRA. LoRA keeps VRAM under 12 GB comfortably.

**Risk:** The decoder's latency advantage over an encoder disappears if trajectory compression differs. Compare at matched context lengths.

---

## D-003 — State compression strategy (TBD)
**Date:** Phase 5  
**Status:** Open — to be decided during training

**Decision:** TBD. Options being evaluated:
1. Schema summarization — reduce tool schemas to name + parameter names only
2. Sliding window — last N turns + first turn
3. Structured state extraction — key-value pairs from tool results
4. Turn importance scoring — weight corrections and recent turns higher

**Will document:** Which strategy was chosen, what was tried and abandoned, what the token count distribution looks like after compression.

---

## D-004 — Two corruption sources for synthetic negatives
**Date:** Phase 3  
**Status:** Revised (2026-10-09) — Gemini dropped per D-006

**Decision:** Use two distinct sources for corrupting passing trajectories into labeled negatives: (1) OpenAI GPT-5-nano/mini, (2) rule-based programmatic corruption.

**Why:** If one model generates all negatives, the classifier learns that model's fingerprint — not the actual failure signal. Two sources (LLM + programmatic) provides diversity at low cost.

**Risk:** All LLM corruptions come from one vendor (OpenAI). Mitigated by also generating programmatic corruptions for Classes 4 and 7, which together cover ~30% of real observed errors (Phase 1 data).

**Gemini dropped:** Per D-006 (Phase 0 results), Gemini was removed from the project to simplify the pipeline. GPT-5-nano/mini are the sole LLM corruption sources.

---

## D-005 — Phase 0 as hard project kill gate
**Date:** Phase 0  
**Status:** Active

**Decision:** If GPT-5-nano is already strong (>85% accuracy) on multi-turn tool-call verification, stop the project. The thesis is dead.

**Why:** The entire project rests on the headroom between frontier-as-judge and a small specialist. No headroom = no project.

**What "strong" means:** >85% binary accuracy AND <20% per-class accuracy variance across the 7 failure classes. High average with one class at 40% is not strong — it still leaves room.

---

## D-006 — Phase 0 baseline results
**Date:** 2026-10-03  
**Status:** Standing

**Decision:** Project confirmed viable. Both frontier judges rubber-stamp calls; trained specialist has clear headroom.

**Results:**

| Model | n | Accuracy | Recall (bad calls) | False-flag rate | Pred-correct rate | p50 latency |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| GPT-5-nano | 210 | 47.4% | 18.3% | 23.8% | 79.0% | 3.35s |
| GPT-5-mini | 210 | 49.0% | 22.9% | 24.8% | 76.2% | 3.59s |

- **Accuracy** = fraction of labels matching ground truth  
- **Recall (bad calls)** = fraction of heuristically-incorrect calls the judge flagged as incorrect  
- **Pred-correct rate** = fraction of calls the judge predicted as correct (regardless of truth)

**Why this confirms the thesis:** Both judges default to "correct" ~77–79% of the time even with explicit system prompts describing the 7 failure modes. Recall on genuinely bad calls is ~18–23% — barely above random for the bad-call class. A specialist trained to detect specific failure patterns has large headroom (>60 pp recall improvement available).

**By domain (accuracy):**
- Retail: 54% (nano), 54% (mini)  
- Airline: 57% (nano), 59% (mini)  
- Telecom: 30% (nano), 34% (mini) ← worst; telecom tasks are longer and more complex

**Other decisions made during Phase 0:**
- Used tau2-bench precomputed results (not re-run), saving ~$50 in API cost; precomputed transcripts are representative since we're evaluating agent reasoning, not re-running the benchmark
- Excluded `transfer_to_human_agents` from candidate calls: this tool is always a valid action when the agent is stuck, so it produces near-zero-information labels
- Heuristic label = "exact match + reward fallback + wrong_argument_value tier" for inclusion in candidate pool; ~50% false positive rate on exact match alone, but acceptable for a manual-review candidate pool
- Used `reasoning_effort="low"` for all Phase 0 calls: 3× faster, 2–4× cheaper, same verdicts on sample test
- Dropped Gemini per user preference; kept OpenAI nano + mini as the two baselines

### Correction (2026-11-11): the judges were re-scored against the hand labels

**What was wrong.** The table above scored the judges against the Phase 0 heuristic labels. D-010 later measured those labels at 24.5% precision (25 genuine errors among the 102 "incorrect" rows that were hand-reviewed). So the 18-23% "recall on bad calls" and the 47-49% accuracy were measured against labels that were mostly wrong: a judge that called a heuristically-"incorrect" call correct was frequently right. The conclusion "both judges rubber-stamp calls" does not follow from those numbers.

**Re-score.** `eval/rescore_phase0.py` joins `results/phase0_raw/openai-{nano,mini}.jsonl` to the 102 hand-labeled rows in `test_real.jsonl` on `trajectory_id` (all 102 match) and recomputes everything. Score = `pred_confidence` if the verdict is "incorrect", else `1 - pred_confidence`; one nano output with no parsed verdict is scored 0.5. AUROC intervals are 95% percentile bootstraps (1,000 row resamples, seed 0). Saved to `results/phase0_rescored.json`.

| Model | AUROC (95% CI) | AUROC without Class 7 | Accuracy | Precision | Recall | False-flag rate |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| GPT-5-nano | 0.709 (0.58-0.81) | 0.681 (0.54-0.82) | 73.5% | 44.4% | 32.0% (8/25) | 13.0% (10/77) |
| GPT-5-mini | 0.667 (0.51-0.80) | 0.754 (0.59-0.89) | 75.5% | 50.0% | 48.0% (12/25) | 15.6% (12/77) |

Recall per class (flagged / real errors):

| Model | 1 wrong arg | 3 wrong tool | 4 repeated | 5 propagate | 7 precondition |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| GPT-5-nano | 3/6 | 3/3 | 0/7 | 1/1 | 1/8 |
| GPT-5-mini | 5/6 | 2/3 | 2/7 | 1/1 | 2/8 |

Per domain, recall and false-flag: nano airline 3/6 and 5/29, retail 4/5 and 1/30, telecom 1/14 and 4/18; mini airline 5/6 and 5/29, retail 2/5 and 3/30, telecom 5/14 and 4/18.

**What changes.** The judges are not rubber-stamps: they catch 32-48% of real errors with a 13-16% false-flag rate, an AUROC of 0.67-0.71 that is statistically indistinguishable from the trained ModernBERT (0.655) and Qwen (0.671) on the same 102 rows (all intervals span roughly 0.5 to 0.8). They are strong on Classes 1 and 3 (nano 6/9, mini 7/9, both seeing the full conversation) and weak on Class 4 and Class 7 (nano 1/15, mini 4/15), and weakest on telecom, where the conversations are long. The headroom argument in D-005/D-006 still holds, but it is smaller and different than stated: the gap to close is real-error recall on Classes 4 and 7 and on long telecom conversations, not a blanket 60 pp. The kill-test decision (continue) stands, because both judges are far below the D-005 threshold of 85% accuracy with low per-class variance, but the stated reason "recall ~20%" should no longer be quoted. Caveats: 25 positives, so every interval is wide, and the 77 correct rows are the atypical calls the heuristic flagged (D-010 limitation 1), so the false-flag rates may be pessimistic.

**Why the first run differed.** Dropping the one unparsed nano output instead of scoring it 0.5 gives nano AUROC 0.714 rather than 0.709. All other numbers match the figures expected for this correction exactly.

## D-007 — Phase 1 taxonomy validation results
**Date:** 2026-10-03  
**Status:** Standing

**Decision:** Manual labels from this phase become `test_real.jsonl` — held-out, never trained on, used only for final evaluation.

**102 trajectories reviewed; label distribution:**

| Class | Count | % of all | % of errors |
|---|---|---|---|
| no_error (benchmark artifact) | 77 | 75.5% | — |
| missing_precondition_wrong_order (7) | 8 | 7.8% | 32% |
| repeated_call_duplicate_side_effect (4) | 7 | 6.9% | 28% |
| wrong_argument_value (1) | 6 | 5.9% | 24% |
| wrong_tool_selection (3) | 3 | 2.9% | 12% |
| failure_to_propagate_tool_output (5) | 1 | 1.0% | 4% |
| stale_state (2) | 0 | 0% | 0% |
| ignored_error (6) | 0 | 0% | 0% |

**Key findings:**

1. **~75% artifact rate.** τ²-bench's exact-match gold actions are noisy. DOB values in conversation transcripts don't match gold, card numbers referenced in gold aren't in the user's profile, etc. This is a known limitation of the benchmark's construction: gold was written before model runs.

2. **25 genuine errors across 5 classes.** This is a small but high-quality held-out test set. Class 4 and Class 7 are the most common real failures in this data.

3. **Classes 2 and 6 absent from real data.** "Stale state / ignored correction" and "Ignoring tool error" were not observed in 102 τ²-bench trajectories. τ²-bench scripted personas rarely issue explicit corrections mid-task, and the simulated environments return graceful results rather than hard errors. Both classes will need to be covered by synthetic negatives in Phase 3.

4. **Content duplicates found despite unique trajectory IDs.** Trajectory IDs were always unique (210 originally; 207 after dedup), but content-hash dedup (same turns + same candidate call) caught real duplicates: 3 in the Phase 0 sample, and 112 in the full telecom pool during Phase 0 extraction (a further 31 telecom duplicates were removed when building the train pool). All were telecom, and came from distinct task_id strings (and trial/agent variants) that produced identical trajectories — a τ²-bench telecom artifact, not a pipeline bug. `prepare_tau2.py` and `build_train_pool.py` now dedupe by content hash. Separately, the same task_id appearing across different source_models and trial seeds is expected τ²-bench behavior (4 trials × multiple models per task) and is not deduplicated.

5. **Telecom tasks dominate repeated-call errors.** Class 4 violations all came from gpt-4.1-mini on telecom tasks with 199–205 turns. The agent loops through `get_details_by_id` calls across very long troubleshooting sessions.

6. **Airline + telecom dominate Class 7.** Missing precondition errors came from GPT-4.1 and Claude Sonnet on telecom (lock_sim_card_pin workflow) and airline (upgrade workflow requiring fee check before booking).

**Implication for Phase 3 (synthetic data):** Synthetic negatives must heavily cover Class 2 (stale state) and Class 6 (ignored error) since real data has zero examples. Classes 1, 3, 4, 7 can be supplemented but have real anchors to learn from.

---

## D-008 — Correct-only training pool strategy
**Date:** 2026-10-07  
**Status:** Standing

**Decision:** Train pool (`train_pool.jsonl`, `bfcl_pool.jsonl`) contains only verified-correct examples. All incorrect/negative training examples come exclusively from Phase 3 synthetic corruptions. Train/test leakage is excluded by `(domain, task_id)` pair, not just `trajectory_id`, because numeric task IDs like "17" appear in both the retail and airline domains.

**Why:** Phase 1 manual review found ~75% of heuristically-labeled "incorrect" τ²-bench examples are benchmark artifacts (DOB mismatch in conversation vs. gold, card numbers not in user profile, etc.). Using heuristic negatives would add ~75% mislabeled noise to the incorrect class. Correct examples carry no analogous artifact risk — the BFCL gold calls and τ²-bench `action_match=True` checks are high-quality positive signals.

**Caveat:** Correct examples are unverified individually; 30–50 spot-checks are recommended before final training runs, but these are not blocking Phase 3.

**Implication for Phase 3:** Synthetic corruption must cover all 7 error classes, with particular weight on Class 2 (stale state) and Class 6 (ignored error), which have zero real examples in Phase 1 data.

---

## D-009 — Phase 3 corruption strategy
**Date:** 2026-10-09  
**Status:** Standing

**Decision:** Implemented in `data/corrupt_pool.py`. Two-tier approach:

**Programmatic (zero cost, implemented as Python transforms):**
- Class 4 (Repeated Call): inject a copy of the candidate call + synthetic `{"status": "success"}` tool result earlier in the turns, making the call look like a duplicate.
- Class 7 (Missing Precondition): remove the last `get_/fetch_/check_/list_` prefixed tool call + its result from the turns, simulating a skipped prerequisite lookup. Requires ≥2 total calls in context. **Dropped from training, see the 2026-10-31 update.**

**LLM-assisted (OpenAI GPT-5-nano, ~$5/run at defaults):**
- Class 1 (Wrong Arg Value): replace one argument with a different value that appears elsewhere in the conversation context.
- Class 2 (Stale State): replace one argument with a value that was valid earlier but was later overridden.
- Class 3 (Wrong Tool Selection): change the tool name to a plausible alternative from the schema.
- Class 5 (Failure to Propagate): replace an argument that should come from a prior tool result with a different value.
- Class 6 (Ignored Error): keep candidate call unchanged; inject a fabricated failing predecessor call + error result into the turns.

**Validation gates (LLM responses):**
- Must parse as valid `{"name": ..., "arguments": {...}}` JSON.
- Must differ from original call.
- For classes 1/2/5/6: tool name must stay the same.
- For class 3: tool name must differ.

**Defaults:** max 1000 negatives per class, max 5000 LLM calls total, 8 parallel workers.

**Output:** `data/derived/negatives_pool.jsonl` — same schema as `train_pool.jsonl` plus `error_class` (int), `error_class_name` (str), `corruption_method` ("programmatic"|"llm").

**Update (2026-10-09) — LLM pass yield and filtering:** The raw LLM pass (1000 negatives, 200 per class) was audited before use. Class 2 (stale state) and Class 5 (failure to propagate) had low yield: Class 2 kept only 45 of 200 because most contexts contain no user correction, and Class 5 kept only 27 of 200 because all 106 BFCL examples have no tool-result turns (nothing to propagate) and 67 of the remaining tau2-bench ones used an invented value. Class 1 kept 171 of 200 (29 used a value absent from the context); Classes 3 and 6 were kept in full (200 each). The filter is `data/filter_llm_negatives.py`, which writes `negatives_llm.jsonl` and merges it with the programmatic negatives into `negatives_pool_final.jsonl` (2643 negatives: Class 1 171, Class 2 45, Class 3 200, Class 4 1000, Class 5 27, Class 6 200, Class 7 1000). The unfiltered output is kept locally as `data/derived/negatives_llm_raw.jsonl` and is excluded from git (`.gitignore`). **Caveat:** the Class 2 filter is a keyword match for a user correction, and a spot check found many false matches ("actually", "wait", "instead" in ordinary requests), so the 45 Class 2 negatives are likely still partly noisy. Classes 2 and 5 remain badly under-represented and need a better generator before training.

**Update (2026-10-09) — Class 2 and Class 5 are now programmatic (supersedes the class assignment above):** `data/corrupt_pool.py` generates both with no LLM (`--classes 2 5`, output `negatives_class2_class5.jsonl`), appended to `negatives_pool_final.jsonl` without disturbing the existing rows (4,023 total before the cleanup in the next update). Class 2 inserts a user turn "Actually, make that <new> not <old>." at a random position within the last 5 turns (never between a tool call and its result) and makes the call use a fabricated stale value (numbers ×1.5/×2/÷2 rounded; ID-like strings get an appended or changed last character). Class 5 (tau2-bench only, since BFCL has no tool results) takes an argument value that appears in the last tool result and in no user turn, and replaces it with a same-shaped value from an earlier tool result or a user turn. Yield: Class 2 1,000 (cap; ~1,876 possible), Class 5 380 (all available; retail 293, telecom 87, no airline). The 45 + 27 filtered LLM Class 2/5 rows were initially kept (Class 2 = 1,045, Class 5 = 407) and then dropped, see the next update. **Caveats:** (1) every Class 2 negative contains the same correction template and no correct example did, so a model could learn the phrase instead of the stale-value signal (addressed by the paired controls in the next update). (2) Class 5 replacements match only the *shape* of the original, not its entity type (e.g. a line id swapped for a plan id), so some are cruder than a real agent mistake. (3) Stale values are fabricated, not drawn from an earlier turn of the real conversation.

**Update (2026-10-09) — Paired Class 2 controls; noisy LLM Class 2/5 rows dropped:** To stop the correction sentence from being a shortcut, every programmatic Class 2 negative now has a paired positive in `data/derived/positives_class2_controls.jsonl` (1,000 rows): identical turns, including the injected "Actually, make that X not Y." turn, and the same tool call but with the correct value restored. They are labeled `correct` with `label_tier: synthetic_class2_control`, `trajectory_id` ending `::ctrl`, and a `paired_negative_id` pointing at the negative; the error fields are removed. They are kept in a separate file and not merged into the negatives, so merge them with the positive pool at training time (keep a negative and its control in the same split). The 45 old LLM Class 2 rows and 27 old LLM Class 5 rows were removed from `negatives_pool_final.jsonl`, since the programmatic generators replace them; the other rows keep their order. Final negatives (3,951): Class 1 171, Class 2 1,000, Class 3 200, Class 4 1,000, Class 5 380, Class 6 200, Class 7 1,000. Positives: 2,517 original correct examples + 1,000 controls = 3,517. `data/filter_llm_negatives.py` regenerates both files. **Remaining shortcut risk:** other classes inject their own artifacts with no matching positives (Class 4 adds a synthetic `{"status": "success"}` result, Class 6 a fabricated `check_*` failure turn), so those classes could be detectable by the artifact alone.

**Phase 3 results — final negative and positive counts (2026-10-09):**

| Class | Name | Count | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | wrong argument | 146 | LLM (gpt-5-nano), after the quality filters |
| 2 | stale state | 1,000 | programmatic |
| 3 | wrong tool | 108 | LLM (gpt-5-nano), after the quality filters |
| 4 | repeated call | 1,000 | programmatic |
| 5 | failure to propagate | 380 | programmatic |
| 6 | ignored error | 200 | LLM (gpt-5-nano) |
| 7 | missing precondition | 0 | not trained on: 1,000 were generated and excluded (see the 2026-10-31 update) |
| — | **Total negatives** | **2,834** | (3,949 before Class 7 was dropped, 2,949 before the quality filters) |
| — | **Total positives** | **3,517** | 2,517 original + 1,000 Class 2 controls |

Classes 1, 3 and 6 are below 1,000 because the LLM budget was capped at 200 per class (`--max-per-class 200`). Class 1 is further reduced to 169: the filter dropped 29 examples whose new value did not appear in the context (171 left), and 2 more were removed as content duplicates of Class 5 rows (see D-012). Class 5 is below 1,000 because only 380 tau2-bench examples had an argument traceable to the last tool result.

**Update (2026-10-31) — Class 7 dropped from training; the prerequisite rules are kept for inference-time use only.** The 1,000 synthetic Class 7 (missing precondition / wrong order) negatives are no longer trained on. `filter_llm_negatives.py` drops class 7 when it builds `negatives_pool_final.jsonl` (2,949 negatives); `negatives_pool.jsonl` still holds the generator's Class 7 rows as raw output. Three approaches were tried, and none works:
1. **Removal generator (`corrupt_class7`).** Deleting a lookup call and its result makes every Class 7 negative 1-2 turns shorter than its source positive, while real Class 7 rows are longer (median 9 prior tool calls against 6 for real correct rows and 4 for training positives). A model learns "short means incorrect" and inverts on `test_real`: the Phase 5 model scored AUROC 0.276, and a probe on 11 structural features alone reproduces it (0.242, Class 7-only 0.248).
2. **Move the lookup to the end of the turns** (reverted). The candidate is not part of the turns, so the lookup lands immediately before the candidate, which is the normal order, and it added a new artifact (every tau2-bench negative then ended with a tool result).
3. **Schema enrichment with prerequisite rules** (data restored; see below). The rules do not surface a signal: a rule-aware probe stays below 0.5 on `test_real` for Class 7 (0.259 with policy-only rules; full dataset 0.238). Only 12% of the 592 tau2-bench Class 7 negatives violate a rule under policy-only rules (29% with the owner's examples), because the generator removes the last lookup of any kind, not necessarily one the candidate's tool requires; 381 of the 1,000 come from BFCL and have no rules; the rules also fire on 8-15% of correct rows. And the real Class 7 rows are mostly not missing lookups: policy-only rules flag 0 of the 8 (the five telecom `resume_line` rows already made the bill and line lookups, and the three airline rows made theirs; the failures look like policy violations such as an expired contract).

**Conclusion:** Class 7 detection requires checking the action against policy at runtime, not a trained classifier. It will be implemented as a rule-based layer after training.

*What is kept:* the `PREREQUISITES` table in `data/tool_schemas.py` (19 write tools across retail, airline and telecom, each clause tagged `policy`, `user_example` or `test_informed`; real tool names only, since `unlock_sim_pin`, `get_plan_info`, `check_upgrade_fee` and `get_customer_and_line_info` do not exist and a locked SIM is checked with the user-side `check_sim_status`). `format_schemas_for_prompt(schemas, domain, include_requires=True)` renders them as `# Requires: ...` lines; the default emits none. They are not embedded in any stored dataset: the data files and the build scripts were restored to their state before the enrichment (every data file other than `negatives_pool_final.jsonl` is byte-identical to commit 056aab2). The notebook's `format_input` shows the candidate tool's rule when the schema text it is given contains one; on all stored data the formatted input is identical to what it was before the enrichment (checked on every training and test row). One risk: the classifier never saw a `# Requires:` line in training, so passing rule text at inference changes its input distribution; keep the rule layer separate from the classifier's input unless it is validated.

*Test set:* `test_real.jsonl` keeps its 8 real Class 7 rows as an evaluation-only set. Expect near-zero recall on them, and report Class 7 separately from the trained classes. The `test_informed` clause (`check_sim_status` before `resume_line`) was written from those rows, so any evaluation of the future rule layer on `test_real` should use the `policy`-only clauses (see D-010).

**Update (2026-11-01) — quality filters on the LLM Class 1 and Class 3 negatives (Class 1: 169 to 146; Class 3: 200 to 108).** A manual read of 15 random rows of each class (plus counts over all rows) showed the tau2-bench negatives were realistic (an ID or value swapped for another one in the conversation; a write tool swapped for a neighboring write or read tool), but some rows were not usable as negatives. `data/filter_llm_negatives.py` now drops, from `negatives_pool_final.jsonl`: **Class 1** rows whose call adds an argument the source call did not have (20, 15 of them BFCL, e.g. `get_watchlist()` became `get_watchlist(stock=...)`: an extra argument, not a wrong value) and self-exchanges where `item_ids == new_item_ids` (3); a rule for calls identical to the source also exists but matches nothing, since the generator already rejects unchanged calls. **Class 3** BFCL rows whose tool is a gold call of no turn of the task (80: an unrelated tool, such as `du` for a logarithm request or `get_tweet` for cancelling a booking, which is too easy to spot) or is also a gold call of the same turn (12: a valid call labeled incorrect, e.g. `toggleHeadlights` when the user also asked for the headlights). Gold calls come from the raw BFCL `possible_answer/` files, because a row's `gold_action` holds only the first gold call of its turn; the script now needs those files and stops if they are missing. The filters run after the seeded shuffle, like the other drops, so every remaining row keeps its position (the new file is the previous one minus exactly 115 rows) and the intermediate `negatives_llm.jsonl` is unchanged. Result: Class 1 has 146 negatives (tau2-bench 83, BFCL 63), Class 3 has 108 (tau2-bench 92, BFCL 16), 2,834 negatives in total.

*Limits:* the 16 BFCL Class 3 rows that remain use a tool that is a gold call of a different turn (wrong timing), which is a realistic mistake, but BFCL Class 3 is now small and the model will learn tool confusion almost entirely from tau2-bench. The same-turn check compares tool names only, not arguments. There is no per-turn gold for tau2-bench, so tau2-bench Class 3 rows get no equivalent check. The 59 Class 1 rows I could not classify cleanly (a catch-all bucket, mostly a different kind of value in the slot) were not reviewed row by row and were all kept.

---

## D-010 — Phase 4: real held-out test set (`test_real.jsonl`)
**Date:** 2026-10-11  
**Status:** Standing

**Decision:** The held-out real test set is `data/derived/test_real.jsonl`, built by `data/build_test_real.py` from all 102 hand-labeled trajectories: the 25 genuine errors (label `incorrect`, with an error class) plus the 77 hand-verified correct calls (the `no_error` rows, label `correct`, error class null). It is never trained on and is used only for final evaluation.

**Composition (102 examples):**

| | Incorrect | Correct | Total |
|---|---|---|---|
| Examples | 25 | 77 | 102 |
| Share | 24.5% | 75.5% | |
| Telecom | 14 | 18 | 32 |
| Airline | 6 | 29 | 35 |
| Retail | 5 | 30 | 35 |
| Agent: gpt-4.1 | 14 | 21 | 35 |
| Agent: gpt-4.1-mini | 9 | 22 | 31 |
| Agent: claude-3-7-sonnet | 2 | 22 | 24 |
| Agent: o4-mini | 0 | 12 | 12 |

Error classes among the 25 incorrect rows: Class 1 wrong argument 6, Class 2 stale state 0, Class 3 wrong tool 3, Class 4 repeated call 7, Class 5 failure to propagate 1, Class 6 ignored error 0, Class 7 missing precondition 8. There are 74 distinct `(domain, task_id)` pairs overall.

**How good was the Phase 0 heuristic?** Both halves come from the heuristic's "incorrect" pool, so the hand labels measure its precision directly: 25 of 102 (24.5%) were genuine errors. By tier: `wrong_argument_value` 4 of 41 (10%), `reward_fallback` 21 of 61 (34%). In 37 of the 77 correct rows the call's arguments differed from the benchmark's gold action but a human judged the call fine, consistent with the gold-action noise in D-007.

**Zero train/test overlap (verified by `build_test_real.py`, which refuses to write the file otherwise).** All 102 rows were checked against `train_pool.jsonl`, `bfcl_pool.jsonl`, `negatives_pool_final.jsonl` and `positives_class2_controls.jsonl` on three keys: `trajectory_id` (also with the `::corruptN` / `::ctrl` suffix stripped, so derived rows count), `(domain, task_id)` pair, and byte-identical conversation turns. All three keys had 0 hits in all four files. The turns check was added because telecom `task_id` strings can realize identical conversations (D-007 finding 4).

**Dropped:** none. All 102 were present in `phase0_candidates.jsonl` with full context, so the raw τ²-bench files were not needed.

**Schema reconciliation:** records follow the training-pool schema (`tool_schemas_text`, `turns`, `candidate_tool_call`, `gold_action`, `label_tier`, `split: "test"`). In `labels_manual.jsonl`, `label` held the class name and `taxonomy_class` the number; in the training schema `label` is `correct`/`incorrect`. So `label` is `incorrect` or `correct`, `taxonomy_class` became `error_class` (null for correct rows), the class name went to `error_class_name` (same names as `corrupt_pool.ERROR_CLASSES`), and the original text is kept as `human_label`. One entry was free text with no class number ("7 - missing precondition, skipped sim pin unlock step"); it was mapped to class 7 and the remainder kept as `human_note`. `label_tier` is `hand_labeled` for all rows and the old heuristic tier is kept as `heuristic_tier`. Rows keep the order of `labels_manual.jsonl`.

**How to use it:** the base rate is 24.5% incorrect, and always predicting `correct` scores 75.5% accuracy, so report precision, recall and F1 (or AUROC and the risk-coverage curve), not accuracy alone. The composition is confounded in two ways: telecom is 44% incorrect versus 17% (airline) and 14% (retail), and the incorrect rows include very long telecom conversations (mean 70 turns versus 25 for correct rows; medians are close, 26 versus 22). Predicting "incorrect iff telecom" scores 71.6%, so report per-domain results too.

**Limitations:**
1. The correct half is not ordinary correct traffic. These are calls the heuristic flagged (the task failed, or the arguments differed from gold) that a human then judged fine, so they are likely harder or atypical, and the false-flag rate measured here may be pessimistic for typical correct calls. They also differ from the training positives, which are gold-matched calls.
2. Only 25 incorrect examples: metrics have wide confidence intervals, and per-class scores are not meaningful for Class 3 (3 examples) and Class 5 (1).
3. Classes 2 and 6 have no real examples, so synthetic-data performance on them cannot be validated on real data.
4. Agent mix differs by label (o4-mini appears only among correct rows), and the incorrect rows skew toward telecom and gpt-4.1.
5. The 8 real Class 7 rows were used to design one prerequisite rule (the telecom `check_sim_status` clause, tagged `test_informed` in `data/tool_schemas.py`; see D-009), so Class 7 results are not fully held out. Use the `policy`-only rules for clean numbers.

---

## D-011 — Phase 5: ModernBERT-base classifier (`notebooks/phase5_modernbert.ipynb`)
**Date:** 2026-10-13  
**Status:** Implemented; not yet trained on a T4

**Decision:** Fine-tune `answerdotai/ModernBERT-base` as a binary classifier (positive class = `incorrect`) on the Phase 2/3 pools (3,517 correct, 3,951 incorrect) and evaluate on `test_real.jsonl` (D-010). Everything runs from one Colab notebook reading `MyDrive/toolcall-verifier/data/derived/`.

**Why ModernBERT over BERT-base:** the input is a conversation plus a candidate call, and it is long. After compression, 48% of training inputs and 82% of `test_real` inputs exceed 512 tokens (BERT-base's limit); the longest is 1,433 tokens. ModernBERT supports 8,192 tokens, so nothing is truncated, and its pretraining includes code, closer to the JSON-like tool calls and arguments we are judging. It still trains in about an hour on a T4 per the plan, so it sets the encoder floor cheaply (D-001).

**State compression (`format_input`):** tools as name + parameter names only (no descriptions or types), limited to tools called in the last 5 turns plus the candidate's tool; last 10 turns (`USER:` / `AGENT:` / `AGENT_CALL:` / `TOOL_RESULT:`, tool results cut to 200 chars, user/agent text to 500); candidate last as `CANDIDATE: name(k=v, ...)`, argument values clipped at 300 chars. Token lengths after compression: training pool p50 474, p90 942, p99 1,156, max 1,433; `test_real` p50 757, max 1,239. All 7,570 inputs are under the 1,500-token budget (`max_length=1536`), so the tightening ladder (shorter window and clips if over budget) never fires; it exists so that right-truncation can never cut off the candidate, which is the last thing in the text.

**One addition to the spec, `EARLIER_CALLS:`:** a one-line list (at most 20, most recent last) of tool calls that fall before the 10-turn window. Measured on the 1,000 synthetic Class 4 (repeated call) negatives: the injected duplicate lands inside a last-10-turn window in only 656 (66%), so without this line 34% of those negatives are indistinguishable from the positive they came from. With it, 999 of 1,000 are visible. Classes 2, 5 and 6 evidence was already inside the window (100%, 99%, 100%).

**Model and hyperparameters:**

| | |
|---|---|
| Head | `classifier_pooling="cls"`. The library default is mean pooling, so this is set explicitly. ModernBERT's standard head is a small dense + GELU + LayerNorm block before the linear 2-way layer |
| Optimizer | AdamW, lr 2e-5, linear schedule, 10% warmup (passed as `warmup_steps`), weight decay 0.01 |
| Epochs / batch | 3 epochs; effective batch 16. Starts at 16 x 1 and on CUDA out-of-memory retries at 8 x 2, then 4 x 4 |
| Length | `max_length=1536`, truncation on, dynamic padding per batch |
| Precision | fp16 on the T4 (no bf16 or FlashAttention there; the library falls back to SDPA) |
| Selection | best epoch by validation F1 on the `incorrect` class (`load_best_model_at_end`), threshold 0.5; seed 42 |
| Trainer | HuggingFace `Trainer`, train loss logged every 50 steps, eval each epoch |

The notebook skips training if `checkpoints/phase5/best/` exists and resumes from the last epoch checkpoint if a run was interrupted. It saves the best model, tokenizer, `train_info.json` and, after evaluation, `test_real_predictions.jsonl` (per-example probabilities for the Phase 7 calibration work). Epoch checkpoints include optimizer state, roughly 1.8 GB each, so allow about 4 GB of Drive space.

**Train/val split: grouped by `(domain, task_id)`, not by row.** The spec asked for a 90/10 stratified split with each Class 2 negative and its paired control in the same split. That is implemented and asserted via `paired_negative_id` (1,000 pairs). But the same problem applies to every derived row: each negative is a copy of a positive with one change, so a row-level split puts a near-identical twin in train. Measured with a plain stratified row-level split: 740 of 747 val rows share a `(domain, task_id)` with a train row, and 482 of 491 derived val negatives have their source positive in train. Grouping by task (`StratifiedGroupKFold`, 10 folds, first fold as val, seed 42) fixes that and covers the pairing requirement. Result: train 6,724 (52.9% incorrect, 492 task groups) / val 744 (52.8% incorrect, 61 groups). Val is small in tasks (airline has only 11 tasks overall), so per-domain val numbers are noisy. Note this is still a deviation: if you want strictly the pair-only grouping, change `groups` in Cell 3.

**Known risks:**
1. **Artifact shortcuts will inflate validation scores.** Every Class 4 negative (1,000) contains a synthetic tool result `{"status": "success"}` that appears in none of the 3,517 positives, and every Class 6 negative (200) contains a fabricated failing `check_*` call with a "prerequisite not met" result that appears in no positive. The model can learn to flag these without any understanding. Neither pattern appears in `test_real` (0 of 102), so those 1,200 negatives (30% of all negatives) may score near-perfectly on val and not transfer. `test_real` is the honest number; read validation F1 as a sanity check only. Control positives for Classes 4 and 6 (as built for Class 2) are the fix if this materializes. **Update (2026-10-30): built, see D-012. The dataset and split sizes quoted in this entry (3,517 positives, 7,468 rows, train 6,724 / val 744) are superseded by D-012.**
2. **Wrong-tool detection is handicapped by the schema rule.** Only tools already called in the last 5 turns, plus the candidate's tool, are listed, so the tool the agent should have used is usually not visible. Class 3 (200 synthetic, 3 real) may stay weak.
3. **The notebook has not been run on a T4 with the real weights.** It was smoke-tested locally on CPU/MPS with a randomly initialised toy ModernBERT: data loading, compression, split and its assertions, the full Trainer loop with epoch checkpoints, best-model save, evaluation on all of `test_real`, skip-on-rerun, resume from a checkpoint, and the out-of-memory retry ladder (via a simulated exception). The smoke test also caught that `group_by_length` no longer exists in current transformers, which would have crashed the first Colab run. Not verified: fp16 numerical stability on a T4, real memory use and runtime at batch 16 x 1536 tokens, and any real accuracy.
4. The evaluation inherits the D-010 limitations: 25 incorrect rows (per-class numbers are near meaningless for Class 3 and 5), no real Class 2 or 6 examples, and a correct half that is atypical.

---

## D-012 — Control positives for Classes 4 and 6
**Date:** 2026-10-30  
**Status:** Implemented; classifier not yet retrained on the new data

**Decision:** Add paired control positives for the Class 4 and Class 6 synthetic negatives, as was already done for Class 2 (D-009). They are built by `data/build_controls_4_6.py` into `positives_class4_controls.jsonl` and `positives_class6_controls.jsonl` and loaded as correct examples by the Phase 5 notebook.

**Why they are needed:** both generators insert a synthetic artifact into the context that appears in no positive and in none of the 102 `test_real` rows:
- **Class 4** inserts a copy of the candidate call followed by a fake tool result `{"status": "success"}`. It is in all 1,000 Class 4 negatives and in 0 of the 3,517 original positives.
- **Class 6** appends a fabricated failing predecessor call (`check_*` / `verify_*`) followed by an error result. It is in all 200 Class 6 negatives and in 0 positives.

Together that is 1,200 of 3,949 negatives (30%; 1,200 of 2,834, or 42%, after Class 7 was dropped and the Class 1 and 3 quality filters) that a classifier can flag by pattern matching on the artifact, with no reasoning about the call. That would inflate validation scores and not transfer to `test_real` (D-011 risk 1). Each control keeps the artifact and removes the error, so the artifact alone no longer predicts the label.

**How each control is built (each negative is the original positive plus the injected turns, verified for every row):**
- **Class 4 control (`::ctrl4`):** same position, same fake success result, but the injected call is a different call (chosen at random, seed 42) that already appears in the turns, not the candidate. The candidate is therefore not a duplicate. Verified for all 834: only the injected call differs from the negative, the injected call exists in the original turns, and the candidate call appears nowhere in the context.
- **Class 6 control (`::ctrl6`):** same fabricated predecessor at the same position, but with a success result, `{"status": "success", "message": "Operation completed: prerequisite met"}`, the same shape and wording as the error `{"status": "error", "message": "Operation failed: prerequisite not met"}`, so only the outcome differs.
- Both carry `label: "correct"`, `label_tier: synthetic_class{4,6}_control`, `trajectory_id: <original>::ctrl{4,6}`, `paired_negative_id`, and `corruption_method: "control"`. They have no `error_class`.

**Counts:**

| Controls | Written | Negatives | Skipped |
|---|---|---|---|
| Class 2 (existing) | 1,000 | 1,000 | 0 |
| Class 4 | 834 | 1,000 | 159 with no other tool call in the turns to inject (133 BFCL, 26 tau2-bench); 7 whose source positive already contains its own candidate call earlier |
| Class 6 | 200 | 200 | 0 |

Dedup against `positives_class2_controls.jsonl`: 0 skipped. Class 2 controls end `::ctrl` and these end `::ctrl4` / `::ctrl6`, so ids cannot collide; no control is content-identical to any other positive. Class 4 controls by source: tau2-bench 410, BFCL 424. Class 6: tau2-bench 96, BFCL 104.

**Resulting training set (7,385 rows, after Class 7 was dropped and the Class 1 and 3 quality filters; see D-009):** 4,551 correct (2,517 real/gold + 2,034 controls) and 2,834 incorrect (38.4%): Class 1 146, Class 2 1,000, Class 3 108, Class 4 1,000, Class 5 380, Class 6 200. With the grouped split (D-011): train 6,607 rows (490 task groups, 38.3% incorrect) and val 778 rows (63 groups, 38.7% incorrect). Every pair stays in one split, checked via `paired_negative_id`: Class 2 1,000 pairs (train 902 / val 98), Class 4 834 (751 / 83), Class 6 200 (171 / 29). The notebook's grouping is by `(domain, task_id)`, which keeps pairs together because a control shares its negative's task; `paired_negative_id` is used to verify it, not to build the groups.

**Effect on the shortcut:** the share of rows carrying each artifact that are incorrect falls from 100% to 55% for the fake success pair (834 positives, 1,000 negatives) and to 50% for the fabricated predecessor (200 and 200). Token lengths are unchanged: the new controls have p50 444 and max 1,280 tokens, none over the 1,500 budget. The evidence also survives compression: the injected call is visible in all 834 Class 4 controls and the error or success result is visible in all 200 Class 6 pairs.

**Remaining limitations:**
1. **166 Class 4 negatives (17%) have no twin** (159 with nothing to swap in plus the 7 above). That does not recreate the shortcut, since the injected pair now appears in positives too and a model has to compare the injected call with the candidate, but those negatives have no matched positive. 133 of the 159 are BFCL turn-0 examples with no earlier calls.
2. **Fixed strings.** Every Class 6 error result is the same string, and every success result is the same string. A model can learn those exact strings instead of what an error means, and real tool errors vary. `test_real` has no Class 6 rows, so transfer to real errors cannot be checked. The same applies to the fake `{"status": "success"}` in Class 4.
3. **Controls are synthetic positives:** 2,034 of the 4,551 positives (45%) are synthetic controls (Class 2: 1,000, Class 4: 834, Class 6: 200); only 2,517 (55%) are real or gold calls. The Class 4 controls also contain a repeated call (of a different tool call), which is deliberate so that a repeat alone does not signal an error, but it means the contexts are slightly unnatural.
4. **Classes 4 and 6 are the only ones paired this way.** Classes 1, 3 and 5 change the candidate without inserting an artifact (Class 7, which removed a turn, is no longer trained on).
5. The notebook's loading, compression and split cells were run locally with the real tokenizer on the new data; training was not rerun, and nothing has run on a T4 yet.

**Update (2026-10-30) — 2 duplicate Class 1 negatives removed (8,502 → 8,500 rows):** A pre-upload check found two pairs of negatives with identical turns and candidate call: an LLM Class 1 row and a programmatic Class 5 row built from the same source, both labeled `incorrect`. Earlier duplicate checks missed them because the Class 5 generator was added after those checks. The two Class 1 rows were dropped (Class 1: 171 → 169; negatives 3,951 → 3,949; Class 5 is unchanged at 380). `data/filter_llm_negatives.py` now removes content duplicates when it builds `negatives_pool_final.jsonl`, keeping the programmatic row, so a rebuild cannot bring them back. Final training set: 8,500 rows, 4,551 correct and 3,949 incorrect, no content-identical rows, and none overlapping `test_real`. **The group split changed as a side effect:** `StratifiedGroupKFold` assigns whole task groups heuristically, so dropping two rows moved groups between train and val. All split numbers in this entry are the new ones; any earlier prints (train 7,661 / val 841, val domains retail 272 / airline 113 / telecom 78 / BFCL 378) are out of date. Val is now retail 288, airline 59, telecom 20, BFCL 475, so **val barely covers telecom** and its per-domain numbers are not meaningful; per-class val counts for incorrect rows are Class 1 23, Class 2 96, Class 3 21, Class 4 107, Class 5 32, Class 6 14, Class 7 97.

**Update (2026-10-31) — Class 7 dropped from training (8,500 → 7,500 rows):** The 1,000 Class 7 negatives were removed from `negatives_pool_final.jsonl` (3,949 → 2,949; rows otherwise identical and in the same order) because Class 7 cannot be learned from synthetic data (D-009). All other files are unchanged and the schema text in the stored data is rule-free again. Final dataset: 7,500 rows, 4,551 correct (60.7%) and 2,949 incorrect (39.3%), no content-identical rows, none overlapping `test_real`. Incorrect by class: 1 = 169, 2 = 1,000, 3 = 200, 4 = 1,000, 5 = 380, 6 = 200, 7 = 0. By domain (correct / incorrect): retail 1,528 / 1,191, airline 150 / 92, telecom 631 / 437, BFCL 2,242 / 1,229. Split (notebook Cell 3 logic, seed 42): train 6,767 rows (4,104 correct, 2,663 incorrect) and val 733 rows (447 correct, 286 incorrect). The group assignment shifted again, so earlier split numbers are superseded. **The validation set has 0 airline rows** (retail 219, telecom 86, BFCL 428), because all airline task groups landed in train; validation says nothing about airline, and per-class validation counts for incorrect rows are small (Class 1 24, Class 2 95, Class 3 29, Class 4 103, Class 5 20, Class 6 15). The Class 4 and 6 negatives, which carry injected artifacts, are now 1,200 of 2,949 negatives (41%); all have controls except the 166 unpaired Class 4 negatives described above. Training is 39.3% incorrect against 24.5% in `test_real`, so the 0.5 threshold will still over-flag on test. The classifier has not been retrained on this data.

**Update (2026-11-01) — Class 1 and 3 quality filters (7,500 → 7,385 rows):** 115 LLM negatives were removed from `negatives_pool_final.jsonl` (2,949 → 2,834: Class 1 169 → 146, Class 3 200 → 108; see D-009); no other file changed and the other negatives keep their order. Final dataset: 7,385 rows, 4,551 correct (61.6%) and 2,834 incorrect (38.4%), no content-identical rows, none overlapping `test_real`. By domain (correct / incorrect): retail 1,528 / 1,188, airline 150 / 92, telecom 631 / 432, BFCL 2,242 / 1,122. Split (notebook Cell 3 logic, seed 42): train 6,607 rows (4,074 correct, 2,533 incorrect) and val 778 rows (477 correct, 301 incorrect). The group assignment shifted again, so the split numbers in the two earlier 2026-10-31 and 2026-10-30 updates of this entry are superseded. **The validation set still has 0 airline rows** (retail 319, telecom 115, BFCL 344), and per-class validation counts for incorrect rows are small (Class 1 23, Class 2 98, Class 3 9, Class 4 102, Class 5 40, Class 6 29), so validation says nothing about airline and almost nothing about Class 3. The Class 4 and 6 negatives, which carry injected artifacts, are now 1,200 of 2,834 negatives (42%); all have controls except the 166 unpaired Class 4 negatives described above. Training is 38.4% incorrect against 24.5% in `test_real`, so the 0.5 threshold will still over-flag on test. The classifier has not been retrained on this data.

Training set: **7,580 rows, 4,551 correct (60.0%) and 3,029 incorrect (40.0%)**; incorrect by class 1: 341, 2: 1,000, 3: 108, 4: 1,000, 5: 380, 6: 200. The documented numbers are correct, so D-012 is not changed; instead both notebooks now carry the 58 validation groups as a list (`notebooks/freeze_split.py`, computed with 1.9.1) and assert exactly 6,816 / 764, so Colab, Kaggle and a laptop train on the same split. A Colab run before this change (such as the interrupted Qwen run, 6,892 / 688) used the other split, and its validation numbers are not comparable with the documented one (test_real is unaffected).

## D-013 — Phase 6: Qwen2.5-1.5B + LoRA classifier (`notebooks/phase6_qwen.ipynb`)

**Decision:** fine-tune `Qwen/Qwen2.5-1.5B` as a sequence classifier (`AutoModelForSequenceClassification`, 2 labels, last-token pooling) with LoRA, on the same data and with the same evaluation protocol as Phase 5 (D-011). Same 7,385 training rows (D-012), same `format_input` compression, same `StratifiedGroupKFold` split grouped by `(domain, task_id)` with the same seed (train 6,607 / val 778), same `test_real.jsonl` evaluation (102 rows, 25 incorrect). **The baseline to beat is the Phase 5 ModernBERT-base AUROC of 0.652 on `test_real`.** Only the model and training setup change, so a difference in score can be attributed to the model.

**Setup:** LoRA r=16, alpha=32, dropout 0.05, targets `q_proj` and `v_proj`, `TaskType.SEQ_CLS` (the `score` head is trained too). Learning rate 2e-4, 3 epochs, micro-batch 8 x gradient accumulation 2 (effective 16), weight decay 0.01, max length 1,536, evaluation and checkpoint per epoch, best epoch by validation F1. The EOS token is the pad token, padding on the right.

**Deviations from the spec, and why:**
1. **`warmup_ratio` replaced with `warmup_steps`** (10% of total optimizer steps, computed in the notebook), because transformers 5.x no longer accepts `warmup_ratio`.
2. **4-bit quantization (NF4, double quantization) with fp16 compute, for T4 compatibility.** A T4 has 16 GB and no bf16 support, so the model is loaded with `BitsAndBytesConfig(load_in_4bit=True, bnb_4bit_compute_dtype=torch.float16)` and an explicit fp16 dtype (the default `"auto"` would load bf16). Trainable LoRA and head weights are kept in fp32 and training uses fp16 mixed precision. The head is excluded from quantization.
3. **Gradient checkpointing and `prepare_model_for_kbit_training`**, to fit 1,536-token sequences in 16 GB.
4. **Out-of-memory fallback:** retries at micro-batch 4 x accumulation 4, then 2 x 8, keeping the effective batch at 16.
5. **Pad token** is passed to the model explicitly (the Qwen config has none).
6. **No Class 7 in training** (D-009); `test_real` still has 8 real Class 7 rows, so expect near-zero recall on them.

**Checked locally (Mac, tiny random Qwen model, real tokenizer, 1 epoch):** all five cells run; the row count and split match D-012; Qwen token lengths over all 7,487 rows (training plus `test_real`) are p50 443, p99 1,214, max 1,497, so nothing is truncated; the saved adapter reloads to the same predictions as the in-memory model; re-running skips training when `best/` exists and resumes from a trainer checkpoint when it does not.

**Not verified:** the 4-bit bitsandbytes path, fp16 numerics, memory use and runtime on a real T4, and the out-of-memory fallback. Smoke metrics came from a random model and mean nothing. No Phase 6 result exists yet.

**Caveats carried over from D-010 to D-012:** `test_real` is small (25 incorrect), so a gap to 0.652 can be noise; validation has no airline rows; the synthetic Class 4 and 6 artifacts are controlled but not eliminated.

**Update (2026-11-06):** the dataset grew to **7,580 training rows (3,029 incorrect, 40.0%)** with the Class 1 context-swap pass (D-015, D-012 update). Both notebooks now assert 7,580 rows exactly (the files are fixed) and check the grouped split within a tolerance, expecting about 6,816 train / 764 val (accepted ranges 6,500–7,100 and 690–840), because the fold can shift slightly between scikit-learn versions. Phase 5 had no such checks before; they were added. **Neither model has been retrained**, so the Phase 5 and Phase 6 results (D-014) come from the earlier 7,385-row dataset. The "7,385 rows" and "6,607 / 778" figures above describe that earlier dataset.

## D-014 — Phase 7: README and project wrap-up

**Status:** Phase 7 complete. `README.md` rewritten for a portfolio/GitHub audience (summary, taxonomy, dataset, models, results, failure analysis, structure, how to run). Project status: baseline established, two models trained (ModernBERT-base and Qwen2.5-1.5B + LoRA, same data and eval protocol), honest evaluation done on `test_real`.

**Results as reported in the README** (test_real, 102 rows, 25 incorrect, threshold 0.5): ModernBERT-base AUROC 0.652, F1 0.356, recall Class 1 17% (1/6), Class 3 33% (1/3), Class 4 86% (6/7); Qwen2.5-1.5B LoRA AUROC 0.671, F1 0.348, Class 1 33% (2/6), Class 3 0% (0/3), Class 4 71% (5/7). These numbers were supplied by the project owner from the Colab runs; the Phase 6 predictions file was not available in this repo, so they were not recomputed here. **The two models are not distinguishable:** the AUROC gap is 0.019 on 25 positives, and class recalls rest on 3 to 7 examples. Neither model is usable as a verifier yet.

**Known gaps stated in the README:** Class 7 is not trained for and is not caught by the rule checker (see the finding below), although `test_real` has 8 real Class 7 rows; Classes 2 and 6 have no real test examples; Class 1 is largely missed; validation has no airline rows; the training negatives are synthetic.

**Confirmed finding: the rule-based Class 7 checker gets 0/8 recall on the real Class 7 errors.** `eval/class7_checker.py` is built (commit `de6fb98`): it looks up the candidate in the `PREREQUISITES` table (`data/tool_schemas.py`) and flags it when no required lookup tool has succeeded earlier in the history (a result containing "error", "failed" or "not found" does not count). On `test_real` it flags 2 of 102 rows, both correct calls (false-flag rate 2/77 = 2.6%); it flags 0 of the 17 non-Class-7 errors and 0 of the 8 Class 7 errors. **All 8 real Class 7 examples already have the required lookups in their history** (for the five telecom `resume_line` rows: `get_customer_by_phone`, `get_details_by_id` and `get_bills_for_customer`; the three airline rows made theirs too). They are policy violations, not missing lookups, so a check for missing lookups cannot catch them. This confirms the earlier probe result (D-009: policy-only rules flagged 0 of the 8), now with the actual checker. A stricter variant that requires every rule clause flags 5 of 8 with 10 of 77 false flags, but those hits come from the `check_sim_status` clause that was written after looking at these rows (`test_informed`, D-010 caveat 5); with policy-only clauses the same variant flags 0 of 8. Combined with an ML classifier, the rule layer adds no true catches and no extra false flags (`eval/combined_predict.py`). `combined_predict.py` has only been run on the first Phase 5 run (AUROC 0.276, trained with Class 7), the only predictions file available; its output for the 0.652 model is still to be produced.

**Next steps if continued:**
1. More airline and retail negatives, ideally realistic or real rather than only synthetic.
2. A different approach to Class 7: not a lookup-presence check but a policy check that reads what the lookup results say (contract end date, line status, SIM state, bill status) and compares them with the workflow, plus more real Class 7 examples to design it on (8 is too few for a held-out evaluation).
3. A larger Qwen model.

## D-015 — Class 1 context-swap pass (146 → 341 negatives)

**Date:** 2026-11-06. **Why:** Class 1 recall was 17% (ModernBERT) and 33% (Qwen) on 6 real test rows (D-014), with only 146 training negatives. `data/analyze_class1.py` (new) compared each of those 146 with its source positive: 92 are `context_swap` (a same-shaped value from elsewhere in the conversation; tau2-bench 66, BFCL 26) and 54 `type_mismatch` (a value of a visibly different shape; tau2-bench 17, BFCL 37), with 0 `plausible_mutation` and 0 `random`. The existing rows were already grounded in the conversation (the Class 1 filter in D-009 requires it), so the weakness is volume and the share of BFCL rows with an obviously different kind of value, not ungrounded values. `type_mismatch` is a syntactic shape test, not a semantic one.

**What changed:** `data/corrupt_pool.py --class1-context-swap` runs a second LLM pass (gpt-5-nano, low reasoning effort) with a rewritten prompt: the wrong value must be copied exactly from the conversation and be the same kind of value as the one it replaces, with three few-shot examples (order ID, reservation ID, date) and the same JSON output. It runs on tau2-bench positives only, skips the 146 sources that already have a Class 1 negative, and skips conversations with no same-shaped alternative value. Each answer is checked strictly: same tool, same argument names, exactly one argument changed, new value in the context and shaped like the one it replaces; content duplicates are removed. 270 calls gave 200 valid rows (4 empty, 53 invalid, 11 duplicates; the first 450-call attempt failed on an expired key and produced nothing). `filter_llm_negatives.py` re-checks them with the Class 1 quality filters and appends them last, after the Class 2/5 rows, so the first 2,834 rows of `negatives_pool_final.jsonl` are byte-identical to before. **195 of the 200 were kept** (`negatives_class1_context_swap.jsonl`, ids end `::corrupt1v2`). The generator stores `prompt_version: class1_context_swap_v2` on each row.

**New counts:** negatives 3,029 (Class 1 341, Class 2 1,000, Class 3 108, Class 4 1,000, Class 5 380, Class 6 200). Training set 7,580 rows: 4,551 correct and 3,029 incorrect (40.0%), against 24.5% incorrect in `test_real`. Class 1 is now 278 tau2-bench and 63 BFCL; the analysis script puts 286 of the 341 in `context_swap` and 55 in `type_mismatch`. The new 195 span retail 150, telecom 38 and airline 7, and **change mostly one argument: `order_id` 142, `customer_id` 38, `user_id` 14**, so the model may learn "wrong ID" rather than wrong values in general. They have no task overlap with `test_real`, and 195 distinct source positives.

**Caveats:** (1) The new rows are mostly retail ID swaps; airline is nearly absent and BFCL gets nothing new. (2) Like the other Class 1 negatives they have no paired controls; the source positive (correct value) is in the training set, which acts as a natural contrast. (3) A spot check of six rows found realistic swaps; one swapped an order in a "cancel both orders" conversation, which is a legitimate but ambiguous mistake. (4) **The notebooks have not been updated and not retrained:** both assert 7,385 training rows and expect a split of about 6,607 / 778, so they fail on this data until `EXPECTED_ROWS` and the split check are changed. The group split will also shift. README and D-011 to D-013 numbers still describe the 7,385-row dataset.

## D-017 — Evidence-aware input for ModernBERT (`format_input_v2`)

**Date:** 2026-11-11. **Status:** Implemented and measured locally; **not trained** (training happens on Colab). There is no D-016 in this file; the number was skipped on purpose when this entry was assigned.

**Diagnosis.** ModernBERT scores AUROC 0.655 and Qwen 0.671 on `test_real` while validation AUROC is 0.84, because validation errors are synthetic and test errors are real. A second problem sits in the input: v1 (`format_input`, D-011) keeps the last 10 turns and clips every tool result to 200 characters, so the evidence that a call is wrong (the correct ID, the order status, the SIM state) is mostly clipped away. Measured on `test_real` (98 of the 102 rows have tool results), the share of tool-result characters the model sees is a median of **11%** (mean 12.5%, pooled 7.6%). On the 25 real errors: Class 1 10-25% (median 19%), Class 3 5-6% (median 6%), Class 4 2-5% (median 3%), Class 5 5%, Class 7 0-19% (median 14%). The GPT judges of Phase 0 saw the full conversation, which is a plausible reason they reach AUROC 0.71 and 0.67 and recall 3/6 and 5/6 on Class 1 and 3/3 and 2/3 on Class 3 (D-006 correction). This is a hypothesis about cause, not a tested one: v2 has not been trained.

**v2 rules** (`format_input_v2` in `notebooks/phase5_modernbert.ipynb`, Cell 2b; v1 is unchanged):
1. Keep up to the last **30** turns; calls before the window go in `EARLIER_CALLS` as in v1.
2. A tool result is shown **in full** if it contains any string value from the candidate call's arguments (3 or more characters, each list item checked separately, nested values included; numbers are not matched). The **2 most recent** tool results are always shown in full.
3. Every other tool result is clipped to 200 characters, user and agent messages to 500. `TOOLS:` and `CANDIDATE:` are as in v1, with `CANDIDATE` last.
4. Token budget **4,096**. If a row is over, tighten in this order: older non-evidence results (200, 100, 50, 20 characters), then messages (300, 200, 120), then turns kept (20, 12, 8, 5, 3). Full-evidence results and the candidate are never clipped.

**Measured with the real ModernBERT tokenizer** (`notebooks` Cell 2b, run locally; v1 budget 1,500, v2 budget 4,096):

| | v1 | v2 |
|---|---|---|
| `test_real` tokens p50 / p90 / p99 / max | 757 / 972 / 1,177 / 1,239 | 2,187 / 3,356 / 4,070 / 4,093 |
| `test_real` rows over budget before / after tightening | 0 / 0 | 5 / 0 |
| `test_real` tool-result chars visible, median (pooled) | 11.1% (7.6%) | 93.3% (55.7%) |
| training rows (7,580) tokens p50 / p90 / p99 / max | 475 / 942 / 1,158 / 1,433 | 819 / 3,476 / 4,062 / 4,096 |
| training rows over budget before / after tightening | 0 / 0 | 331 / 0 |
| training tool-result chars visible, median (pooled), 5,384 rows with tool results | 15.2% (10.0%) | 100% (82.2%) |
| Class 4 negatives with the earlier duplicate call visible | 999 / 1,000 | 1,000 / 1,000 |

Visibility on the real errors, v1 to v2 (median per row; range): Class 1 19% to 51% (39-100%), Class 3 6% to 100%, Class 4 3% to 15% (11-43%), Class 5 5% to 74%, Class 7 14% to 93% (73-100%). Class 4 stays low because its real examples are 199-205-turn telecom conversations where 30 turns cover a small part of the history; the duplicate itself is visible through `EARLIER_CALLS`.

**Size claims, checked.** (a) The full uncompressed conversation fits 8,192 tokens for **95** of the 102 test rows (94 if the full tool schema text is included), not 96; the longest is 21,252 tokens. The 1-row difference probably comes from how the turns are rendered. (b) The v2 format fits 4,096 tokens for all 102 test rows and all 1,185 `train_pool` trajectories **after the tightening ladder**; **before** it, 97 of 102 test rows and **1,097** of 1,185 trajectories fit, not 1,175. The 88 that do not (86 retail, 2 airline) have a candidate whose item IDs match large product-detail results, which the evidence rule keeps in full. Nothing is over budget after the ladder, so nothing is truncated and the candidate is never cut. If the intended rule matched fewer results, the evidence rule here is the one to compare.

**Training cells changed** (`checkpoints/phase5_v2/`; v1 checkpoints and predictions are not touched): `MAX_LEN = 4096`; SDPA attention (`attn_implementation="sdpa"`); gradient checkpointing (non-reentrant); micro-batch 2 x accumulation 8, falling back to 1 x 16 on a CUDA out-of-memory error (effective batch 16); data, grouped split (6,816 / 764 rows, verified locally), seed, `[CLS]` pooling, lr 2e-5, 3 epochs, warmup 10% and the skip-if-trained guard are as before. Cell 5 now prints a 95% bootstrap interval for AUROC (1,000 resamples, seed 0, the same method as `eval/rescore_phase0.py`) and AUROC without Class 7.

**Checked locally, not run:** the load, v1, v2 and split cells ran end to end with the real tokenizer and the 7,580-row data. The training and evaluation cells were only syntax-checked: PyTorch is not installed here, and no training was run in this session. I confirmed in the installed transformers source that `TrainingArguments` has `gradient_checkpointing_kwargs`, `warmup_steps` and `eval_strategy`, and that ModernBERT supports gradient checkpointing and SDPA. Not verified: fp16 stability, memory at 2 x 4,096 tokens on a T4, and that gradient checkpointing and SDPA work together in the installed version.

**T4 time estimate: about 1.5 hours, plausible range 1 to 2.5 hours; low confidence (a factor of 2 either way).** No ModernBERT run has ever been timed on a T4 for this project (the checkpoints folder is empty), so this is arithmetic, not a measurement. The train split is 9.96 M tokens per epoch (mean 1,461 tokens, p50 842, p90 3,512). Batch-2 padding adds about 50% (15.0 M tokens computed per epoch) because lengths vary widely. Training FLOPs per epoch are about 12 PFLOP with gradient checkpointing (4 x forward; attention is 24% of the forward FLOPs). At a sustained 6, 10 or 15 TFLOPs, which is what I assume a T4 reaches in fp16 (peak is 65, and I expect SDPA without FlashAttention on this GPU to cost efficiency), that is about 49, 29 or 20 minutes per epoch, plus about a minute of validation per epoch: 2.5, 1.5 or 1.0 hours for three epochs. The 10 TFLOPs figure is a guess. If the first logged steps are much slower than 29 minutes per epoch, the cheapest fix is to avoid batch-2 padding waste (group similar lengths) rather than shorten the input.

**Risks to read the result with:**
1. **Train and test lengths differ a lot.** The v2 median is 819 tokens in training and 2,187 on `test_real`, because 44% of the training rows (3,364 of 7,580) are BFCL, which has no tool results, while the real test rows are long tau2-bench conversations (the incorrect ones are long telecom runs, D-010). The model sees few long inputs in training, so a gain in visibility may not turn into a gain in AUROC.
2. **No training signal that the visible evidence is what matters** until the run happens; v2 changes what the model can see, not what it was taught.
3. The evidence rule is a string match, so a correct call whose arguments match generic values (an ID that appears in every result) shows more results in full. The ladder keeps that within budget.
4. The uncommitted tolerance change to the val-size assertion (640 instead of 690, from a Colab run that produced 6,892 / 688) was already in the working tree and is committed with this notebook. `notebooks/phase6_qwen.ipynb` has the same change and was left as it was.

**Update (2026-11-12) — v2 results (Kaggle run on the legacy 7,580-row data; files in `docs/kaggle_v2_*`).** ModernBERT-base with `format_input_v2`, 4,096 tokens, 3 epochs, split 6,816 / 764, trained on 2 x T4 (7.75 hours, see D-020); evaluated on `test_real` (102 rows, 25 incorrect). **AUROC 0.686, 95% interval 0.535-0.834** (1,000 bootstrap resamples); **0.752 without Class 7** (0.577-0.915, 94 rows, 17 incorrect). At threshold 0.5: accuracy 0.716 (always "correct" scores 0.755), precision 0.429, recall 0.480, F1 0.453; confusion matrix: of 77 correct rows 16 flagged, of 25 incorrect rows 12 caught and 13 missed. Recall per class: Class 1 0.33 (n = 6), Class 3 0.33 (3), Class 4 1.00 (7), Class 5 1.00 (1), **Class 7 0.12 (8)**; no real Class 2 or 6 rows. Per domain (accuracy, F1): retail 0.629, 0.235 (5 incorrect); airline 0.829, 0.000 (6 incorrect, none caught at 0.5); telecom 0.688, 0.667 (14 incorrect).

| threshold | F1 | precision | recall | false-flag rate |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0.3 | 0.409 | 0.279 | 0.760 | 0.636 |
| 0.5 | 0.453 | 0.429 | 0.480 | 0.208 |
| **0.7** | 0.323 | **0.833** | 0.200 | **0.013** |

**The 0.7 operating point:** it flags 6 rows, 5 of them real errors (precision 0.833), catches 5 of 25 errors (recall 0.20) and wrongly flags 1 of 77 correct rows (false-flag rate 1.3%). If almost no false alarms is the requirement, this is the setting; it misses four errors in five.

**Comparison on the same `test_real` rows (AUROC):**

| scorer | AUROC |
|---|---|
| ModernBERT v1 (`format_input`, 1,536 tokens; D-011) | 0.655 |
| length-only logistic regression (D-019) | 0.654 |
| GPT-5-mini judge (Phase 0) | 0.667 |
| Qwen2.5-1.5B LoRA, v1 input (D-013) | 0.671 |
| **ModernBERT v2 (this run)** | **0.686** |
| GPT-5-nano judge (Phase 0) | 0.709 |

**What this does and does not show.** v2 is 0.031 above v1, 0.032 above the length baseline and 0.023 below the nano judge, but its interval (0.535-0.834) contains every other number in the table, so **the 102-row test set cannot rank any of these scorers** (v1 and the length baseline agree to three digits; the warning in D-019 still applies). The evidence the input change was meant to produce, reading the tool results, is not in these numbers: Class 7, the class v2 made most visible (tool results 14% to 93% visible, above), is the worst (recall 0.12, 1 of 8), and the gain without Class 7 (0.752) comes from classes with 1 to 7 rows. Validation looks very different: validation AUROC after epochs 1 / 2 / 3 was 0.824 / 0.966 / 0.971 (F1 0.879 at epoch 3), because validation errors are synthetic and test errors are real; the gap from the diagnosis above is unchanged (0.971 against 0.686). The last epoch was the best by validation F1, so there is no sign of overfitting by that criterion. **The time estimate above ("about 1.5 hours, range 1 to 2.5") was wrong by a factor of five: the run took 7.75 hours** (D-020).

## D-018 — Mining real errors from tau2-bench failed runs (stage 1: review file only)

**Date:** 2026-11-11. **Status:** Split locked, candidates picked, 420 runs localized by gpt-5-mini, review file built (273 candidates, none reviewed yet), review tool built. Nothing here is a training or test file.

**Why.** Real errors are the bottleneck: `test_real` has 25 incorrect rows and no real Class 2 or 6 (D-010), and the training negatives are synthetic (validation AUROC 0.84 against 0.655 on `test_real`). The 10,832 simulations in `data/raw/tau2-bench/data/tau2/results/final/` hold about 3,600 failed runs that nobody has used for real labels.

**Numbers verified** (26 files, `reward < 1` means failed): 10,832 simulations, 3,651 failed (retail 500, airline 369, telecom 2,782); 486 failed runs contain a tool error when "error" at the start of a tool result counts (478 using the `error` flag alone). Failed tasks not in `test_real`: telecom 87 as stated, but **retail 67 and airline 24, not 50 and 20**. The 50 and 20 are what you get by excluding by bare `task_id` across domains, which wrongly removes retail tasks that share a number with an airline task in `test_real`; D-008 requires the `(domain, task_id)` pair, which gives 67 and 24. Every failed run has reward exactly 0.0.

**1. The split (`data/derived/real_split.json`, locked, `data/mine_real_errors.py split` refuses to overwrite it).** Every `(domain, task_id)` in `test_real.jsonl` is test (74 tasks). The remaining tasks of each domain are sorted, shuffled with `random.Random(f"{20261111}-{domain}")` and the first 30% go to test. Result: retail 114 tasks (test 53 = 27 + 26 new, train 61), airline 50 (test 30 = 22 + 8, train 20), telecom 114 (test 52 = 25 + 27, train 62).

**Consequence you must act on before retraining:** 37 of the 61 new test tasks (retail 19, airline 3, telecom 15) already appear in the current training files (`train_pool`, the three control files, `negatives_pool_final`), through 1,309 rows (881, 38 and 390; 17% of the 7,580). Those rows must be **removed from training** before the next run, otherwise the new test tasks leak (D-008). The task lists are in `overlap_with_current_training` in `real_split.json`; the filter is "drop rows whose `(domain, task_id)` is in `tasks[domain].test`". I followed the 30/70 rule as written instead of keeping training-used tasks on the train side; the alternative would have left about 28 new test tasks instead of 61, which cuts the measurement the project is short of. If you prefer that, the split must be regenerated before any labeling.

**2. Candidates (`real_run_index.jsonl`, 1,200 runs).** Failed runs, excluding tasks in `test_real` (899 runs), then content duplicates (50 removed; the D-007 hash: sha256 of the rendered turns plus the last call), leaving 2,702; then at most 3 runs per `(task, agent model)`, chosen with a fixed seed (1,502 dropped). Selected: retail 294, airline 120, telecom 786 runs, covering 67, 24 and 87 failed tasks; 823 runs are on train tasks and 377 on test tasks; 239 contain a tool error.

**What `reward_info` shows about where a run went wrong** (over all 3,651 failed runs). The checker records outcomes, not steps. `db_check.db_match` is false in 3,521 (96%); at least one expected action was missed in 3,346 (92%); 316 have an unmet natural-language assertion and 162 an unmet "communicate" check; only 72 (2%) have every expected action matched and a correct database. The reward basis is mostly `ENV_ASSERTION` (2,746 runs, telecom), then `DB` (905), `COMMUNICATE` (869), `ACTION` (207). So it tells you which expected action or end state was missing, but never which call in the run was the wrong one, and an unmet exact-match action is often an artifact (D-007: about 75% of such failures were not agent errors). That is why a localization step and human review are needed.

**3. Localization with an LLM (offline labeling only, never part of the model).** `localize` gives gpt-5-mini (reasoning effort low) the policy, a short checker summary and the full run (agent calls numbered C0, C1, ...; tool results capped at 3,000 characters), with the class definitions and priority order from the taxonomy and the instruction that expected actions are noisy hints. It returns JSON: the index of the first incorrect agent call or `"none"`, a class 1 to 7, and one sentence of evidence. The first batch is 150 runs per domain (airline has only 120, so all of them): 420 runs, with heuristic-flagged runs first and a seeded random fill. **Cost estimate: $1.44** (2.73 M input tokens, 900 assumed output tokens per run, gpt-5-mini at $0.25 / $2.00 per 1M; the limit was $4). The script prints the estimate and refuses to send anything above the limit; every response is cached in `real_localization_cache.jsonl`, keyed by run and prompt version, so a rerun only sends what is missing.

**Result (run 2026-11-11, after replacing a rejected key; the first attempt failed with 401 and spent $0).** All 420 runs were labeled for **$1.16** (estimate $1.44: runs averaged 5,961 input and 632 output tokens against the 6,506 and 900 assumed). Outcomes: **273 runs got a flagged call, 144 returned `"none"`** (airline 39 of 120, retail 77 of 150, telecom 28 of 150), and 3 answers could not be parsed (raw text is in the cache). Proposed classes over the 273: Class 7 139 (51%: airline 55, telecom 47, retail 37), Class 4 45 (34 telecom), Class 1 41, Class 3 28, Class 5 19, Class 6 1, **Class 2 0**. By domain airline 80, retail 73, telecom 120; by split train 188, test 85; by agent model gpt-4.1-mini 83, claude-3-7-sonnet 64, gpt-4.1 70, o4-mini 56. These are the model's proposals: nobody has checked them, and Class 7 in particular may be over-proposed (the 8 real Class 7 rows in `test_real` were policy violations, D-014, which an LLM given the policy will readily find).

**4. Heuristics for Classes 2 and 6 (review order only).** Class 6: a tool returned an error and the agent's next move is a write call (a different tool, not a read-only lookup, not `transfer_to_human_agents`), or its next message does not mention the error and a write call follows. Class 2: after a user message with a correction cue ("actually", "instead", "I meant", ...) that does not ask to revert, a later call still contains a value from an earlier user message and none of the new value of the same shape. Over the 1,200 selected runs they fire on 28 (Class 6) and 4 (Class 2). **They are low precision**: a sample of 5 flagged candidates held two clear false positives (a hand-off to a human after an error, and a user asking to change an address back), which I then excluded; I have not measured the precision of what remains. **The LLM did not confirm them:** of the 28 Class 6 and 4 Class 2 flagged runs it proposed Class 6 for none and Class 2 for none (they came back as Classes 1, 3, 4, 5, 7 or none), and it named the same call as a heuristic in only 1 of 30 cases. The heuristics are therefore only a fallback now (`proposal_source: heuristic` applies only to flagged runs with no LLM answer, and there are none), and Classes 2 and 6 remain essentially absent from real data, as in D-007.

**5. Review file and tool.** `real_candidates_review.jsonl` has one row per candidate: run id, task, domain, agent model, split, the flagged call, proposed class, evidence, `proposal_source` (`llm` or `heuristic`), up to 20 turns before and 4 after the flagged call (tool results capped at 3,000 characters), the checker summary, and empty `human_label`, `human_class`, `human_note`. Order: rare classes (2, 3, 6, 7) and airline first. It now has **273 rows, all from the LLM** (see the result above; `real_candidates_samples.txt` prints five of them in full, one each of Classes 1, 3, 4, 7 and 6). **The tool is `data/review_real.py`, not `data/review.py`**: `data/review.py` is the Phase 1 labeling tool that the README documents, and overwriting it would have destroyed that. It records accept, change class, reject or skip, appends each decision to `real_review_decisions.jsonl` with an fsync before the next candidate appears, and resumes at the first undecided one; `--merge` writes the decisions into the review file and updates the linked positives. Tested with scripted input on a scratch copy: decisions persisted, a bad command was refused, rerunning resumed at the right place, `--include-skipped` returned the skipped one, `--merge` filled the fields.

**6. Paired correct examples (`real_positive_candidates.jsonl`).** For each flagged run, every agent call before the flagged one that did not return an error (and is not a hand-off to a human) is listed with a link to its candidate, `status: "pending"`: 1,327 now. They are references (run, call index, turn index), not training rows, and `--merge` marks them `accepted` or `rejected` with the candidate. Caveat: a call before the first incorrect call is only assumed correct (it can be redundant or mildly wrong), so treat them as unverified positives.

**Before any of this becomes a dataset:** finish the LLM step, review, drop the 1,309 overlapping rows from training, check content duplicates across the split (telecom task ids can realise the same conversation, D-007), and decide how to cap long tool results (the Phase 2 pools cut them at 2,000 characters; the review window cuts at 3,000).

### Follow-up (2026-11-12): natural Class 5 search and a second labeler for the train candidates

**Natural Class 5 (`data/mine_class5.py`, no API).** Over the 780 failed runs that were not localized, a call is proposed as Class 5 when it uses a different value than a checker-missed action of the same tool and (tier A) the value it used appeared in an earlier tool result while the expected value arrived in a later result, or (tier B) the expected value is in one of the 3 most recent results and the value used appears only in older context. It found **21 candidates, all retail: 19 tier A, 2 tier B; test 7, train 14**. They are in `real_candidates_review.jsonl` with `proposal_source: "heuristic_class5"` and `origin: "natural"` (existing rows untouched), with 132 linked pending positives. **Expect low precision**: the first tier-A examples I read are an agent choosing the wrong one of several orders listed for the same user, which is closer to Class 1 than to a value that changed. The heuristic needs the checker's missed action, so it cannot see the airline and telecom runs without one.
