**Second opinion (`data/second_opinion.py`).** For the 202 natural train-split candidates (188 LLM, 14 heuristic Class 5), gpt-5-nano at medium reasoning effort labeled the run again with a freshly written prompt (a yes/no question per class in a different order, a different answer format, and no access to the first labeler's answer). **Cost $0.360** (estimate $0.352, cap $0.40, 202 runs, none unparsed). A candidate is `auto_accepted` when both name the same call and class, `auto_rejected` otherwise; test-split candidates are `test_needs_human_review` and never auto-accepted. **Agreement is low: 39 of 202 (19%) accepted, 163 rejected.**

| First labeler's class | n | same call | same call and class | second says "none" |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 wrong arg | 29 | 13 | 9 (31%) | 13 |
| 3 wrong tool | 17 | 5 | 4 (24%) | 8 |
| 4 repeated call | 32 | 0 | 0 (0%) | 17 |
| 5 failure to propagate | 29 | 9 | 0 (0%) | 17 |
| 7 missing precondition | 95 | 39 | 26 (27%) | 40 |

Read this as "the two cheap labelers disagree", not as "the first one was wrong": for Class 5 the second labeler named the same call 9 times but called it Class 1 (5), Class 7 (3) or Class 6 (1), so the 1/5 boundary is the issue; for Class 4 it never named the same call and answered "none" in 17 of 32. The 163 `auto_rejected` rows are not deleted and are not evidence that the first labeler was wrong; the 39 `auto_accepted` rows are those both agree on, and queue 3 (D-019) audits 40 accepted rows by hand to measure their precision.


---

## D-019 — Inducing Class 2 and Class 6 situations, and outside sources for them

**Date:** 2026-11-12. **Status:** pilot, scale-up, soft faults and induced value changes run and auto-labeled (nothing reviewed by a human yet; review queues built); CriticTool converted to a separate file; no training, nothing merged into the training files. See the Follow-up at the end of this entry.

**Why.** Real Class 2 (stale value) and Class 6 (ignored error) are almost absent from the failed tau2-bench runs (0 and 1 of 273 LLM proposals, D-018): users rarely change their minds and tools rarely fail. So we create the situation and let a real agent react. We change the environment and the user's instructions, never the agent's calls.

**Part A: what runs locally.** tau2-bench runs as a library (`tau2.runner.run_single_task`; CLI `tau2 run --domain ... --agent-llm ... --user-llm ...`), models go through litellm and read `OPENAI_API_KEY`, which our `.env` provides (loaded with python-dotenv, never printed). It declares Python >= 3.12 and we have 3.11, but the source is 3.11-compatible. Install steps that worked: `python3.11 -m venv data/raw/.venv-tau2` (gitignored), `pip install --upgrade pip` (the old pip found no wheels for this macOS and tried to build numpy), `pip install` the pyproject dependencies normally (plain `--ignore-requires-python` pulls a numpy that needs 3.12), `pip install --no-deps --ignore-requires-python -e data/raw/tau2-bench`, then `pip install websockets aiohttp` (voice modules import them). Domain tools raise `ValueError`; the environment turns any exception into a tool message `Error: <text>` with `error=True` (`environment.get_response`). Error styles already used: retail "Order not found", "User not found", "Non-pending order cannot be modified", "Payment method not found", "Insufficient gift card balance ..."; airline "Reservation {id} not found", "Not enough seats on flight {n}", "Gift card balance is not enough", "Certificate cannot be used to update reservation"; telecom "Customer with phone number {p} not found", "Line must be suspended to resume", "Bill {id} not found for customer {c}". Tasks are in `domains/<d>/tasks.json`: the user simulator gets `reason_for_call`, `known_info`, `unknown_info` and `task_instructions` inside a `<scenario>` block, plus generic guidelines (disclose progressively, never invent facts, emit `###STOP###`); gold actions are in `evaluation_criteria.actions`.

**Part B: the two perturbations (`data/induce_errors.py`).**
- **Fault (Class 6).** A wrapper environment (domain `<d>__fault`) fails ONE assistant call: a lookup or a write call, kind and position (the 1st or 2nd call of that kind) chosen by a fixed seed per task. The call is not executed, so state is unchanged, and the tool returns `Error: <message>`, with the message drawn from the domain's own list for that tool (above), plus "Service temporarily unavailable. Please try again later." which you asked for and which is not a tau2 message. The faulted call (tool call id, name, arguments, kind, message) is recorded; if the run never reaches that call it is `fault_not_triggered`.
- **Correction (Class 2).** The user simulator's `task_instructions` get one added mid-conversation change of mind. Types: payment method, address, checked bags (quantity), passenger date of birth, flight date, data refuel amount (quantity), returned item. Each is built from a gold write action of the task (old value = the gold argument, new value = a different valid one: another method the user owns, another user's address, bags + 1, another item in the same order, date + 1 or + 10, refuel amount 1.0) and records old value, new value, type, wording (4 forms x 7 openings x 4 timings, so not one template) and timing; every timing says "before the agent makes any change" (a first smoke run showed a correction after the write cannot test anything). The actual correction turn is found in the transcript afterwards: the first user message after the old value was stated that contains the new value and a correction cue.
- **Split.** Only tasks in `real_split.json`; each row stores `split` from it and `source: "induced_fault"` or `"induced_correction"`.

**Part C: pilot.** Agent gpt-5-nano (reasoning effort low); user simulator **gpt-4.1-mini**: gpt-4.1-nano, the cheaper candidate, is retired on the API (404 "deprecated"), and gpt-4.1-mini delivers the correction in most runs at about $0.006 a run, so I did not test further models. Evaluation type `ACTION` (the checker needs an evaluation type; this one makes no LLM calls). Max steps 50 (retail, airline) and 60 (telecom). **Cost estimate printed before the run: $1.37** (calibrated from the existing gpt-4.1 runs' recorded costs, scaled by price, with a x2 reasoning factor; the $5 limit was not close). **Spent: $1.35** over the 148 recorded runs (about $0.05 more went on 6 runs interrupted when I restarted with more workers). All runs cached in `data/raw/induced/induced_runs.jsonl` (full messages; moved out of git on 2026-11-12, see the second follow-up); 0 errors.

Plan: 148 runs, not 150: only **23 airline tasks** support a correction (25 per domain otherwise): 73 correction runs, 75 fault runs; 77 on test-split tasks, 71 on train-split.

| Domain | Runs | Class 2 candidates | Class 6 candidates | Correct handlings (paired positives) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| retail | 50 | 3 | 0 | 43 |
| airline | 48 | 3 | 1 | 37 |
| telecom | 50 | 0 | 1 | 20 |
| **total** | **148** | **6** | **2** | **100** |

Candidates by split: train 5, test 3. These are auto-labels only; a human must confirm each.

**Agent failure rates under each perturbation.**
- Class 6 (faults): the fault fired in 55 of 75 runs (retail 21 of 25, airline 16, telecom 18; 20 never reached the chosen call; of the 55, 35 were lookups and 20 writes). The agent proceeded as if the call had worked in **2 of 55 (3.6%)** (one lookup in airline, one write in telecom); in 52 it acknowledged the error, retried, looked something else up or asked the user, and in 1 it stopped after informing the user. gpt-5-nano handles injected errors well.
- Class 2 (corrections): the correction was delivered in 46 of 73 runs (63%), was never delivered in 10, and the old value was never stated in 17 (16 of them telecom). In 38 of the 46 a later call touched the changed field, and **6 of those 38 (16%)** used the old value (retail 3, airline 3, telecom 0). **Telecom corrections do not work**: 23 of 25 runs hit the step limit and the user rarely states the refuel amount first, so only 4 corrections were delivered; use retail and airline for Class 2.
- The labels are weak in places. Of the 6 Class 2 candidates, 2 are airline payment corrections to a travel certificate, which airline policy forbids for updates (the old method may be correct; flagged in the evidence), and 2 more carry "the user mentioned the old value again later". The 2 Class 6 candidates: acting after a "temporarily unavailable" lookup without mentioning it (a likely real Class 6) and a telecom `enable_roaming` after a faulted `disable_roaming` (may be a legitimate recovery).

**Scaling to about 40 real errors per class** (yields from this pilot: Class 2 6 of 73 correction runs, 12.5% if telecom is dropped (6 of 48); Class 6 2 of 75 fault runs, 2.7%; both with a very wide interval, 2 and 6 events; cost about $0.009 a run, $0.007 in retail and airline). Assuming every candidate is confirmed: Class 2 needs about 320 retail and airline runs, about $2.5; Class 6 about 1,500 runs, about $13. If only half are confirmed (a guess; none reviewed yet): about $5 and $27. Class 6 is the expensive one because gpt-5-nano rarely ignores an error. A stronger or weaker agent, or faulting a write call right after the agent has told the user it will act, would change that, but that is untested.

**Review files.** `induced_candidates_review.jsonl` (8 rows, same format as `real_candidates_review.jsonl` plus `source` and `induced` details, auto-label in `proposed_class` and `evidence`, empty `human_*`) and `induced_positive_candidates.jsonl` (100 correct handlings, `status: pending`). Review with `python data/review_real.py --candidates data/derived/induced_candidates_review.jsonl --decisions data/derived/induced_review_decisions.jsonl --positives data/derived/induced_positive_candidates.jsonl`. `induced_candidates_samples.txt` prints five candidates in full (3 Class 2, both Class 6; only 2 Class 6 exist). Class 6 positives are the agent's next call after the faulted one; Class 2 positives are calls after the correction that use the new value.

**Part D: other sources (research only, nothing downloaded for these).**
- **BFCL multi-turn execution errors.** Its tools do return errors: the file system returns `{"error": "..."}` for "No such file or directory", "File exists", "Is a directory", "Not a directory", "Directory not empty", "Unsupported path. Only one folder level at a time is supported.", and any exception becomes the string `Error during execution: <message>` (`multi_turn_utils.py`). So an agent can ignore them, but the executable code is not in our sparse checkout (only data and constants); running it needs `bfcl-eval` (heavy, D-006) or fetching `func_source_code`. Rough cost from the local data (function-doc size and turns per case, about 3 calls per turn, x2 output for reasoning, about 4 characters per token, so treat as plus or minus 2x): gpt-5-nano about $1.8 per 200 cases and about $7 for all 800 (base, long-context, miss-function, miss-parameter); gpt-5-mini about $9 and $36. How many runs contain an ignored error is unknown until a small trial.
- **Public datasets** (checked on arXiv, GitHub or Hugging Face pages in this session; where a page did not say, "not stated"):

| Dataset | Exists? / license / size | Real or synthetic errors | Ignored tool errors (6) | User corrections (2) | Fits our format |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| CriticTool (arXiv 2506.13977) | yes; dataset card Apache-2.0 (GitHub shows no license; paper CC BY 4.0); external_error files 8.7 MB, 799 records, 439 distinct | API-failure texts (rate limits, resets, timeouts, quotas); how produced is not stated | no recorded mishandling, only ground-truth next actions | no | yes, converted below |
| SpecTool = ToolScan (arXiv 2411.13547) | yes, v1 was titled SpecTool and v2 ToolScan; paper CC BY 4.0; data link and size not on the page (a search summary says 150 human-annotated queries, unconfirmed) | model outputs per the abstract | not stated | not stated | unknown |
| ToolFailBench (arXiv 2607.04686, July 2026, ICML 2026) | yes; repo Apache-2.0; 1,000 tasks (5 domains x 200), traces in a separate HF dataset (license not stated) | real model traces from 19 models, labels from a rule classifier plus two LLM judges | covers ignoring tool *results* (Result-Ignore), not tool errors | no (single task) | no (single-turn "parametric trap" tasks), closest to Class 5 |
| ParaRecover (arXiv 2609.12345, EMNLP 2026) | yes (the ID looks odd but the page exists); repo MIT; 10,626 instances, 14 error types, JSONL | not stated | not stated (error localization and recovery in parallel tool use) | not stated | unclear (DAG of parallel tool calls) |
| ToolRobustBench (arXiv 2608.23635) | yes; 15,456 instances, 7 models; no code or data link or license on the page | perturbations; how injected not stated | tool-output perturbations are the main finding | has a "user-intent" family | cannot tell without data |
| ToolSandbox (arXiv 2408.04682) | yes; repo has a LICENSE file (name not read), scenarios are code, trajectories are generated at run time and not shipped | n/a | errors appear in dialogues | scenarios include insufficient-information turns | would need running it |

No public text dataset with real user corrections inside tool-use conversations was found (the closest, Full-Duplex-Bench-v3, is voice with mid-utterance self-corrections). So induced runs remain the only source of Class 2.

**Part E: CriticTool converter (`data/convert_critictool.py` -> `data/derived/external_critictool.jsonl`, 878 rows, training split only, not merged).** License: Apache-2.0 on the Hugging Face dataset card (the card's README is empty; the GitHub repo shows no license), so I treated use as allowed and kept the source and license in this entry. I downloaded only the four `external_error/*.json` files (8.7 MB) into `data/raw/critictool/` (gitignored, parsed as data, never run). Every record ends in an environment error and its `ground_truth` starts with a retry of the failed call, followed by the next step. **The dataset stores no model outputs, so there are no recorded cases of an agent mishandling the error.** I therefore built both sides from the ground truth, paired through the same context: correct = the retry (439 rows), Class 6 = the next step taken as if the call had worked (439 rows, `error_class: 6`, `source: "critictool"`). The negatives are constructed, not model mistakes, and the next step does not always need the failed call's output, so they are noisier than real Class 6. 799 records collapse to 439 after removing duplicate contexts across the four files (base, long-context, noisy-query, mixed). You said these errors are GPT-4o simulated; I could not confirm that from the pages I read (the texts look like genuine API failures), which does not change the train-only rule.

**Source-shortcut baseline (`eval/shortcut_baselines.py`).** There was no length baseline in the evaluation scripts, so the script adds both and prints them together, on the notebook's grouped split. Current 7,580 training rows, validation AUROC: **length 0.575, source (tau2 / bfcl) 0.569**, both 0.578; with the 878 CriticTool rows added in memory (50% incorrect): length 0.574, source 0.571, both 0.584. On `test_real` a length model trained on all training rows scores **0.654**, essentially the trained ModernBERT's 0.655: the incorrect real rows are long telecom runs. So the reported test AUROC is no evidence that the model reads the error. Re-run the script after merging any outside data and compare the trained model against it.

**Deviations and caveats.** (1) 148 runs, not 150 (airline has 23 eligible tasks). (2) The tool for the pilot is `induce_errors.py` under the tau2 environment, not the tau2 CLI, because the per-run fault and instruction edits need the Python API. (3) Auto-labels for Class 2 are simple: any later call whose changed-field argument equals the old value counts, which is why a user re-asking for the old value or a policy rule can make it a false positive; for Class 6, "acknowledged" is a keyword and question-mark test on the agent's text. (4) The pilot has 2 Class 6 candidates; every rate above rests on very few events. (5) The 100 correct handlings are auto-labeled correct, not reviewed. (6) The user simulator sometimes invents a detail (a credit card "ending in 4127"); it does not affect the labels but it is not in the task.

### Follow-up (2026-11-12): user model, scale-up, soft faults, induced value changes, review queues, coverage

Hard limit for the follow-up prompt $4.50; **spent $3.64** (`data/derived/api_spend.json`: step 0 $0.076, step 1 $1.498, smoke runs $0.108 (counted inside steps 2 and 3), step 2 $0.753 in all, step 3 $0.954 in all, step 5 $0.360). The pilot's $1.35 is not in that file. Runs were done in batches of 14 to 24 with a printed estimate and a cap check before each batch; every paid step stopped for a stated reason below. All runs: `data/raw/induced/induced_runs.jsonl` (gitignored); plans: `induced_plan_{usersim,class2,soft,soft_r1,prop}.jsonl`.

**Step 0: gpt-5-nano as the user simulator (10 runs, retail 5 + airline 5, the first ten pilot corrections; $0.076, cap $0.20).** Judged "delivered properly" = the old value stated first and the correction arriving before the agent's first write. gpt-5-nano: **7 of 10** (9 of 10 delivered at all; one never stated the old value; 2 arrived after the agent had already written). gpt-4.1-mini on the same 10 tasks: 3 of 10 before the first write (9 of 10 delivered at all). So **the rule (at least 8 of 10) was not met and I kept gpt-4.1-mini for steps 1 to 3**, although nano scored better on this strict measure. The reasons it did not win: nano put the correction in the user's second message in 3 of its 7 proper runs and by message 7 in 6 of 7, whatever timing was asked for (so the timing variation is mostly lost), in one retail run it spoke like an agent ("Please provide the following to proceed...") and the cost was the same ($0.076 against $0.077 for the same ten runs), since the agent dominates the cost. One finding for later: with gpt-4.1-mini only 3 of 10 pilot corrections arrived before the first write, which lowers the Class 2 yield (below).

**Step 1: Class 2 scale-up (retail and airline, `source: "induced_correction"`, cap $1.50, spent $1.498, stopped by the cap).** 192 new runs plus the 48 pilot runs = 240 (retail 148, airline 92), same perturbation and split rules, test tasks first (until the test split had 20 candidates, reached by the 7th batch of 24; then train), up to 3 repetitions per task with another field type, another wording and timing where the task allows (`::r1`, `::r2`, `::r3`). Correction delivered in 207 of 240 (86%); 164 had a later call touching the changed field and **30 of them (18%) used the old value** (pilot: 16%). **Candidates: 39 (test 21, train 18)**, up from 6; test-split types: flight date 10, returned item 7, payment 4 (no address or baggage candidates came out). Cases where policy may make the old value correct (airline payment to a travel certificate: 4) and cases where the user mentions the old value again later (21) are flagged in `auto_flags`. 8 train rows are unambiguous (`auto_accepted`), 10 flagged.

**Step 2: Class 6 quiet errors (`source: "induced_fault_soft"`, test tasks only, all three domains, cap $1.20, spent $0.753).** The wrapper does not execute the chosen assistant call and returns a normal-looking response in the tool's own schema: the record exactly as it still is (retail `get_order_details` / `get_user_details` result for a modify, cancel, return or exchange call; airline `get_reservation_details` result for a flight, bag, passenger or cancel call; telecom `suspend_line`, `resume_line`, `refuel_data` with a "no change" message) or the tool's own empty result (airline flight searches `[]`, retail `list_all_product_types` `{}`). I did not use `{"status": "declined"}`-style bodies because none of the domains returns that shape. Plan: every test task whose gold actions include a soft-capable tool (retail 47, airline 12, telecom 15 (capped)), then a second pass over the tasks that offer a second occurrence (27) with the other occurrence: 101 runs. **Fault triggered in 78 (77%)**; telecom reached it in 3 of 15. **Failure rate 20 of 78 (26%)**: 16 proceeded to a write without noticing (retail 9, airline 7, telecom 0) and 4 told the user it was done without a further call (retail); 22 ended by informing the user, 36 acknowledged, re-read or retried. This is far above the hard-error rate (3.6%), so the 5% early stop never applied. **Candidates: 16 test** (airline 7, retail 9; plus 1 from the pilot's hard faults = 17 test Class 6), short of the target of 20 because every eligible test task had been used (cap not reached). The 4 "claimed success" runs have no flagged call and give no row. Quiet failures are the realistic Class 6, and the review screen shows the unchanged record next to the call.

**Step 3: Class 5 induced value changes (`source: "induced_propagation"`, cap $1.00, spent $0.954).** The first lookup (`get_order_details` / `get_reservation_details`) of the id the agent is working with, an id from the task's gold actions, returns the record under a **new id** (retail: an order split into a new order id `#W` + 7 digits, airline: a reservation reissued under a new 6-character code) and a `previous_order_id` / `previous_reservation_id` field with the old one (a small schema extension: the real schemas have no such field). From then on the environment accepts the new id (translated back to the stored record), answers the old id with the domain's own "not found" error, and rewrites the old id in every later result. The agent's calls are never changed. Telecom (a migrated line) was not built: telecom runs hit the step limit in almost every run. Old and new value, reveal call and message are recorded; every later call using the old id is a Class 5 candidate (first one per run), one using the new id is correct. 144 runs (retail 109, airline 35; the 72 test tasks first): the change was revealed in 123 (85%); 82 had a later call using either id and **41 of them (50%) used the old id**, a much higher rate than for faults or corrections, partly because the id also sits in the user's own task text (flagged `user_repeated_old_value`: 18 candidates). **Candidates 41 (test 19, train 22)**, one short of the target of 20 test because the test tasks ran out. The early stop (under 5% after 60 runs) did not apply. Caveat: the old-id call fails with "not found", so a stale call is quickly visible to the agent; the label is on the call, not on a final outcome.

**Auto-acceptance of induced train candidates.** `auto_accepted` = a train candidate with no flag (no policy note, no later user mention of the old value, no assistant text between a faulted call and its next call); flagged ones are `needs_human_review`; test candidates are `test_needs_human_review`. Induced train candidates: 41, of which 21 are `auto_accepted` (Class 5 12, Class 2 8, Class 6 1). Natural train candidates auto-accepted by the second labeler: 39 (D-018 follow-up). Pool for the audit: 60.

**Step 6: review queues (`data/review_queues.py`, frozen in `review_queue.jsonl`, decisions in `review_queue_decisions.jsonl`).** Run `python data/review_queues.py review` (resumes; `--queue N`; `stats`; `merge`). Every decision stores `origin` (natural or induced), the queue and the item kind, and the screen says ORIGIN in capitals and shows the injection (the fault and what the tool returned, or old value -> new value with the correction as the user said it, or the old -> new id) plus the auto-label, flags and, for natural train rows, the second labeler's answer. Tested with scripted input: a decision was saved before the next item, rerunning resumed at the next item, and `stats` counted by origin.

| Queue | Items | Estimated time |
|---|---|---|
| 1. all test-split error candidates, order 2/5/6, then 3/1, then 4/7 (induced 57: class 2 21, class 5 19, class 6 17; natural 92: class 1 12, 3 11, 4 13, 5 11, 6 1, 7 44) | 149 | about 149 min (1 min each) |
| 2. random correct examples, test split, seeded, stratified by domain (airline 46, retail 77, telecom 27; induced 48, natural 102) | 150 | about 50 min (20 s each) |
| 3. random auto-accepted train candidates (natural 26, induced 14, drawn from a pool of 60) | 40 | about 40 min |
| total | 339 | about 4 h |

Queue 3 measures the precision of auto-acceptance; queue 2 measures how often an assumed-correct call is actually correct. `--merge` writes `human_label`, `human_class`, `human_note`, `human_queue`, `human_origin` into the candidate files and flips `status` on the correct examples. Nothing here writes a training or test file.

**Step 7: coverage projection (`data/coverage_projection.py`, 65% of test-split candidates assumed confirmed, plus the 25 rows already in `test_real`).** Class 1 13.8 (6 + 7.8 natural), **Class 2 13.7 (all induced)**, Class 3 10.2, Class 4 15.5, **Class 5 20.5 (7.2 natural, 12.3 induced, 1 in test_real)**, **Class 6 11.7 (0.7 natural, 11.1 induced)**, Class 7 36.6. Nothing falls below 10, but Class 3 is at 10.2, Class 2 and Class 6 exist only as induced behaviour (a classifier tested on them measures detection in perturbed runs, not in the benchmark's own runs), and Class 5 is 60% induced; report recall for natural and induced rows separately. The 65% is a guess, and the Class 5 natural heuristic in particular looks lower.

**What is still unverified.** All of the above are auto-labels. The induced Class 5 and Class 2 rows share artifacts (a `previous_*` field, an old value present in the task text), so a model could learn those artifacts instead of the behaviour: re-run `eval/shortcut_baselines.py` with the induced rows before merging any of them into training. The soft-fault "claimed success" runs (4) have no flagged call. The airline certificate corrections remain policy-ambiguous.


### Second follow-up (2026-11-12): repo hygiene, precision reporting, artifact check, pairing (no API, no training)

**Run files out of git.** `data/derived/induced_runs.jsonl` (37 MB of full transcripts) is now `data/raw/induced/induced_runs.jsonl`, under the existing `data/raw/` ignore, removed from the index with `git rm --cached`; `induce_errors.py` and `review_queues.py` read the new path. **The repo has no remote** (`git remote -v` is empty, one local branch `main`), so the file was **never pushed**; it is still in local history (commits 314fd25 and 54a02d6), which I did not rewrite, so the `.git` folder (120 MB in total now) still holds it until you decide to rewrite that history or never push it. The other tracked files over 5 MB are datasets rather than run dumps (the negatives and positives pools, `train_pool`, `bfcl_pool`, `external_critictool`, 7 to 34 MB each) and were left. `induced_runs_usersim.jsonl` (0.9 MB) stays.

**Labeler precision in `review_queues.py stats`.** After human review it prints, for the test split (queue 1) and the train audit (queue 3): per class, and per source, how many first-labeler proposals the human confirmed with the same call and class, accepted with another class, or rejected (an accept always means the same call: the reviewer judges the flagged call and cannot move it); a per-class recommendation, "auto-accept train labels" only when the confirmed same-class precision is at least 70% with at least 8 reviewed, otherwise "human review or drop" (classes with no reviewed test candidate also get "human review or drop"); the same table for the second labeler; the induced sources' auto-labels; and the confirmed rate of the correct examples. Natural and induced are always separate rows. Limit of the second labeler's table: it only labeled natural train candidates, and queue 3 draws only the ones it agreed on, so it can measure the precision of its agreement and never of its disagreements. Dry-run with synthetic random decisions (`--decisions <file>`) to check the output, nothing real was recorded.

**Shortcut check for the induced rows (`python eval/shortcut_baselines.py --induced`).** 510 rows: the 98 error candidates and the 412 paired correct handlings, features computed only from the history before the call. Results:
- Single features, AUROC (0.5 = none): `previous_*` field present 0.588 pooled but **0.500 inside every source** (it only marks the propagation runs, so pooled it is a proxy for the source); old value in the user's text 0.510 pooled, **0.620 within propagation**; "not found" in history 0.402 pooled, 0.266 within propagation (i.e. 0.734 the other way), 0.279 within hard faults (2 errors only, ignore), 0.590 within soft faults. Length: 0.444.
- Classifiers on the induced rows alone, 5-fold grouped by task, out-of-fold AUROC: source label only **0.629**; the three artifact features **0.645**; artifact + source **0.679**; length only 0.553; all together 0.684. The source alone beats chance because the error share differs by source (corrections 15%, hard faults 4%, soft faults 31%, propagation 29%).
- **One feature is over 0.65: "not found" in history, within propagation (0.734 flipped).** Cause: the candidate is the first stale call, while paired correct calls come after an earlier stale attempt that already got "not found" back, so a "not found" in the history points to "correct".
- **Proposed fix, tested** (kept in the script): (1) drop propagation positives whose history already holds "not found"; (2) match positives 1:1 to candidates inside every source, type, domain and split cell; (3) inside a cell also match on "old value in the user's text". After it, 174 rows (98 errors, 76 correct): source alone 0.510 (was 0.629), artifact features alone 0.525, artifact + source 0.506 (was 0.679): the classifier-level shortcuts disappear. **Not fully removed:** inside propagation "old value in the user's text" still scores 0.686 (n small), because a stale call almost always happens when the user's own text carries the old id (18 candidates are flagged `user_repeated_old_value`) and there are too few positives with that property to match. Options before any merge: keep only the matched rows; drop or down-weight the propagation candidates flagged `user_repeated_old_value`; or, with new API runs, collect more correct handlings where the user repeats the old id.

**Paired positives (same script, `pairing` table).** Every error-candidate cell (source, type, domain, split) has correct handlings except one: hard-fault write in telecom test has 1 and 1, fine; passenger date of birth in airline train has **4 candidates and 2 correct**, and airline payment (train) is 4 and 4. Overall **test: 57 errors, 235 correct (4.1 per error); train: 41 errors, 177 correct (4.3 per error)**. By source, counting only cells that have candidates: corrections test 21 errors / 66 correct, train 18 / 65, spread unevenly (flight date test 10/39, train 4/36; payment retail test 4/12, train 3/5; returned item test 7/15, train 3/18); soft faults test 16/36 (airline 7/8, retail 9/25); propagation test 19/52 (retail 14/36, airline 5/16), train 22/48 (retail 16/38, airline 6/10). The numbers are comparable (about 1 to 4 per error, never zero) except the airline date-of-birth train cell. Positives also exist for types with no error at all (retail address 78, airline baggage 9, telecom refuel 4, hard-fault lookups and writes), which would unbalance a training set if used as they are; use only cells that have candidates, as the fixed set above does. Hard faults have 2 candidates against 56 positives, so that source adds almost nothing for Class 6.

**Update (2026-11-14) — why the induced Class 2 and Class 6 auto-checks produced false candidates (queue-1 review, `data/strict_check.py`).** Queue 1 is complete (149 decisions: 79 accepted, 31 reclassified, 22 rejected, 17 skipped). The first checks confirmed **3 of 16** induced Class 2 candidates (6 moved to other classes, 7 rejected; 5 more skipped) and **5 of 13** induced Class 6 candidates (5 moved, 3 rejected; 4 more skipped); induced Class 5 was confirmed 19 of 19.

*Class 2, what went wrong (from the 21 transcripts).* The check is "a call after the correction uses the old value at the corrected field", which is true for many correct calls:
1. **The call re-sends the record's current state (8 of the 10 airline items, 0 confirmed).** `update_reservation_flights` must list every flight, so a call that only upgrades the cabin carries the date the reservation really has; the correction was about a later step (t32: "once the upgrade is confirmed we can change the flight to May 22") or about another reservation. Your outcomes for those 8: rejected 4, Class 7 twice, Class 6 once, Class 4 once.
2. **The correction is about another object than the call.** t37: the user corrects the date of the cancellation of IFOYYZ, the flagged call upgrades M20IZO. t30: the correction is about the espresso machine in an exchange order, the flagged call returns the sneakers from another order.
3. **The user brought the old value back or reverted.** t11: after "use PayPal instead" the user says "return everything to the original payment methods" (the check only searched for the word "credit"); t16: the user later asks separately to return the Smart Watch; t56: the gift card comes back for the refund; t30: "please proceed with the return of the sneakers now". 7 of the 16 reviewed items.
4. **The call is really another class.** A retry after a failed call (t32 second upgrade attempt, "gift card balance is not enough": Class 6), an identical repeat of an earlier call (t37: Class 4), a return on an order that is not delivered (t30: Class 7).
The policy did not forbid the new value in any of these 16 (the check already flags the airline travel-certificate case; it did not fire). **The 3 confirmed items** are retail task 92: the user changes which item to return, the agent returns the old item, nothing was reverted, one order, no retry.

*Class 2, the stricter check.* Drop when (a) the call re-sends the record's current state (the flights in the call equal the flights in the latest reservation lookup), (b) the correction names a reservation or order that the call does not use, (c) the user later repeats the old value or a revert phrase ("original payment method", "never mind", "scratch that", "as before") before the call. Kept but flagged for the reviewer: repeat of an earlier call, retry after an error, failed call, a value that never appeared in the conversation. **Against your decisions: 16 reviewed, kept 4 and 3 of them were your confirmed items (precision 3 of 4, was 3 of 16), dropped 12 and none was confirmed; of your 5 skipped items it keeps 1.** The one kept false candidate is airline t21 (a retry after an error).

*Class 6, what went wrong (13 items).* The check says "the next write call after an unacknowledged soft fault is Class 6", which assumes the next call depends on the faulted one. (1) **Independent step**: the next write acts on another order and succeeded (retail t104, t30, telecom: rejected; t16 skipped): the agent was right to continue. (2) **The call is wrong by itself, so it is Class 1**: an argument that never appeared in the conversation (`PLACEHOLDER_ID_LEAST_EXPENSIVE_TABLET`, `original_payment_method`, `gift_card_0000001`) or an exchange of an item for itself: 3 of 5 reclassified items (and one confirmed Class 6 item, retail t110, also has an invented payment id). (3) **Same object, but the real problem is a precondition** (airline t12, t17: Class 7): these are not separable from the confirmed Class 6 ones with the features I could compute. **The stricter check** drops calls with an invented argument and calls on a different object that went through. **Against your decisions: 13 reviewed, kept 7 and 4 of them were confirmed (precision 4 of 7, was 5 of 13), dropped 6 and 1 of them was confirmed (retail t110); of your 4 skipped items it keeps 3.** That is a modest gain, and it loses one confirmed item. The deeper cause is the design: a soft fault on one write makes the next write a candidate whether or not it depends on it. A better scenario would fault the write that a later step needs (for example the cancellation before a re-booking), which this check cannot select after the fact.

*Caution.* Both sets of rules were written after reading these 34 decisions, so the agreement above is not an out-of-sample estimate; the next batch (queue 4) is the test. The rules and the report are in `data/strict_check.py` (`python data/strict_check.py report`).

**Update (2026-11-14, second part) — filling the gaps: budget, results, queue 4, and the projection.**
*Budget.* `api_spend.json` showed $3.64 spent against its $4.50 hard limit, so the requested $2.50 cap exceeded what was left ($0.86); on your instruction the hard limit was raised to $6.14 (scope noted in the file) and **Class 2 runs were skipped**. Reason: from the cached runs the stricter Class 2 check keeps 3 of 147 test-split correction runs (about $0.38 per candidate at $0.0078 per run, all from a few tasks); address (75 runs), baggage (11) and passenger-date-of-birth (2 test runs) corrections produced no test candidate (date of birth gave 4 of 4 in the train split, where the test tasks offer almost no such action). **New spend $0.88 of the $2.50: Class 6 $0.293 (39 planned runs and one 1-run probe), Class 3 $0.586; total $4.52 of $6.14.**
*Class 6 (target 15, got 9; stopped because the plan was used up, $0.41 of the cap unspent).* The earlier soft-fault plans had already faulted 86 of the 105 faultable gold writes in the 83 retail and airline test tasks, so the new plan (`induced_plan_soft2.jsonl`, rows with `strict: true`) holds the 19 unused (task, nth) pairs and two reruns of each of the 10 pairs that had produced a strict candidate (a rerun is a new sample: a probe showed 2 of 22 messages identical). Of 39 runs 10 did not reach the fault, 6 ended without a next call, 13 acknowledged or checked, 10 proceeded; the strict check kept **9, all airline, from 5 tasks (12, 17, 21, 22, 29) and 6 (task, nth) pairs, so they are not independent of each other**. More reruns would only add copies of the same few tasks, so I stopped. Candidates passing the strict check carry its soft flags in `auto_flags` (`strict_verified_or_retried_later`, `strict_never_claims_done`).
*Class 3 (cap $0.60, spent $0.586).* All 249 test-split failed runs that had not been localized were localized with the same gpt-5-mini low-effort prompt (about $0.0024 per run; the pre-run estimate of $0.60 for 214 runs was too high, so a second call finished the remaining 34). Only the Class 3 proposals became candidates (`real_class3_more_keys.json`; `mine_real_errors.py build` drops the other proposals of these runs, which stay in the cache): **45 candidates, 43 telecom and 2 retail, none in airline**; the review should expect about the queue-1 pattern for natural Class 3 (27% confirmed as Class 3, 45% moved to Class 1, 9% to Class 7, 18% rejected). Note for later: `mine_real_errors.py build` regenerates `real_candidates_review.jsonl` without the fields added after it (`origin`, `auto_status`, the second opinion, the Class 5 heuristic candidates); I ran it once, saw that, restored the file and appended the 45 rows instead, so **do not rerun `build` on that file**.
*Queue 4.* `review_queues.py add-queue4` appended **54 items** (induced Class 6: 9; natural Class 3: 45), rare classes first (order 2, 6, 3, 4, 5, 1, 7 by real test errors); the lines of queues 1 to 3 are byte-identical to before (checked). Review with `review_queues.py review --queue 4`; `stats` and `merge` know queue 4, and the builder takes queue-4 decisions as test candidates.
*Real test errors per class, from your decisions plus `test_real`, and the projection once queue 4 and the 17 skipped queue-1 items are reviewed* (`python data/coverage_projection.py`; waiting candidates are turned into classes with the transition rates measured on queue 1 for the same origin and proposed class; the last column uses the in-sample precision of the strict checks for induced Class 6, 4 of 7):

| class | test_real | natural (reviewed) | induced | **total now** | natural incl. test_real | projected after queue 4 | with strict-check rate |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 wrong argument | 6 | 25 | 3 | **34** | 31 | 59.4 | 56.4 |
| 2 stale state | 0 | 0 | 3 | **3** | 0 | 3.9 | 6.8 |
| 3 wrong tool | 3 | 5 | 0 | **8** | 8 | 20.4 | 20.4 |
| 4 repeated call | 7 | 8 | 1 | **16** | 15 | 17.8 | 17.5 |
| 5 failure to propagate | 1 | 0 | 19 | **20** | 1 | 20.0 | 20.0 |
| 6 ignored error | 0 | 1 | 6 | **7** | 1 | 12.3 | 14.4 |
| 7 missing precondition | 8 | 33 | 6 | **47** | 41 | 57.1 | 53.9 |

Read it with care: **Class 2 stays the gap (3 real errors, all induced, projected 4 to 7)**; Class 6 reaches about 12 to 14 only if the strict-check candidates confirm at the in-sample rate, and all its induced examples come from airline; Class 3 reaches about 20 if the queue-1 pattern holds, but 43 of its 45 new candidates are telecom; Class 5 is 19 of 20 induced (1 natural). The projection rests on transition rates from 9 to 41 decided items per pair. Natural and induced errors should be reported separately (D-019).

## D-020 — Training moves to Kaggle (notebooks `kaggle_phase5_modernbert.ipynb`, `kaggle_phase6_qwen.ipynb`)

**Date:** 2026-11-12. **Status:** generated and smoke-tested locally; **nothing has run on Kaggle or on two GPUs.**

**Why.** Colab's GPU quota ran out in the middle of the Qwen run (about 4.5 hours of T4 for 1,293 steps at 0.08 it/s, from the Colab screen). Kaggle gives 2 x T4, about 30 GPU hours a week, 12-hour sessions and runs a notebook unattended ("Save & Run All (Commit)"). Data is a private Kaggle dataset `toolcall-verifier-data` (the 7 files of `data/derived/`).

**What was built.** `notebooks/make_kaggle_notebooks.py` reads the two Colab notebooks and writes the Kaggle ones by exact string replacement; each replacement must match once, so if a Colab notebook changes where the script touches it, the script stops. The model, data, input format, split, hyperparameters and metrics are the Colab code, copied. Changes: (1) environment detection (Kaggle, Colab or local; `TCV_ENV` forces one for tests); on Kaggle the data folder is found by searching `/kaggle/input/` for `train_pool.jsonl`, everything is written to `/kaggle/working/`, no Drive. (2) A configuration cell: `EVAL_ONLY`, `EVAL_CHECKPOINT`, `TIME_BUDGET_HOURS = 11`, `USE_BOTH_GPUS`, `MODEL_PATH_OVERRIDE`. (3) No `!pip` and nothing that waits for input: `pip_install` is a non-interactive subprocess whose failure (for example Internet off) is printed, not fatal. (4) Per-epoch and per-log-line elapsed time and an estimated finish time (`RunProgress`); when 11 hours of the session are used, it saves a checkpoint, stops, evaluates, and writes `completed: false` into `train_info.json`. (5) Resume: an earlier run's output attached as an input is searched for `checkpoints/<phase>/trainer/checkpoint-*` (all kept checkpoints are copied so the "best" one the Trainer remembers exists) and for a finished `best/`; a finished model skips training. (6) Eval-only mode: skips tokenizing the training data and training, evaluates `EVAL_CHECKPOINT` (a `best/` or a `trainer/checkpoint-N` folder), falls back to the base model's tokenizer when the checkpoint has none, and runs on CPU when there is no GPU (Qwen is then loaded unquantized in fp32 because bitsandbytes needs CUDA, with a printed note that scores can differ slightly). (7) Everything the evaluation cell prints is also written to `/kaggle/working/eval_report.txt`, line by line. (8) On success the Trainer checkpoints (about 1.8 GB each for ModernBERT) are deleted so the output stays small.

**Two GPUs.** The effective batch stays 16 = per-device batch x GPUs x gradient accumulation, worked out at run time (`micro_batch_plan`, asserted to divide): ModernBERT tries 2 GPUs with `nn.DataParallel` through the Trainer (2 x 2 x 4, then 1 x 2 x 8) and, on any error that is not out-of-memory, prints "FALLING BACK TO ONE GPU" and continues on one (2 x 8, then 1 x 16); Qwen uses **one** GPU and prints why: in the installed transformers (5.22.2) `Trainer` does not wrap a 4-bit quantized model in DataParallel, yet `n_gpu` would still be 2 and double the batch, and bitsandbytes 4-bit weights cannot be replicated across devices. So the Qwen notebook forces one GPU (ladder 8 x 2, 4 x 4, 2 x 8) and the second T4 idles; a real two-GPU Qwen run would need DDP (`accelerate launch`), which is not built. A guarded `reference_compile = False` is set on ModernBERT only where that config attribute exists (4.5x; it is gone in 5.x).

**Checked locally** (tiny random models, real tokenizers, a fake `/kaggle/input` and `/kaggle/working`): fresh run; rerun skips training; a run stopped by a tiny time budget (completed false) and a second run that resumes from that run's attached output (starts at step 1 of 12); a finished run attached as input; eval-only on a checkpoint without tokenizer files (same AUROC as the in-run evaluation); a mocked 2-GPU machine where the first attempt raises out-of-memory and the second a non-memory error (the plan goes 2 x 2 x 4, 2 x 1 x 8, falls back, then 1 x 2 x 8); the batch arithmetic for every combination. Both notebooks' code cells parse and contain no `!pip`.

**Not verified:** DataParallel on real T4s (speed, memory, fp16 with checkpointing); 4-bit QLoRA on Kaggle; the real-model CPU fp32 path for Qwen; `args._n_gpu = 1` on a CUDA machine; whether `pip install` works there (Internet must be on; the base models are downloaded); runtime. A resume from a checkpoint saved in the middle of an epoch under a different micro-batch plan skips a slightly wrong number of samples (harmless statistically, but not exact). The ModernBERT v2 estimate of 1.5 hours (D-017) is unmeasured; Qwen at about 4.5 hours fits the 11-hour budget on one T4.

**The split question (item 8).** The Colab notebook printed train 6,892 / val 688 while D-012 and D-017 say 6,816 / 764 for the same 7,580 rows. Cause: the first fold of `StratifiedGroupKFold(10, shuffle=True, random_state=42)` differs by scikit-learn version, 6,892 / 688 with 1.5.2, 1.6.1 and 1.7.2 (Colab) and 6,816 / 764 with 1.8.0 and 1.9.1 (the documented one). D-012 was right for 1.8+, so it was not changed; both Colab notebooks and the generated Kaggle ones now carry the 58 validation groups frozen in the split cell (`notebooks/freeze_split.py`) and assert exactly 6,816 / 764 (D-012 update). The interrupted Colab Qwen run used the 6,892 / 688 split.

**Housekeeping check (nothing to change).** The repo has no remote and no remote branches, so the 37 MB `induced_runs.jsonl` was never pushed. It was untracked in `3cace76` and now lives in gitignored `data/raw/induced/`; the scripts read it there. It is still in the history of commits `314fd25` and `54a02d6` (history was not rewritten; `.git` is 120 MB). The other tracked files over 5 MB are datasets or regenerable intermediates, not run dumps: `negatives_pool_final` 35 MB, `negatives_pool` 21 MB, `negatives_class2_class5` 16 MB, `train_pool` 15 MB, `positives_class2_controls` 11 MB, `bfcl_pool` 11 MB, `positives_class4_controls` 10 MB, `external_critictool` 7 MB, `negatives_llm` 7 MB. The labeler precision report (`review_queues.py stats`) and the induced artifact check with pairing counts (`eval/shortcut_baselines.py --induced`) were already built in `3cace76`; I ran both (the report with made-up decisions in memory, the check on the real induced runs) and they work as specified.

**Update (2026-11-12) — the first Kaggle run (ModernBERT v2) took 7.75 hours: diagnosis and speed fix.** Sources: the executed notebook and `train_info.json` (`docs/kaggle_v2_run.ipynb`, `docs/kaggle_v2_train_info.json`, `docs/kaggle_v2_eval_report.txt`).

*Diagnosis.* `kaggle_phase5_modernbert.ipynb`, legacy data (6,816 / 764), v2 input, 4,096 tokens, 3 epochs, fp16, SDPA, gradient checkpointing on. **Both T4s were used through `nn.DataParallel` inside the Trainer** (the log says "2 GPU(s)", `train_info.json` has `n_gpu: 2`, and the DataParallel warning "gather along dimension 0, all input tensors were scalars" appears); not DDP. **Effective batch 16 = 2 per device x 2 GPUs x accumulation 4**, 1,278 optimizer steps (426 per epoch). **Time:** elapsed 27,865 s (7.74 h), `train_runtime` 27,560 s; the epochs took 2h30m05s, 2h34m15s and 2h32m21s, each including one 154 s validation pass and a checkpoint save; **about 21 s per optimizer step**, 1.3 s per row. Validation ran 4 times (three epoch ends plus a final `trainer.evaluate()` that repeats epoch 3), 154 s each, 10 minutes or 2.2% of the run: **the time is training, not evaluation or saving.** **Warnings:** no out-of-memory message, nothing about attention or gradient checkpointing; only the DataParallel gather warning, the Hugging Face unauthenticated-request notice, the expected "classifier weights newly initialized" notice and a harmless tokenizer length notice from the measurement cell. One oddity, not investigated: the logged training loss at the end (about 1.7) is about four times the validation loss (0.42), which would fit a loss summed over the 4 accumulation steps; gradient norms were 16 to 500 against a clipping norm of 1.0, so clipping was always active. It does not affect speed.

*Where the time went.* (1) **Padding: with random batches, 50.5% of the tokens the model processed were padding** (batches of 4 as DataParallel loads them; 33.6% for batches of 2 on one GPU; training rows have p50 845 and p90 3,515 tokens, so one long row pads its whole batch); validation batches were in file order (43% padding). (2) Gradient checkpointing, about 30% more compute, although it is only needed if memory requires it. (3) **Two T4s through DataParallel were slower than one:** with a FLOP model of the run (ModernBERT-base, full SDPA attention, padded lengths) the Kaggle run did about 107 PFLOP in 27,098 s of training = **4.0 TFLOPs for both GPUs, 2.0 per GPU**, while the Colab single-T4 projection of the same notebook that you reported ("about 3 hours" for 6,892 rows; I have not seen that log) works out to 77 PFLOP in 10,800 s = **7.1 TFLOPs on one T4**. If both figures are right, two GPUs gave 0.55 times the throughput of one. The log cannot say why (DataParallel copies the model to the second GPU at every micro-step and gathers on GPU 0, there is no NVLink, and the micro-batch per GPU is only 2; or the Kaggle environment is slower); no GPU utilization was recorded.

*Fixes (both notebooks, Colab and Kaggle: `notebooks/add_speed_fixes.py` patches the Colab notebooks, `notebooks/make_kaggle_notebooks.py` regenerates the Kaggle ones, the shared code is in `notebooks/length_bucketing_code.py`).*
1. **Length-bucketed batches.** `group_by_length` is not used (its name and place changed across transformers versions: the local 5.22.2 has `train_sampling_strategy="group_by_length"`, other 5.x versions may not), so `LengthBucketTrainer` overrides the Trainer's sampler hooks with `LengthBucketSampler`: each epoch a fresh random permutation is cut into chunks of 50 batches, each chunk is sorted by length and cut into batches, then all batches are shuffled; the batch with the longest row goes first, so an out-of-memory error shows at step 1 (every epoch has the same worst-case shape). **Padding waste on the training set, random order to length-bucketed: legacy 6,816 rows 50.5% to 2.3% at loader batch 4, 33.6% to 1.6% at batch 2; final preview 5,549 rows 53.2% to 2.5% at batch 4, 35.0% to 1.8% at batch 2.** An epoch now processes 51% (batch 4) or 67% (batch 2) of the padded tokens it did before (legacy: 10.2M instead of 20.2M at batch 4, 9.97M of them real). The notebooks print this at the start of training. Not tested: whether batches of similar-length rows change the result; each optimizer step still accumulates several micro-batches of different lengths, but a micro-batch is now more uniform in length (and so partly in label, since the classes differ in length), so compare validation metrics in the next real run.
2. **GPU setup: one T4 by default (`USE_BOTH_GPUS = False`).** Reasons: the comparison above (two GPUs ran at about 0.55 times the throughput of one), and DDP is not an option I can vouch for: it needs `torchrun` or `notebook_launcher` with the notebook restructured into a script, and there is no GPU here to test it, so an unattended multi-hour Kaggle run would be its first test. The setting also hides the second GPU with `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES` before CUDA starts, so nothing can wrap the model in DataParallel (the earlier `args._n_gpu = 1` guard stays as a second layer). `USE_BOTH_GPUS = True` brings DataParallel back (with the same ladder), so `BENCHMARK_STEPS` can compare the two on the real hardware; if it shows the 2-GPU setting is faster, the default should change. Phase 6 already trained on one GPU (4-bit models cannot use DataParallel).
3. **Gradient checkpointing off unless needed.** A ladder of (per-device micro-batch, checkpointing), fastest first, effective batch always 16: Phase 5 (4, off), (2, off), (2, on), (1, on); Phase 6 (2, off), (1, off), (8, on), (4, on), (2, on). `GRADIENT_CHECKPOINTING = True/False` forces a side. The memory behind the order is arithmetic, not measurement (ModernBERT-base roughly 3 to 4 GB of activations per 4,096-token row without checkpointing, so micro-batch 4 may or may not fit a 15 GB T4; Qwen roughly 5.5 GB per 1,536-token row); an out-of-memory error at step 1 moves to the next entry in about a minute. `train_info.json` records `gradient_checkpointing`.
4. **Validation once per epoch, batched and length-sorted.** `eval_strategy="epoch"` (unchanged), validation batches sorted by length (padding 43% to 2%, 1.65M to 0.95M padded tokens on the legacy validation rows) with a larger batch (8 for ModernBERT, 4 for Qwen), and the duplicate final pass is gone: the best epoch's metrics for `train_info.json` come from the per-epoch evaluations (`best_eval_metrics`, with `evaluate()` as the fallback). The pass should take clearly less than the 154 s it did; not measured.
5. **`BENCHMARK_STEPS = None`** in the first cell of all four notebooks. Set to a number (say 100), the notebook trains that many optimizer steps (no checkpoints, no validation during it), then prints the setup actually used (GPUs, micro-batch, checkpointing, peak memory), step 1 separately (longest batch plus warm-up), the mean and standard error of the following steps, rows per second, one timed validation pass, and the projected full run (steps x mean + epochs x validation + 90 s of saving per epoch, with a note about the time budget); then it stops with `SystemExit` and saves nothing.

*Estimates for the final data (`python notebooks/measure_speed.py report`: a FLOP model divided by an effective rate; estimates, not measurements, good to about a factor of 2).* The model: ModernBERT-base 6N FLOP per token plus full attention (12 x layers x d x n per token), x 4/3 with checkpointing; Qwen2.5-1.5B 4N per token (6N with checkpointing) plus attention. Rates come from the two earlier runs: ModernBERT 7.1 TFLOPs (Colab, as reported) or 2.0 (Kaggle, per GPU); **Qwen 11.1 TFLOPs from the Colab run recorded above (1,293 steps at 0.08 it/s), the only Qwen timing that exists.**
- **ModernBERT-base, final data (about 5,550 training rows, policy on, 4,096), bucketed, 3 epochs, one T4:** 27.8 PFLOP without checkpointing, **about 1.1 h at the Colab-derived rate and 3.9 h at the pessimistic Kaggle per-GPU rate**, plus about 10 minutes of validation and saves; with checkpointing 1.4 to 5.2 h. The old settings (random batches, 2 GPUs, checkpointing) would take about 5.9 h on this data at the observed Kaggle rate. Either way it fits a 12-hour session; the benchmark will say where in 1.1 to 3.9 h it lands.
- **Qwen2.5-1.5B 4-bit, at the calibrated rate:** as the notebook is today (v1 input, at most 1,536 tokens) on the final data: about 1.3 h (micro-batch 2, no checkpointing) to 1.9 h (micro-batch 8, checkpointing), against the 4.5 h of the Colab run, mostly from the removed padding. **A v2-style input at 4,096 tokens: 7.83M Qwen tokens per epoch, 239 PFLOP, about 6.0 h** (checkpointing unavoidable: about 15 GB of activations per row without it). **At 2,048 tokens: 4.88M tokens, 3.3 h with checkpointing, 2.2 h without** (micro-batch 1). **Does it fit 12 hours?** At the calibrated rate yes, but the 4,096 case needs at least 6.0 TFLOPs of effective rate to finish inside the 11-hour budget and the rate is one noisy figure (Colab's progress-bar number), so the margin is under 2x. If the benchmark shows less: fewer epochs (2 epochs of 4,096 is 4 h), 2,048-token inputs (3.3 h), or **Qwen2.5-0.5B (1.9 h at 4,096, same rate assumed)**; the notebook can also stop at the time budget and resume in a second session (`restore_prior_run`). Two caveats: the Qwen tokenizer produces about 5.7% more tokens than ModernBERT's for the same text, and a v2 input for Qwen would need the budget ladder to count with the Qwen tokenizer (with ModernBERT's count, 459 of the 5,549 rows exceed 4,096 Qwen tokens); and Phase 6 still uses the v1 input, no Qwen v2 input has been built or evaluated.

*Verified here (CPU, tiny models, transformers 5.22.2):* all four notebooks run end to end in final and legacy mode (Colab with `TCV_SMOKE`, Kaggle with `TCV_ENV=kaggle`); a probe of the real Trainer's dataloaders shows homogeneous batches, the longest batch first, a different order each epoch, every row seen once, and ascending lengths in validation; the benchmark mode prints its report and stops without saving; a simulated out-of-memory error walks down the ladder ((4, off) to (2, off) to (2, on)) and the third attempt trains; the two-GPU path with a simulated non-memory failure still falls back to one GPU. **Not verified:** anything on a GPU: whether micro-batch 4 without checkpointing fits at 4,096 tokens, the real seconds per step, whether one T4 really beats two (the benchmark answers both), whether the length-bucketed batches leave the validation metrics unchanged, and how Kaggle's "Save & Run All" shows the benchmark's `SystemExit` (it ends that run as an error, so run the benchmark as an interactive session).

## D-021 — Class 7 state-flip negatives and the policy input

**Date:** 2026-11-12. **Status:** data written (`data/derived/class7_stateflip.jsonl`, training split only, **not merged**); `INCLUDE_POLICY` exists in the Phase 5 notebook and is **off**; nothing trained.

**Why.** The real Class 7 errors in `test_real` are policy violations: the lookup happened, its result shows the action is not allowed, and the agent acts anyway. The rule checker (D-014) found 0 of 8 because the lookups were there, and the old synthetic Class 7 data deleted lookups, which made negatives shorter than positives (D-009). Here the conversation and the call stay as they are and one lookup result is edited so the call breaks a policy rule.

**Method (`data/make_class7_stateflip.py`).** Source: correct tau2-bench rows of `train_pool.jsonl` whose task is on the TRAIN side of `real_split.json` (818 of 1,185 rows) and whose candidate is a write tool a rule covers. Twelve rules, each copied verbatim from `retail/policy.md`, `airline/policy.md` or `telecom/main_policy.md` (asserted at import) and recorded on the row with document, section and text; none was taken from `test_real` or a review candidate. Order status: cancel needs `pending`, modify needs `pending`, return and exchange need `delivered`; airline: basic economy flights cannot be modified (only when the flights change; a cabin-only change is allowed), payment methods must be in the profile (book, and modify when flights change), passenger count cannot change, bags cannot be removed, the cancellation conditions; telecom: no resume when the contract end date is past, resume only after overdue bills are paid. The lookup result is parsed as JSON, one value is changed and it is written back with `json.dumps`, only if that reproduces the original text exactly. Every other lookup of the same thing that carries the old value is changed the same way (so the conversation does not contradict itself), and a row is skipped when a user or agent message states the old value for that order (it would contradict the edit) or the original call is not clearly compliant. The unedited row stays in `train_pool.jsonl` and is the paired correct example (`paired_correct_id`). Rows carry `source: synthetic_stateflip`, `origin: synthetic`.

**Result: 194 negatives** (9 of the 12 rules fired; by write tool, flipped of eligible: retail exchange 96/182, modify items 28/105, return 24/96, cancel 11/51, modify address 5/13; airline book_reservation 15/24, update_reservation_flights 8/26, cancel_reservation 0/5; telecom resume_line 7/11). By rule: exchange 96, modify 33, return 24, cancel 11, airline payment in profile (book 15, modify 6), basic economy 2, telecom overdue bill 4, contract ended 3. **164 of 194 (85%) are retail, 23 airline, 7 telecom.** The passenger-count and bag rules fired 0 times (no train-split candidates of those tools) and cancel_reservation 0 of 5 (3 flown, 2 with a stated reason that could itself allow it). Skips: 283 retail rule applications were skipped because a message stated the old status of the order, and 14 airline rows were cabin-only changes. The telecom SIM-lock example is not in `main_policy.md`, so it is not used; the old `PREREQUISITES` clause for it was test-informed (D-009).

**An artifact found and fixed.** In all 2,326 unedited order lookups of `train_pool`, `pending` means no fulfillments and every other status has them. My first version edited the status only and left 99 inconsistent orders (`pending` with fulfillments, `delivered` or `processed` without), which a model could spot instead of reading the policy. The retail flips now change the status and the fulfillment info together (`pending` empties it; a new shipment with a derived 12-digit tracking id and the order's item ids is added otherwise), so retail pairs differ in two JSON values of one lookup, not one. The pair check (`python eval/shortcut_baselines.py --stateflip`) confirms: no field other than `turns` differs, no turn differs outside the recorded lookups, the order-consistency feature has AUROC 0.500 (8.5% of positives and negatives; those are post-action results), a message naming a different status than the lookup has AUROC 0.485.

**Shortcut baselines.** On the 388 pair rows alone (validation 44 rows, so noisy): length AUROC 0.475, source 0.500. Character length of negative minus positive: mean -17 (min -206, max +135); negative longer in 26%, equal in 36%, shorter in 39%; the direction is constant within a rule (cancel and modify about +100, return and exchange about -40 to -60, payment removal about -100), so there is no common direction. Added in memory to the 7,580 current rows (a different grouped fold): length 0.641 and source 0.619 against 0.575 and 0.569 before, because the negatives are all tau2-bench, which raises its incorrect share from 45.2% to 47.6% against BFCL's 33.4%. Merging them without more tau2 positives or BFCL negatives makes the source cue stronger.

**Policy input (`INCLUDE_POLICY`, default off).** `data/policy_snippets.py` maps 18 write tools (retail 6, airline 6, telecom 6) to their policy sections or labeled blocks, verbatim up to whitespace, written to `data/derived/policy_snippets.json`; `notebooks/add_policy_flag.py` embeds them in the Phase 5 notebook (and `make_kaggle_notebooks.py` carries them to the Kaggle one). With the flag on, `format_input_v2` adds a `POLICY:` line after TOOLS and before the history, the same text for every row with that tool (so a correct example and its flipped twin carry the same text), counted in the 4,096 budget and never clipped; with it off the output is identical to the committed version (500 of 500 rows). Measured with the ModernBERT tokenizer: the block is median 116 tokens (p90 210, max 333) on the 3,819 of 7,580 training rows that have one; training inputs p50/p90/p99/max 820/3,476/4,062/4,096 off and 900/3,599/4,071/4,096 on; **nothing is over 4,096 after the tightening ladder, off or on**; 313 training rows need a tighter ladder level because of the block. `test_real`: 69 of 102 rows have a block (median 165), p50 2,187 to 2,317, max 4,093 to 4,053, 4 rows tighter. The 194 flips: median block 165, p50 3,040 to 3,180, max 4,092 to 4,095, 28 rows tighter.

**Limits.** Not trained, so nothing is shown about transfer: the 8 real Class 7 test rows are telecom and airline, and 85% of the flips are retail status rules, which the real errors do not use. Rules are single-field edits of one lookup; real violations can depend on several results. The textual-contradiction skip is a regex, not a reader.

## D-022 — Final dataset builder (`data/build_final_dataset.py`), dry run only

**Date:** 2026-11-12. **Status:** written and dry-run; **no final file written** (`--write` also needs `--review-complete`). No human decisions exist yet, so the review pool contributes nothing.

**Rules (as implemented).** Sources: the current 7 files minus every row whose `(domain, task_id)` is a TEST-split task (D-018), reviewed natural and induced candidates, CriticTool (train only), state flips (train only). TEST rows come only from human decisions (`test_real` plus accepted queue-1 candidates and queue-2 correct examples); a synthetic row in test fails an assertion. Review-pool TRAIN labels are used only where a class gate passes (at least 8 reviewed test candidates, at least 70% confirmed with the same class: the D-019 rule), correct examples only when a human accepted them (`--train-positives gated` instead requires 90% confirmed of at least 8; the 90% is my choice, not yours), and induced errors enter only with their paired correct handlings. Every row has `source` and `origin`, `split` is stored in the row: validation is the first fold of `StratifiedGroupKFold(10, shuffle, 42)` grouped by `(domain, task_id)` over the training rows, which needs scikit-learn 1.8 or later (D-020). Held-out synthetic test for Classes 2 and 6: whole task groups with synthetic Class 2 or 6 negatives are moved out of training, their negatives and paired controls become `test_synth`, the other rows of those groups are dropped so nothing leaks.

**Dry-run counts (decisions file: 0 decisions).** The 7,580 current rows lose **1,309** rows of new test tasks (retail 881, telecom 390, airline 38, as D-018 predicted), leaving 6,271. The held-out synthetic set takes **68 task groups**: 374 rows (Class 2 negatives 145, Class 6 negatives 42, 187 paired controls) and drops 730 other rows of those groups; 29 state flips are dropped for the same reason. Result: **train 5,589 rows, val 621, test 102 (only `test_real`: 25 incorrect), test_synth 374**; incorrect 2,345 of 5,589 in train (42.0%), 260 of 621 in val.

| Class | train | val | test | test_synth |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 wrong value | 182 | 22 | 6 | 0 |
| 2 stale state | 588 | 71 | 0 | 145 |
| 3 wrong tool | 65 | 9 | 3 | 0 |
| 4 repeated call | 633 | 82 | 7 | 0 |
| 5 not propagated | 215 | 4 | 1 | 0 |
| 6 ignored error | 512 | 57 | 0 | 42 |
| 7 precondition | 150 | 15 | 8 | 0 |
| correct | 3,244 | 361 | 77 | 187 |

Class 6 includes CriticTool's 439 negatives (train and val together) and Class 7 is the state flips (150 train, 15 val). By domain (train/val/test/test_synth): airline 107/0/35/44, retail 1,701/265/35/58, telecom 441/35/32/74, BFCL 2,560/223/0/198, CriticTool 780/98/0/0. **Validation again has no airline rows and only 4 Class 5 rows**, so its per-domain and per-class numbers say little; test has the same 25 real errors as before until the review adds more.

**Waiting for the human review** (planning numbers): 92 natural and 57 induced test candidates (queue 1), 466 natural and 235 induced test correct examples in the pool (queue 2 takes 150 of them), and train auto-accepted candidates held back by the gate: natural Class 1 9, Class 3 4, Class 7 26, induced Class 2 8, Class 5 12, Class 6 1 (20 induced candidates need human review and 163 natural ones were auto-rejected). Until those are decided, no review-pool row enters training or test.

**What was exercised.** With 30 made-up decisions (kept outside the repo, in memory only) the code built 22 test rows (accepted, class-changed, wrong-with-class; rejected, skipped and class-less ones left out) from natural and induced runs in the training-row format (roles user, assistant, assistant_tool_call, tool; tool results cut at 2,000 characters). **Not exercised:** a passing class gate (so the train-side rows from the review pool), `--train-positives gated`, and `--write`.

**Caveats.** (1) The new dataset is smaller in training (5,589 against 7,580) because of the test-task removal and the held-out groups; the notebooks still assert 7,580 rows and recompute the split, so they need a change (read `split` from the rows) before they can use it. (2) CriticTool's 878 rows are in training as asked; D-019 found they strengthen the source cue. (3) The state flips are 85% retail (D-021). (4) The test set is human-reviewed only, so its size depends on the review: 25 real errors today.

**Update (2026-11-14) — training gates, decided from the queue-1 results (`TRAIN_AUTO_ACCEPT` in `data/build_final_dataset.py`).** Queue 1 (149 test-split candidates) is reviewed: 79 accepted, 31 reclassified, 22 rejected, 17 skipped. Per (origin, class), the first labeler's share confirmed with the same call and class, and the decision:

| origin, class | reviewed | confirmed | share | train labels from the review pool |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| natural Class 1 wrong argument | 11 | 11 | 100% | **auto-accepted** |
| natural Class 4 repeated call | 11 | 8 | 73% | **auto-accepted** |
| natural Class 7 missing precondition | 41 | 29 | 71% | **auto-accepted** |
| induced Class 5 failure to propagate | 19 | 19 | 100% | **auto-accepted** |
| natural Class 3 wrong tool | 11 | 3 | 27% | dropped unless a human reviews the item |
| natural Class 5 failure to propagate | 9 | 0 | 0% | dropped (5 of 9 moved to other classes, 4 rejected) |
| natural Class 6 ignored error | 1 | 1 | (1 reviewed) | dropped (too few to judge) |
| induced Class 2 stale state | 16 | 3 | 19% | dropped (D-019: the check was too loose) |
| induced Class 6 ignored error | 13 | 5 | 38% | dropped (D-019) |

The data rule of D-022 (at least 8 reviewed and at least 70% confirmed) selects exactly these four pairs, so the thresholds and the list agree; **the builder now uses the list itself** (a fixed set, so a later queue cannot change a gate silently) and prints the data rule next to it, marking any pair where the two would decide differently. Human review overrides the gate in both directions: a train-split candidate that a human reviewed (queue 3) enters with the human's class if accepted or reclassified and is dropped if rejected, whatever its class. Error candidates of queues 1 and 4 are the test candidates. Built into the dry run with the real decisions: **test 212 rows, 135 incorrect** (`test_real` 102 plus 110 reviewed candidates); real test errors per class **Class 1: 34, 2: 3, 3: 8, 4: 16, 5: 20, 6: 7, 7: 47**; train 5,570, validation 679, test_synth 374. Two things to know: (1) **the 12 induced Class 5 train candidates are still held back by the pair rule** (an induced error enters only with a human-accepted paired correct handling, which needs the queue-2 review or `--train-positives gated`), so induced rows are 0 in training today; (2) the added natural train rows (Classes 1 and 7) include 3 whose task group was already held out as synthetic test, so they are dropped (the builder now does this explicitly instead of failing the no-straddle check). The test set is 64% incorrect (135 of 212) until queue 2 adds correct examples.

**Update (2026-11-15) — real test errors per class after queue 2 (`python data/coverage_projection.py`; the builder's dry run gives the same test counts: 148 incorrect + 159 correct = 307 rows).** Sources: `test_real`, queue 1, queue 2 (items a human marked wrong with a class, not held for queue 5), queue 4 (nothing decided yet). Natural = benchmark runs without perturbation (`test_real` is natural); induced = a perturbed run.

| class | test_real | queue 1 | queue 2 | queue 4 | total | natural | induced | queue-2 errors held for queue 5 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

| 2 stale state | 0 | 3 | 1 | 0 | 4 | 0 | 4 | 0 |
| 3 wrong tool | 3 | 5 | 4 | 0 | 12 | 8 | 4 | 1 |
