**Checks and package.** `data/check_final_dataset.py --dir data/derived/final_v3a`: ALL CHECKS PASSED (no leakage, no duplicate content, unique ids; length-only and source-only baselines as in v2: validation 0.584 and 0.569, test 0.675 and 0.578). Packaged by `data/package_for_kaggle.py --variant v3a` into `data/kaggle_upload_v3a/` (4 files, 81.4 MB; train 6,374 rows, 817 real-origin; val 749; test 363; test_synth 374; 7,860 rows). SHA-256 and sizes: `final_train.jsonl` `ed9efc2ada402e1d6f55305d92d87562235f6e4d5a6488207eab564c71b1e6cc` (66,432,971 bytes); `final_val.jsonl` `1ebfb5d92aef67bd6e69a14e5eab8414906242966a2537e385559f1b558fbb56` (6,601,566; equal to the v2 file); `final_test.jsonl` `f1c20d8e6656ce44be3746489abc98ed102abc46e04b2d1ee2de6ee0a68fdee6` (4,468,166; equal); `final_test_synth.jsonl` `fb2b869b2175ebafc190c35c9a1263652f0bb4ca17873ff07af78b6a51ced779` (3,904,332; equal). Runbook section (h).

**v3b: more real errors (API cap $1.40; balance $1.95).** *Localization.* The 531 train-split failed runs not yet localized (438 telecom, 93 retail) were estimated at $1.43 for all (the ModernBERT stand-in tokenizer over-counts by about 9%; $1.28 with the calibrated estimate of D-026); 520 fit the cap by the estimate. `data/mine_real_errors.py localize-train` (new stage; same gpt-5-mini prompt, reasoning low, prompt version `localize_v1`, telecom first, a seeded shuffle within each domain, a stop rule on the real cost) localized **520 runs (438 telecom, 82 retail) for $1.265 of real cost**, so the total spend is $6.21 against the limit that `api_spend.json` now carries ($6.36 = the earlier $4.95 plus your $1.40 cap and a cent); 11 retail runs remain. 380 of the 520 runs gave a proposal (138 "none", 2 unparsed). `append-train` appended 378 candidates and 1,340 pending positives to the review files with `batch: train_more` (nothing existing was rewritten; `build` must still not be rerun); the builder ignores `train_more` rows in the standard path, so the v2 and v3a builds are unchanged. Proposals by (domain, class): telecom Class 7 169, Class 3 59, Class 4 62, Class 1 48, Class 5 7, Class 6 1; retail Class 7 17, Class 3 6, Class 1 5, Class 4 2, Class 5 2.

*The gate (as D-026).* Natural Classes 1, 4, 7 on the first labeler's label alone; the read-only and line-probe rules applied to the errors; the earlier calls of every admitted-class candidate as correct rows through `screen_positive`; rows only from task groups outside validation, the held-out test and the test set; content already in the dataset skipped. **Admitted: 126 errors** (Class 1: 9 = retail 4 + telecom 5; Class 4: 51 = telecom 49 + retail 2; Class 7: 66 = telecom 53 + retail 13) **and 654 correct rows.** Left out: 65 Class 3, 9 Class 5 and 1 Class 6 candidates (not admitted classes); **71 candidates because their task group is in validation, the held-out test or the test set (the largest single loss, 71 of about 300)**; 74 Class 7 candidates because the flagged call is read-only (the guide's rule 1: telecom Class 7 proposals are mostly lookups); 26 Class 1 candidates that are first line lookups (the line-probe rule); 6 errors and 168 correct rows as content already in the dataset; 29 correct rows as identical repeats, 6 for a structural Class 7 signal, 2 for a wrong line lookup.

*Yield against the estimate of D-026.* Estimated about 205 usable errors and 820 correct rows; actual 126 errors and 654 correct rows. The shortfall is the task-group exclusion (kept so that validation stays byte-identical), fewer Class 4 proposals than predicted (62 against 92) and more read-only Class 7 proposals removed by the rule.

**Results: real training errors per class and domain, before (v2) and after (v3b)** (`eval/real_errors_by_domain.py`, `results/final_v2/real_errors_by_domain_v3.txt`; v3a changes only the correct rows):

| real errors in training | airline | retail | telecom | all | synthetic or external |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Class 1 wrong arg | 4 to 4 | 3 to 7 | 3 to 8 | 10 to **19** | 184 |
| Class 4 repeated call | 3 to 3 | 4 to 6 | **15 to 64** | 22 to **73** | 632 |
| Class 5 failure to propagate | 2 to 2 | 6 to 6 | 0 to 0 | 8 to 8 | 206 |
| Class 7 policy violation | 26 to 26 | 15 to 28 | 13 to 66 | 54 to **120** | 163 |
| Classes 2, 3, 6 | 0 | 0 | 0 | 0 | 609 / 66 / 490 |
| **all real errors** | 35 to 35 | 28 to 47 | **31 to 138** | 94 to **220** | |
| real correct rows | 232 to 249 | 166 to 420 | 182 to **708** | 580 to **1,377** | |

Class 4 in telecom rises from 15 to 64 real errors (and from 22 to 73 overall, against 632 synthetic ones); telecom errors from 31 to 138. Classes 2, 3 and 6 still have no real training error. Expected label quality of the new errors: the first labeler's measured precision is 73% for Class 4 and 68% for Class 7 (measured on the test split of all domains, not on telecom alone), so roughly a quarter to a third of the new Class 4 and 7 errors may have the wrong class or not be errors; they are weighted three times per epoch with `REAL_WEIGHT = 3`.

**Tool-only baseline across the versions** (`results/final_v2/tool_balance_v3b.txt`): train in-sample 0.690 (v2), 0.673 (v3a), 0.707 (v3b); validation 0.672, 0.669, 0.667; test 0.678, 0.707, 0.716; natural test rows 0.671, 0.689, 0.705. The new real Class 4 and 7 errors re-introduce a tool-name signal (repeated `get_details_by_id` and `get_customer_by_phone` lookups are errors, the first lookups of the same tools are correct), so v3b's in-sample shortcut is stronger than v3a's: the model has to be compared with this baseline.

**Checks and package.** `data/check_final_dataset.py --dir data/derived/final_v3b`: ALL CHECKS PASSED (no leakage, no duplicate content, unique ids, 8,640 rows). `val.jsonl`, `test.jsonl`, `test_synth.jsonl` are byte-identical to v2 (SHA-256 as in v3a). Packaged into `data/kaggle_upload_v3b/` (85.9 MB): `final_train.jsonl` `d55797e89b262fd5c1272b6a178f8f63d5626b106e365db8238884bb1e574dab` (7,154 rows, 70,893,819 bytes; 1,597 real-origin rows); `final_val.jsonl` (749 rows), `final_test.jsonl` (363), `final_test_synth.jsonl` (374) with the hashes of v3a. Runbook section (i): the same flags as (h) (`DATASET = "final"`, `REAL_WEIGHT = 3`, 3 epochs, one GPU), about 2.3 hours because an epoch has 10,348 rows.

**Summary of the two versions.**

| | final_v2 | v3a | v3b |
|---|---|---|---|
| training rows | 6,231 | 6,374 | 7,154 |
| errors / correct | 2,444 / 3,787 | 2,444 / 3,930 | 2,570 / 4,584 |
| real errors | 94 | 94 | 220 |
| real correct rows | 580 | 723 | 1,377 |
| real-origin rows | 674 | 817 | 1,597 |
| rows per epoch with `REAL_WEIGHT = 3` | 7,579 | 8,008 | 10,348 |
| validation / test / test_synth | 749 / 363 / 374, byte-identical in all three | | |

## D-029 — Analysis of the v3b run (220 real training errors, `REAL_WEIGHT = 3`), the recommendation, and the Qwen preparation (2026-11-21)

No API call and no Kaggle run. The run is the Kaggle notebook `modernbert_v3b`, version "v3b · AUROC 0.771", trained on the v3b dataset of D-028.

**Step 0: files.** From `~/Downloads/v3b · AUROC 0.771/` and its `best/` subfolder: `test_predictions.txt`, `test_synth_predictions.txt`, `val_predictions.txt` to `results/final_v3b/*.jsonl` (363, 374 and 749 rows); `model (1).safetensors`, `config.txt`, `tokenizer.txt`, `tokenizer_config.txt`, `train_info.txt` to `models/final_v3b/` as `model.safetensors`, `config.json`, `tokenizer.json`, `tokenizer_config.json`, `train_info.json` (also `docs/final_v3b_train_info.json`). **The evaluation report was not in that folder**: it is `~/Downloads/eval_reportv3b.txt` (Nov 21 20:53), copied to `docs/final_v3b_eval_report.txt`. **Missing or not copied:** the executed notebook; a special-tokens file (not needed: the tokenizer files are enough); `training_args.zip` and its unzipped folder (a pickled `TrainingArguments`, not opened or loaded: unpickling a downloaded file can run code; `train_info.json` has the settings). `models/` is gitignored. Files in `models/final_v3b/` (SHA-256, bytes):

| file | bytes | SHA-256 |
|---|---|---|
| `model.safetensors` | 598,439,784 | `617accea7753c55ff139c71abc14b283b7bd96ff3a9ba9042159f2e10d004c24` |
| `config.json` | 2,108 | `538d50966465c05bc290ec4cd4d0b2c5d6d18fc9a6055afb63c7b491517b558e` |
| `tokenizer.json` | 3,583,327 | `0fc88ec706f80b2eb15174d04fa7377948ca3f07065985deec1e28d3fb1cda9d` |
| `tokenizer_config.json` | 380 | `fb54f027372062b2ca52282efb04d178a8b57167a00cd8f4e816515823a2c016` |
| `train_info.json` | 8,344 | `aca17fca87dc84a2582ad48475db522b4a95676358b08edcf4f1f0456358931c` |

The config and tokenizer files equal those of final_v2 (same hashes); the weights differ. **Local check** (`eval/verify_model_locally.py --model models/final_v3b`, CPU): the model loads as `ModernBertForSequenceClassification` (150M parameters) and on 10 test rows (the 5 shortest, 27 to 173 tokens, and 5 of 1,786 to 2,164 tokens) reproduces the saved GPU predictions with a maximum difference of 0.0047 (mean 0.0009). **The run:** `train_info.json` confirms the v3b data (`n_train` 10,348 = 7,154 unique rows with 1,597 real-origin rows at weight 3, `n_val` 749), one GPU, micro-batch 2, 3 epochs, no gradient checkpointing, **6,780 seconds (1.88 hours)**, validation AUROC 0.927 (final_v2 0.909), which is 4.58 training samples per second against the 3.85 assumed in the runbook (the estimate of 2.3 hours was too high). Headline figures of the evaluation report: test AUROC 0.771 (0.720-0.820), without Class 7 0.762; at 0.5 recall 0.494, false-flag 0.197; recall at 5% and 10% false alarms 0.229 (threshold 0.895) and 0.329 (threshold 0.731); Class 7 recall 0.49 (31/63); test_synth AUROC 0.756; real-origin validation AUROC 0.962 (92 rows, 20 errors).

**Part A, figures verified** (`eval/analyze_final_v3b.py`, output `results/final_v3b/analysis.txt`; 2,000 bootstrap resamples): natural rows AUROC 0.753 (0.699-0.807), 269 rows / 98 errors, false-flag 0.19 at 0.5 (recall 0.40); induced rows 0.724 (0.606-0.832), 94 / 72, false-flag 0.27 (final_v2: 0.73). All as stated. Overall 0.771 (0.720-0.818; the notebook's 1,000-resample interval is 0.720-0.820). Induced correct rows flagged at 0.5: 6 of 22 against 16 of 22 for final_v2, and **every one of the 10 that final_v2 flagged and v3b does not** is a change in one direction (none the other way): the induced shift is largely gone.

**1. Paired comparison** (v3b minus the other; interval of the AUROC difference; share of resamples in which v3b is better):

| | overall (363 rows) | natural rows (269) | induced rows (94) |
|---|---|---|---|
| v3b AUROC | 0.771 (0.720-0.818) | 0.753 (0.699-0.807) | 0.724 (0.606-0.832) |
| vs final_v2 | +0.039 (-0.005 to +0.084), 96% | +0.038 (-0.011 to +0.087), 93% | +0.074 (-0.035 to +0.203), 90% |
| vs gpt-5-mini | **+0.052 (-0.020 to +0.119), 92%, p about 0.15** | **+0.059 (-0.028 to +0.149), 91%, p about 0.19** | +0.026 (-0.112 to +0.170) |
| vs gpt-5-nano | +0.151 (+0.065 to +0.232), significant | +0.131 (+0.034 to +0.234), significant | +0.138 (-0.045 to +0.304) |
| vs length only | +0.096 (+0.038 to +0.151), significant | +0.128 (+0.057 to +0.201), significant | +0.165 (+0.024 to +0.305), significant |
| vs tool name only | +0.055 (+0.001 to +0.108), p about 0.04 | +0.049 (-0.016 to +0.111), p about 0.13 | +0.146 (+0.005 to +0.297), significant |

**v3b is not significantly better than gpt-5-mini, overall (p about 0.15) or on natural rows (p about 0.19), and not significantly better than final_v2 (p about 0.08 overall, 0.14 natural).** It is significantly better than gpt-5-nano, than the length shortcut and, overall and on induced rows, than the tool-name shortcut (the tool name alone gives AUROC 0.716, 0.705 on natural rows: the model is only 0.05 above it). Point estimates and the share of resamples (90 to 96%) favour v3b everywhere; the intervals include 0 because 98 natural errors cannot resolve a difference of 0.05.

**2. Operating points** (threshold from the correct rows of the subset; recall at the best threshold with at most that false-alarm rate):

| | 5% false alarms | 10% | 20% | at 0.5 (recall / false-flag) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| overall: v3b | 0.229 | 0.329 | 0.512 | 0.494 / 0.197 |
| overall: gpt-5-mini | 0.100 | 0.471 | 0.594 | 0.547 / 0.145 |
| overall: final_v2 | 0.029 | 0.212 | 0.412 | 0.688 / 0.347 |
| natural: v3b | 0.133 | 0.286 | 0.480 | 0.398 / 0.187 |
| natural: gpt-5-mini | 0.245 | 0.510 | 0.602 | 0.531 / 0.135 |
| natural: final_v2 | 0.051 | 0.153 | 0.316 | 0.551 / 0.298 |

v3b has overtaken gpt-5-mini only at 5% false alarms overall (0.229 against 0.100, driven by the correct rows that gpt-5-mini scores at 0.91 or more); at 10% and 20% false alarms, overall and on natural rows, **gpt-5-mini is still better** (natural 10%: 0.51 against 0.29). **A caveat on the length baseline: on all rows it reaches recall 0.212 at 5% and 0.329 at 10% false alarms, the same as v3b (0.229 and 0.329)**, because the test errors have longer histories than the correct examples (D-025); v3b's operating-point recall overall is not evidence of more than length until it is compared on natural rows, where the length baseline is lower. Per class (errors of the class against all correct rows; AUROC, recall at 10% false alarms; v3b / gpt-5-mini / final_v2): Class 1 0.724 / 0.823 / 0.659 and 0.23 / 0.63 / 0.13; Class 2 0.861 / 0.979 / 0.759 (4 rows); Class 3 0.754 / 0.732 / 0.718 and 0.33 / 0.67 / 0.22; **Class 4 0.802 / 0.789 / 0.617 and 0.47 / 0.53 / 0.00**; Class 5 0.756 / 0.769 / 0.756 and 0.40 / 0.45 / 0.20; Class 6 0.780 / 0.773 / 0.820 and 0.38 / 0.44 / 0.38; **Class 7 0.785 / 0.598 / 0.773 and 0.32 / 0.30 / 0.29**. The real-data fix paid off most where it added data: Class 4 recall at 0.5 rose from 0.26 to 0.63 (natural 0.12 to 0.62) and its AUROC from 0.617 to 0.802; telecom recall at 0.5 from 0.31 to 0.44, telecom AUROC 0.727 to 0.771 (airline 0.783, retail 0.763). Classes 1, 2 and 6 remain where gpt-5-mini is clearly ahead.

**3. The Class 7 drop** (natural recall at 0.5 from 0.78 to 0.40; all rows 0.79 to 0.49). *It is mostly a threshold effect*: v2 flagged 30% of the natural correct rows at 0.5, v3b 19%. Class 7 against the correct rows, natural rows: **AUROC 0.780 (v2) and 0.777 (v3b); recall at 10% false alarms 0.27 and 0.29, at 20% 0.47 and 0.53**; on all rows AUROC 0.773 and 0.785. *The tool-name shortcut explains little of the recall*: only 5 of the 63 Class 7 test errors are `cancel_reservation`; v2's recall on the other 58 is the same (0.78); the 14 correct `cancel_reservation` rows were all at 0.9 or more in v2 (a shortcut that costs AUROC), and in v3b their median score is 0.17 (36% at 0.5 or more) while the 6 cancel errors score a median 0.97: **the shortcut is broken** (AUROC within the tool 0.571 for v2, 0.714 for v3b, 0.482 for gpt-5-mini). *But without the cancel rows v2 ranks Class 7 better*: natural AUROC 0.823 (v2) against 0.777 (v3b), recall at 10% / 20% false alarms 0.38 / 0.68 against 0.25 / 0.47; all rows 0.815 against 0.789. By domain, Class 7 AUROC: airline 0.773 to 0.812, retail 0.767 to 0.826, **telecom 0.747 to 0.708** (recall at 0.5 0.50 to 0.25; 16 errors). The real Class 7 training errors in telecom went from 13 to 66, and the 53 new ones are all `unlabeled` (the heuristic found no sub-reason), labeled by the first labeler at the measured 68% precision: **the evidence is consistent with the noisier new telecom Class 7 labels hurting telecom Class 7 (and Class 7 excluding the cancel rows), but with 16 telecom errors it is not conclusive**. Airline and retail Class 7, where the added labels were few or the old ones clean, improved.

**4. Correct rows scored at 0.9 or more by v3b: 9 of 193** (final_v2: 14; at 0.7 or more: 20 against 23). All are `test_real` correct rows (hand-checked in phase 4), none has JSON-style agent text, their history is short (median 10 turns against 18), and gpt-5-mini scores them with a median 0.18. By tool: `cancel_reservation` 5 of 14 (was 14 of 14), `get_details_by_id` 2 of 14, `update_reservation_flights` 1 of 2, `get_data_usage` 1 of 2; by domain airline 6, telecom 3. The common thread is still the airline write calls with a policy condition (cancellations and flight changes), now spread over several tools, and two repeated-looking telecom lookups.

**5. Recommendation: v3b is the final ModernBERT.** Evidence: it has the best AUROC overall, on natural and on induced rows, at 5% false alarms, on Classes 3, 4 and 7, and on validation (0.927); the induced false-flag fell from 73% to 27%; the `cancel_reservation` shortcut is broken; Class 4 and telecom, the two weak spots of D-027, improved; the real-origin validation AUROC stays 0.96. It is not proven better than gpt-5-mini or final_v2 (all intervals include 0), and it still loses to gpt-5-mini at 10% and 20% false alarms and on Classes 1, 2 and 6. *A v3a run is not worth it for the project* (it would only attribute the gain between tool balancing and the real errors; v3b contains both, and its run time is 1.9 hours); it is a cheap ablation if the report needs the attribution. *A `REAL_WEIGHT = 2` run is not worth it:* a difference between weights of about 0.02 is far inside the paired interval of 0.05 to 0.09, so one more run could not tell, and **every further comparison on this test set adds selection bias** (the test set already chose between final_v1, final_v2 and v3b). What would change the ranking: more real test rows, or the Qwen comparison (Part B), not more weights. If one more ModernBERT run is made, the best use is cleaning the 53 unlabeled telecom Class 7 labels, not a hyperparameter.

**Part B, Qwen: step 6, the notebook.** `notebooks/kaggle_phase6_qwen.ipynb` is regenerated by `make_kaggle_notebooks.py` with `notebooks/qwen_v2_code.py`: (a) **`format_input_v2` with the policy block** replaces the v1 `format_input` for tokenization and evaluation (the formatting cell is copied from the ModernBERT notebook, so both format an input identically; the ladder counts tokens with Qwen's tokenizer and fits the new configuration value `QWEN_MAX_LEN`); (b) `DATASET = "final"` (it already was the default); (c) **`REAL_WEIGHT`** (default 1; real-origin rows appear k times per epoch; `train_info.json` records `real_weight`, `n_train_unique`, `n_real_origin_train`, `qwen_max_len`); (d) **`QWEN_EPOCHS`** (new, default 2, see step 7); (e) the evaluation saves `val_predictions.jsonl` (source, origin, real_origin), prints the validation AUROC per source group and the real-origin validation AUROC, and no longer overwrites `test_predictions.jsonl` with the legacy format in final mode (the same bug as in the ModernBERT notebook, D-025). The flags `NORMALIZE_AGENT_TEXT` and `AGENT_TEXT_CHARS` are in the configuration cell (shelved, off) because the copied cell uses them. **Already in the notebook and unchanged:** the length-bucketed sampler, one GPU by default (a bitsandbytes 4-bit model cannot use `nn.DataParallel`), `BENCHMARK_STEPS`, the micro-batch and checkpointing ladder, the 11-hour time budget with resume, and the final report (per class by origin, Class 7 sub-reasons, per domain, test_synth, baselines).

**Step 7: token budget and time** (`eval/qwen_lengths.py`, `eval/qwen_time_estimate.py`; outputs `results/final_v3b/qwen_time_estimate.txt` and `qwen_train_tokens_*.json`; Qwen2.5 tokenizer, v3b data, policy block on).

| budget | train rows tightened (natural length over the budget) | test rows tightened | tool-result characters visible, train / test | rows still over after the ladder | tokens per epoch with `REAL_WEIGHT = 3` (10,348 rows) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **4,096** | 638 (8.9%) | 34 (9.4%) | 76% / 67% | 0 | 13.02 M (unweighted 9.46 M) |
| 2,048 | 2,073 (29.0%) | 176 (48.5%) | 42% / 44% | **5 train, 2 validation** | 8.88 M |

**The budget is 4,096.** At 2,048 the ladder removes more than half of the tool results (44% of the characters visible on test against 67%), and 7 rows still exceed 2,048 after the ladder, which the notebook's `encode` rejects with an assertion; the 4,096 input equals what ModernBERT sees (68.9% on test) and every row fits. Qwen's tokenizer gives about the same number of tokens per epoch as ModernBERT's (13.0 M against 12.9 M with the weighting).

**Time.** FLOP model (4N FLOP per token for the frozen 4-bit backbone plus attention, 6N with checkpointing, which 4,096 tokens need: about 15 GB of activations per row otherwise) divided by an effective rate. Rate: 11.1 TFLOPs, from the only Qwen timing that exists (the Colab run, 1,293 steps at 0.08 it/s, a progress-bar figure). **Cross-check of the method on the measured v3b ModernBERT run: the FLOP model gives 45 PFLOP for 6,576 s of training, 6.9 TFLOPs, against the 7.1 assumed from Colab: the estimate would have been within 4% of the measured 1.83 hours**, which supports the method for ModernBERT; the Qwen rate rests on one noisy figure.

| Qwen2.5-1.5B, 4,096 tokens, checkpointing | tokens per epoch | PFLOP | time at 11.1 TFLOPs | margin to the 11-hour budget | rate needed for 11 h |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **`REAL_WEIGHT = 3`, 3 epochs (the asked configuration)** | 13.02 M | 382 | **9.5 h** | **1.16x** | 9.6 TFLOPs |
| `REAL_WEIGHT = 3`, **2 epochs** | 13.02 M | 255 | **6.3 h** | **1.73x** | 6.4 TFLOPs |
| `REAL_WEIGHT = 2`, 3 epochs | 11.24 M | 332 | 8.3 h | 1.33x | 8.4 TFLOPs |
| `REAL_WEIGHT = 2`, 2 epochs | 11.24 M | 221 | 5.5 h | 2.00x | 5.6 TFLOPs |
| `REAL_WEIGHT = 1`, 3 epochs | 9.46 M | 281 | 7.0 h | 1.57x | 7.1 TFLOPs |
| Qwen2.5-0.5B, `REAL_WEIGHT = 3`, 3 epochs | 13.02 M | 121 | 3.0 h | 3.7x | 3.0 TFLOPs |
| (2,048 tokens, `REAL_WEIGHT = 3`, 3 epochs, with checkpointing) | 8.88 M | 238 | 5.9 h | | (not usable, see above) |

Add about 15 minutes in every case: the final evaluation of test, test_synth and validation is about 7 minutes and each validation pass 2.3 minutes. **The asked configuration does not fit with a safe margin** (9.5 hours plus the evaluation against the 11-hour budget; the rate would have to be only 16% lower than the one figure we have for it to be stopped by the time budget). **The smallest change that fits is two epochs (`QWEN_EPOCHS = 2`, default; 6.3 hours, margin 1.7x)**, everything else as asked (4,096 tokens, `REAL_WEIGHT = 3`, one GPU); if the 100-step benchmark on the real GPU projects the 2-epoch run under 5 hours, three epochs fit (under 7.5 hours) and `QWEN_EPOCHS = 3` is allowed. Also possible if the benchmark is worse than expected: `REAL_WEIGHT = 2`, or `MODEL_PATH_OVERRIDE = "Qwen/Qwen2.5-0.5B"` (3.0 hours; it changes the model, so the comparison is no longer with the 1.5B). Whatever happens, the notebook stops at the 11-hour budget, saves the best adapter and can be continued from its output (`restore_prior_run`).

**Not verified:** everything on a GPU (the real rate, the micro-batch that fits at 4,096 tokens, whether 4-bit with `QWEN_MAX_LEN = 4096` fits a T4 at micro-batch 1 with checkpointing: the ladder tries 2 and 1 without checkpointing first and then 8, 4 and 2 with it, so the first attempts fail fast on the longest batch).

**Step 8: smoke test and runbook (j).** Smoke test (`notebooks/smoke_test_cells.py`, `TCV_SMOKE=1`, a tiny random model in place of Qwen, the real v3b files from `data/kaggle_upload_v3b`, CPU only): the v2 formatting cell, `REAL_WEIGHT = 3` (192 unique rows became 288 per epoch, the `REAL_WEIGHT = 3: ... -> ... rows per epoch` line printed), the LoRA training cell, the evaluation cell (test and test_synth reports, `val_predictions.jsonl` with `source`, `origin`, `real_origin`, validation AUROC by source group and the real-origin validation AUROC) and `train_info.json` (`real_weight`, `n_train_unique`, `n_real_origin_train`, `qwen_max_len`, `input_format`) all ran; with `BENCHMARK_STEPS = 3` the benchmark block printed (full run = 12 steps) and the notebook stopped with the expected `SystemExit`. **This tests the flag paths and the data flow only.** Not tested (needs a GPU): 4-bit loading, bitsandbytes, the real speed, memory at 4,096 tokens, and the notebook's Drive/Kaggle resume path with a real Qwen adapter. One notebook comment was reworded so that it agrees with the runbook rule (3 epochs only if the benchmark run with 2 epochs projects under about 5 hours). `docs/kaggle_runbook.md` section **(j)**: reuse the dataset `toolcall-verifier-data-v3b` (no new upload, attach only it), the exact configuration cell, the benchmark first (Run All, `BENCHMARK_STEPS = 100`, what to send back, a decision table by projected hours), then Save & Run All with 2 epochs, the checks at the start (8,640 rows, split 7,154 / 749, 10,348 rows per epoch) and what to send back.

**Part B, Qwen: the first Kaggle benchmark failed before it measured anything (out-of-memory fix, 2026-11-21).** Reported: T4, `DATASET = "final"`, v3b data, 4,096-token budget, `QWEN_EPOCHS = 2`, `BENCHMARK_STEPS = 100`. The retry ladder went micro-batch 2 without checkpointing (out of memory), 1 without (out of memory), 8 with checkpointing (out of memory), and then the cell stopped at 4 with checkpointing with no error and no output. No Kaggle message was reported (the report said "paste any message here"; whether Kaggle showed "tried to allocate more memory than is available" is not known). No API calls.

*The ladder was wrong for this input.* It was inherited from the 1,536-token v1 input (D-020): "try without checkpointing first, then 8, 4, 2 with it". At 4,096 tokens the first three entries cannot work; and D-029 itself had said that checkpointing is unavoidable ("about 15 GB of activations per row without it"), while the notebook still tried without. That is my error: I changed the input length and the epochs and did not change the ladder.

*Memory estimate* (`eval/qwen_memory_estimate.py`, `results/final_v3b/qwen_memory_estimate.txt`; arithmetic from the model's shapes, not a measurement). Fixed part 2.2 GiB (NF4 weights 0.63, fp32 embedding 0.87 because `prepare_model_for_kbit_training` casts it, LoRA and head with optimizer 0.03, CUDA context 0.6). Activations 126 KiB per token and layer without checkpointing (13.7 GiB for a 4,096-token row; 5.1 GiB = the 5.5 GB of D-020 for a 1,536-token row); with checkpointing the fp32 residual stream is kept (168 KiB per token over the 28 layers) and one layer is recomputed (196 KiB per token with its gradients). Longest batch first (it is the first batch of every epoch), so the peak is at step 1.

| micro-batch, checkpointing on | tokens | expected (SDPA attention) | pessimistic (math attention) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1** | 4,096 | **3.8 GiB** | 6.4 GiB |
| **2 (new default)** | 8,192 | **5.5 GiB** | 10.7 GiB |
| 4 | 16,384 | 8.7 GiB | 19.1 GiB |
| 8 | 32,768 | 15.3 GiB | 36 GiB |
| 1 and 2, checkpointing off | | 18.0 and 33.8 GiB | |

Against 14.6 GiB (what the driver shows on a 16 GB T4). **The estimate reproduces the three out-of-memory errors** (1 and 2 without checkpointing are far over the limit, 8 with it is just over) **and predicts that micro-batch 4 with checkpointing fits** (8.7 GiB; it would only fail if attention fell back to the math kernel, and that would have been reported as an out-of-memory error, not as a silent stop). The activation part would have to be 3.8 times higher than estimated for micro-batch 2 to fail, 7.6 times for micro-batch 1. **So 4,096 tokens stay**; `QWEN_MAX_LEN = 2048` is not needed (and would cut the evidence, D-029 step 7). Evaluation at batch 2 (validation) and 4 (final): 3.0 and 3.8 GiB expected; the old final-evaluation batch of 8 was 5.4 GiB expected, 19 pessimistic, so it is now 4. The estimate is checked only against the outcomes above and the earlier Colab run; peak memory after optimizer step 1 is now printed and saved in `train_info.json`, so the first real run measures it.

*Cause of the silent stop: not known.* What I can say. (1) The old loop built the Trainer outside `try`, and on an out-of-memory error ran `del trainer; gc.collect(); torch.cuda.empty_cache()` INSIDE the `except` block, where the exception's traceback still references the failed step, so `empty_cache()` could not release anything at that point, and nothing collected the Trainer's reference cycles before the next model was loaded; three failed attempts could therefore leave GPU memory behind. (2) I tried to demonstrate (1) on the CPU toy model (`notebooks/smoke_test_oom.py`, a probe that counts live `PeftModel` objects right before each model build, with an out-of-memory error raised inside the training step): **the old pattern (a copy of the notebook with the old cleanup) also left 0 live models at the next build, so (1) is NOT demonstrated** (by the time the next model is loaded the garbage had been collected on the CPU); what it could do to the CUDA caching allocator (reserved blocks, fragmentation) is not testable here. (3) Other candidates: the host running out of RAM after four `from_pretrained` loads (Kaggle then restarts the kernel without a Python error: this fits "stopped, no error, no output" best), or a CUDA error state after the failures. None can be checked without the log. What the fix does about it: it starts at a setting expected to fit (no retries expected), cleans up in the right place, logs GPU and host memory at every attempt, switches `faulthandler` on, and stops with exact instructions if a cleanup leaves more than 0.5 GiB allocated.

*The fix* (`notebooks/qwen_oom_code.py`; patches `phase6_qwen.ipynb` once in place, `make_kaggle_notebooks.py` calls it first and the Kaggle notebook is generated from the patched Colab one, so both versions have the same code; rerunning the generator is idempotent):
1. **Flags** `MICRO_BATCH = 2` and `GRADIENT_CHECKPOINTING = True` in the first cell (accumulation = 16 / micro-batch; the old `None` automatic mode is gone; `None` now counts as True). **The ladder only goes down from `MICRO_BATCH` with the checkpointing setting fixed**: defaults 2 then 1; never a larger batch, never checkpointing off unless `GRADIENT_CHECKPOINTING = False` is set by hand.
2. **`train_attempt()` builds the model, the Trainer and trains inside one function**; on an out-of-memory error (`torch.cuda.OutOfMemoryError`, or a `RuntimeError` saying "out of memory") it records the message, **leaves the `except` block**, releases the Trainer's references (`accelerator.free_memory()`, model, optimizer, datasets) and returns `(None, message)`. Build errors (the model load) are inside the same `try`, which they were not before. The caller then runs `release_gpu()` (`gc.collect()`, `synchronize`, `empty_cache`, `ipc_collect`, again) and logs the GPU memory.
3. **Logging**: `[GPU memory | ...]` (allocated, reserved, peak, driver-free) and the host RAM (psutil) before each attempt, after a failed attempt before and after the cleanup, after optimizer steps 1 and 10, and after training. `train_info.json` gets `attempts` (micro-batch, checkpointing, outcome, peak GiB) and `peak_gpu_gib`.
4. **Leak check**: if more than 0.5 GiB more than at the start is still allocated after a cleanup, the notebook stops with the flags to restart with instead of retrying into a crash. A warning is printed if memory is already in use before the first attempt (an earlier cell run in the same session).
5. Also: `attn_implementation="sdpa"` explicit; validation batch 2 and final-evaluation batch 4; the training objects are released before the final evaluation loads its own copy of the model; in `BENCHMARK_STEPS` mode the GPU is released before the `SystemExit`, so the real run can follow in the same session; the Colab and Kaggle text that described the old ladder is updated.

*Time with checkpointing on* (`eval/qwen_time_estimate.py`). **The earlier estimate already included it**: `qw_flops` counts 4N per token without and 6N with checkpointing (frozen backbone: forward 2N + activation gradients 2N, and the recompute adds a forward pass, 2N: **+47% of the FLOPs in this setup, not the +30% of a full fine-tune**), and all the figures in step 7 were for micro-batch 2 with checkpointing. The calibration run (Colab) also used checkpointing. So **the 2-epoch run is still 6.3 hours** (255 PFLOP at 11.1 TFLOPs; without checkpointing it would have been 4.3 hours, which does not fit the memory); micro-batch 1 is 6.2 hours. Not in the FLOP count: dequantizing the 4-bit weights for every forward, recompute and backward pass, about 0.11 h at micro-batch 2 and 0.23 h at micro-batch 1. With the evaluation (7 minutes plus two validation passes of 2.3 minutes) **about 6.5 hours, margin 1.7x to 11 hours**. Sensitivity: the 2-epoch run plus evaluation fits as long as the real rate is at least 6.5 TFLOPs (59% of the one calibration figure); at 70% of it 9.3 hours, at 60% 10.8 hours, at 50% 12.9 hours (stopped by the time budget, adapter saved, continued from its output). Three epochs need 9.8 TFLOPs (88%): no. **No further change is needed for the time; the smallest change if the benchmark says otherwise remains `REAL_WEIGHT = 2` (5.5 h for 2 epochs), then the 0.5B model.**

*Tests* (CPU, toy model, `TCV_SMOKE=1`; **flag paths and cleanup logic only, not GPU memory or bitsandbytes**): defaults give `attempt 1/2: micro-batch 2, checkpointing on`; `MICRO_BATCH = 1` gives one attempt; `MICRO_BATCH = 8` with a simulated out-of-memory error (raised inside `Trainer.training_step`, with the training frames on the traceback) in every attempt above 1 goes 8, 4, 2 and succeeds at 1, with 0 live models before every model build and 0 after; `MICRO_BATCH = 3` stops with an assertion; `GRADIENT_CHECKPOINTING = False` gives (2, off), (1, off); `BENCHMARK_STEPS = 3` prints the benchmark block and stops; the whole notebook (cells 0-6) with `REAL_WEIGHT = 3` runs to the report, `train_info.json` has `attempts`. The Colab notebook was compiled and its `build_trainer` call checked (3 arguments, as defined); it was not run (it needs Drive).

*Runbook (j)* updated: the two new flags in the configuration block, a fresh session for the benchmark, what to send back (the `[GPU memory | ...]` lines), what to expect (one attempt, about 5.5 GiB after step 1), the stop rule (peak over 11 GiB or the first attempt out of memory: stop and send), and what to do if the cell ends without a message (look for the Kaggle message, restart, `MICRO_BATCH = 1`).

**Not verified:** everything on a GPU: that micro-batch 2 with checkpointing fits (estimated 5.5 GiB of 14.6 with a factor 3.8 of room), the real peak and speed, `accelerator.free_memory()` and the cleanup on CUDA, that `attn_implementation="sdpa"` is accepted by the installed transformers with a 4-bit model, and the cause of the silent stop.

**Part C, Qwen: the first full run (Kaggle `qwen_v3b`, Version 3), the evaluation crash and the evaluate-only path (2026-11-22).** Log: `docs/qwen_v3b_run_log.txt` (copied from `~/Downloads/download.txt`). No API calls, no Kaggle runs by me.

*What the log shows (measured).* The out-of-memory fix worked at the first attempt: `attempt 1/2: micro-batch 2, gradient checkpointing on` trained without any retry. **Peak GPU memory after optimizer step 1 (the longest batch): 4.85 GiB allocated, 5.33 GiB reserved, of 14.56** (the estimate said 5.5 GiB expected; it was 12% high, on the safe side), host RAM 4.4 of 31.3 GiB (this process 3.5 GiB). Data and split as expected (8,640 rows, 7,154 / 749; 10,348 rows per epoch with `REAL_WEIGHT = 3`; longest input 4,096 tokens; 1,294 steps; padding waste 1.9% after length bucketing, 34.4% without). **Training: 2 epochs, `train_runtime` 24,620 s = 6.84 hours** (epochs of 3h23m and 3h24m, 168 s per validation pass at batch 2), against the estimate of 6.3 hours: **9% longer, an effective rate of 10.3 TFLOPs against the 11.1 of the calibration (7% lower)**, so the FLOP method was good to within 10% for this model too (the earlier cross-check on ModernBERT: 4%). Validation (749 rows; it is mostly BFCL and CriticTool rows, not the real-origin test distribution): epoch 1 F1 0.910, AUROC 0.971, loss 0.194; epoch 2 F1 0.926, AUROC 0.982, loss 0.217; best epoch 2. For orientation only: the ModernBERT v3b model has a validation AUROC of 0.927 on the same 749 rows (its test AUROC is 0.771); **a validation AUROC is not a test AUROC** and says nothing yet about the Qwen model on the 363 test rows. The adapter was saved to `checkpoints/phase6/best/` and the Trainer checkpoints removed; the first GPU line after training shows the memory released (0.02 GiB allocated).

*The crash.* At the start of the final evaluation, `PeftModel.from_pretrained(load_base_model(), ...)` -> `AutoModelForSequenceClassification.from_pretrained` of the base model -> transformers' `log_state_dict_report` -> `create_loading_report` -> `_style()`, which calls `sys.stdout.isatty()`; `sys.stdout` was our `Tee`, which had only `write`, `flush` and `close`. The report is printed because the base `Qwen/Qwen2.5-1.5B` has no `score` head (`score.weight | MISSING`): it came up in the evaluation and not in the training cell because the Tee only starts at the evaluation. **The ModernBERT notebook has the same Tee but did not crash**: its evaluation loads a complete saved model (no missing keys, no report). It was a latent bug in both Kaggle notebooks. **The Colab notebooks have no Tee** (they never replace `sys.stdout`; checked), so "all four notebooks" is two: `kaggle_phase5_modernbert.ipynb` and `kaggle_phase6_qwen.ipynb`, both generated from the one `Tee` in `make_kaggle_notebooks.py`.

*The fix.* `Tee` is now a complete file-like wrapper: `isatty()` returns False (so that no colour codes reach `eval_report.txt`), `fileno()` (the real stdout's), `encoding` and `errors` (the real stdout's, with defaults), `writable()`, `readable()`, `seekable()`, `writelines()`, `closed`, `flush()` that tolerates a closed file, and `__getattr__` that delegates everything else to the real stdout (with a guard for its own attributes and dunders, so that copying cannot recurse). **Regression test `notebooks/test_tee.py`**: takes the `Tee` class out of each notebook, makes it `sys.stdout`, saves a tiny random Qwen2 base model without the head, loads it as `Qwen2ForSequenceClassification` (this prints the LOAD REPORT with a MISSING key, the call that crashed), calls `_style()` directly and exercises `print(file=sys.stdout)`, `logging.StreamHandler(sys.stdout)`, `fileno`, `encoding`, delegation and the closed state. **Negative control (`--old-from-git`, the Tee of the previous commit): it fails with the same traceback as on Kaggle** (`log_state_dict_report` -> `create_loading_report` -> `_style` -> `AttributeError: 'Tee' object has no attribute 'isatty'`); both fixed notebooks pass. A first version of the test failed on the old Tee at an earlier line and so did not show the real path; the loading step now runs first.

*The evaluate-only path for Qwen: what I found and changed.* `restore_prior_run` was only called in the TRAINING branch: with `EVAL_ONLY = True` the notebook evaluated `EVAL_CHECKPOINT` or this run's own `best/`, so with `EVAL_CHECKPOINT = None` and the notebook output attached it would have stopped with "nothing to evaluate". Now (Qwen notebook): with `EVAL_ONLY = True` and `EVAL_CHECKPOINT = None` it calls `restore_prior_run(CKPT_DIR, copy_trainers=False)` (new parameter: look for a finished model only, copy no Trainer checkpoints), which walks `/kaggle/input` for a folder `best` whose parent is named `phase6` (so `.../<notebook output>/checkpoints/phase6/best` is found wherever Kaggle mounts the output), accepts it if it has `adapter_config.json` and its `train_info.json` says `completed`, and prints `found a finished model from an earlier run: <path>`; with several attached outputs it uses the most recently written and lists all; with none, the assertion now says what to attach and lists what is under `/kaggle/input`. With `EVAL_ONLY = False` (the training branch) the same function was already there: with the output attached, training is skipped. The shared helper change (the parameter) is also in the ModernBERT notebook; its evaluation cell is unchanged. `EVAL_CHECKPOINT` still takes an explicit path. The evaluation loads the base in 4-bit on a GPU (`load_base_model`, the `score` head excluded from quantization and freshly initialized), then `PeftModel.from_pretrained(base, best/)`, which brings the LoRA weights and the trained head (`modules_to_save`; the adapter file contains `base_model.model.score.weight`).

*Local test of the evaluate-only path* (`notebooks/smoke_test_qwen_evalonly.py`, CPU, a tiny random Qwen2 as base: **flag paths, the loading code and the attached-output logic; not the 4-bit GPU path**). A: the SMOKE training run trains an adapter, evaluates it, and the tiny base (without the head) and the tokenizer are exported; `best/` is copied to `<input>/notebooks/someuser/qwen-v3b/checkpoints/phase6/best` as an attached notebook output. B: `EVAL_ONLY = True`, `EVAL_CHECKPOINT = None`, the base from the folder (`MODEL_PATH_OVERRIDE`), TCV_SMOKE off: it finds `best/`, loads the base through `from_pretrained` (LOAD REPORT with the MISSING head printed through the new Tee, no traceback), puts the adapter on it and **writes validation, test and test_synth predictions identical to those of the training run (749, 363 and 374 rows, maximum difference 0.0)**, so the head comes from the adapter. C: the same with an explicit `EVAL_CHECKPOINT`. F (control): the same adapter with a different `score` tensor gives a different result (maximum difference 0.62), and peft refuses an adapter without the head (`KeyError`). D: `EVAL_ONLY = False` with the output attached: `found a finished model`, no `training with` line, no Trainer folder. E: nothing attached: stops with "nothing to evaluate ... attach the notebook that trained it". The previous tests were repeated on the regenerated notebooks (ladder test with the simulated out-of-memory error, Tee test).

*Runbook (k)* added: a NEW notebook `qwen_v3b_eval` (so that the notebook holding the output stays untouched; I cannot verify that Kaggle lets a notebook attach its own version), T4 and Internet on, exactly two inputs (Your Work > Notebooks > `qwen_v3b` > Version 3, and `toolcall-verifier-data-v3b`), the first-cell flags (`DATASET = "final"`, `EVAL_ONLY = True`, `EVAL_CHECKPOINT = None`, `QWEN_MAX_LEN = 4096`, `NORMALIZE_AGENT_TEXT = False`, policy block on), what to see in the first minutes, **about 15 minutes** (scoring 1.8 M tokens at the measured 3.5 k tokens per second is 8.5 minutes, plus start, the 4-bit load and the report), what to send back, and a reminder to download `best/` from Version 3 as a copy.

**Not verified:** the 4-bit GPU load of the base followed by `PeftModel.from_pretrained` in this notebook after the fix (the same code ran in the Colab evaluation of D-017 and in the SMOKE test on the CPU, but the Kaggle run crashed before reaching it); where exactly Kaggle mounts an attached notebook output (the search finds `checkpoints/phase6/best` at any depth, and the data folder of the run was found at `/kaggle/input/datasets/<user>/<dataset>`); that the output of a version that ended in an error still holds `best/` (you report that it does); the evaluation time (an extrapolation from the validation pass of the run).

## D-030 — Analysis of the Qwen2.5-1.5B result, the ensemble, the synthetic-real gap, speed and cost, and the final README results (2026-11-22)

No API call and no Kaggle run. The run is the Kaggle notebook `qwen_v3b_eval` (evaluate only, D-029 part C) on the adapter of `qwen_v3b` Version 3 (2 epochs, `REAL_WEIGHT = 3`, v3b data, 4,096-token v2 input with the policy block).

**Step 0: files.** From `~/Downloads`: `eval_reportqwen.txt` -> `docs/final_qwen_v3b_eval_report.txt`; `test_predictions_qwen.txt`, `test_synth_predictions_qwen.txt`, `val_predictions_qwen.txt` -> `results/final_qwen_v3b/{test,test_synth,val}_predictions.jsonl` (363, 374, 749 rows); `best_Qwen/train_info.txt` -> `docs/final_qwen_v3b_train_info.json` and the adapter files -> `models/qwen_v3b/` (already ignored through `models/`): `adapter_model.safetensors` (from `~/Downloads/adapter_model.safetensors`), `adapter_config.txt` -> `adapter_config.json`, `tokenizer.txt` -> `tokenizer.json`, `tokenizer_config.txt`, `train_info.txt` -> `train_info.json`, `README.txt` -> `README.md`. Nothing was missing. The Kaggle log of the evaluation run was not among the files (see step 4).

| file | bytes | SHA-256 |
|---|---|---|
| `models/qwen_v3b/adapter_model.safetensors` | 8,743,512 | `97fe9ffa85b7237a83f091528e646b3a66fe1606dad7c77f4e14dd201d991f35` |
| `models/qwen_v3b/adapter_config.json` | 1,125 | `401b73d5be6440a0338efcd0094fec0024642436c6b233a51537dfd5b75e74ef` |
| `models/qwen_v3b/tokenizer.json` | 11,421,991 | `21e2b58ce119ac9c0d306b7a35d538fe02f55e7f2af95cb0a2d563e892790684` |
| `models/qwen_v3b/tokenizer_config.json` | 697 | `ddb9f850ca6559a928bb25d511f72e3c6eff81395334a4e0eeec670448333d09` |
| `models/qwen_v3b/train_info.json` | 6,146 | `4589d850e34b4aed610d035ae9ff24fd33c57a756e8bf83340c6982aa0a9a988` |
| `results/final_qwen_v3b/test_predictions.jsonl` | 103,215 | `d95f5ffef070cc33e7d4187e7046f9b97007b865c993a3bcc6be10c32e29f382` |
| `results/final_qwen_v3b/test_synth_predictions.jsonl` | 102,670 | `4c1e8ed4d6da859bfb5ff3f54c881c5bcfe16bdededaf7072214ae66e4e4502e` |
| `results/final_qwen_v3b/val_predictions.jsonl` | 205,626 | `b2025f5d0614b643504e8b450fdabe3d5330303dd6f60bbe19634392c95172ed` |
| `docs/final_qwen_v3b_eval_report.txt` | 5,146 | `2a578a0053fa54ad75fdc1c35a4d36fc3154556c42ddb6b47e2ed97cf231cfd8` |

**Checked:** the adapter file has 113 tensors, 2,182,144 parameters (the 2,182,144 trainable parameters of the log), including `base_model.model.score.weight` (2 x 1536), so the trained head is in it; the config says r 16, alpha 32, dropout 0.05, targets `q_proj` and `v_proj`, `modules_to_save` `classifier`, `score`, base `Qwen/Qwen2.5-1.5B`; the AUROCs recomputed from the three prediction files are 0.7649 (test; report 0.765), 0.9998 (test_synth; 1.000) and 0.9817 (validation; 0.982); the 363 test rows are the same rows in the same order as `results/final_v3b/test_predictions.jsonl`. **Not checked: that the adapter reloads and reproduces the predictions** (that needs the 3 GB base model, which I did not download; D-029 part C tested the reload path with a tiny model). Also: `train_info.json` says `peak_gpu_gib` 9.08 for the whole training run (the 4.85 GiB of D-029 part C was after optimizer step 1; **my assumption that the longest batch comes first and gives the peak was wrong by almost 2x**, it still fits 14.6 GiB with 5.5 GiB to spare), and its `token_budget: 1500` is a leftover of the v1 notebook code (`qwen_max_len: 4096` is the budget that was used).

**Note on the history of this entry.** An earlier pass of D-030 (commits `551fd68`, `b1c132a`, 11:25 to 11:38 today, when only the report was available) did step 3 from the two reports and step 4 (speed and cost), and said that steps 1, 2 and 5 waited for the predictions. When I added step 0 above, my write replaced that text in `DECISIONS.md` by mistake (it is in `git show b1c132a:DECISIONS.md`); the code and results files of that pass are intact (`eval/speed_cost.py`, `eval/synth_real_gap.py`, `results/final_qwen_v3b/speed_cost.json`, `synth_real_gap.txt`). Steps 3 and 4 below are redone with the predictions and the same figures checked against it.

**Step 1: paired comparison** (`eval/analyze_final_qwen_v3b.py paired`, `results/final_qwen_v3b/analysis_1_paired.txt`; the 363 test rows, 2,000 paired bootstrap resamples, seed 0, the same resample for both scores; a pair of AUROC differences is "significant" here only if its 95% interval excludes 0, and **no correction for the many comparisons** is made: 5 comparisons x 3 subsets x per-class and per-domain cuts, so a lone borderline interval below should be read as a hint).

| AUROC (95% CI) | overall (363; 170 errors) | natural (269; 98) | induced (94; 72) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Qwen2.5-1.5B** | 0.765 (0.714-0.814) | **0.803 (0.753-0.853)** | **0.533 (0.370-0.691)** |
| ModernBERT v3b | 0.771 (0.720-0.818) | 0.753 (0.699-0.807) | 0.724 (0.606-0.832) |
| GPT-5-mini | 0.719 (0.666-0.770) | 0.695 (0.620-0.760) | 0.698 (0.567-0.820) |
| GPT-5-nano | 0.619 (0.562-0.677) | 0.623 (0.549-0.691) | 0.586 (0.449-0.728) |
| length only | 0.675 (0.620-0.730) | 0.626 (0.556-0.693) | 0.559 (0.424-0.700) |
| tool name only | 0.716 (0.661-0.767) | 0.705 (0.643-0.765) | 0.579 (0.440-0.718) |

Paired difference, Qwen minus the other (95% interval of the difference):

| Qwen minus | overall | natural | induced |
|---|---|---|---|
| ModernBERT v3b | -0.006 (-0.052 to +0.037) | **+0.049 (+0.005 to +0.095)**, p 0.034 | **-0.191 (-0.367 to -0.013)**, p 0.035 |
| GPT-5-mini | +0.046 (-0.026 to +0.111) | **+0.108 (+0.027 to +0.192)**, p 0.007 | **-0.165 (-0.334 to -0.001)**, p 0.049 |
| GPT-5-nano | **+0.146 (+0.057 to +0.228)** | **+0.180 (+0.086 to +0.277)** | -0.054 (-0.288 to +0.175) |
| length only | **+0.090 (+0.025 to +0.152)** | **+0.177 (+0.105 to +0.252)** | -0.027 (-0.237 to +0.173) |
| tool name only | +0.049 (-0.007 to +0.100) | **+0.098 (+0.034 to +0.163)** | -0.046 (-0.254 to +0.167) |

**What this says, and the correction it forces to the headline.** (1) **Overall, Qwen and ModernBERT v3b are indistinguishable** (-0.006, interval -0.052 to +0.037), and neither is significantly better than GPT-5-mini (+0.046 for Qwen, interval includes 0; D-029 had +0.052 for ModernBERT). (2) **But the two models differ in where they are good, and the overall figure hides it:** on the 269 natural rows Qwen is ahead of ModernBERT by +0.049 (interval just excludes 0; p 0.034) and of GPT-5-mini by +0.108; on the 94 induced rows (72 errors, 22 correct) it is **behind by 0.191** (just excludes 0) and at chance (0.533, interval 0.370-0.691). They cancel in the pooled AUROC. So the sentence "Qwen does not beat ModernBERT on real rows" is only true for the pooled set; **on the natural rows, which are the least artificial part of the test set, Qwen is nominally better**. The induced rows are errors created by injecting faults into real runs (D-022), so they are less typical, and with 22 correct rows their AUROC is very noisy; whether the induced shortfall is a real weakness or noise cannot be told from 94 rows. (3) Qwen's lead on the natural rows rests on one borderline interval among many (not corrected for multiple comparisons); I would call it "a hint that Qwen is better on natural errors", not a result. (4) Against the shortcut baselines: Qwen is clearly above length-only, and above tool-name-only on natural rows (+0.098), but not overall (+0.049, interval -0.007 to +0.100): the tool name alone carries a good part of the signal in this test set, as in D-029.

Operating points (recall at a false-alarm rate fixed on the correct rows of the subset): overall at 5% / 10% false alarms **Qwen 0.106 / 0.218, ModernBERT 0.229 / 0.329, GPT-5-mini 0.100 / 0.471, length-only 0.212 / 0.329**; on **natural rows Qwen 0.204 / 0.276, ModernBERT 0.133 / 0.286, GPT-5-mini 0.245 / 0.510**. The strict-threshold recall of Qwen is low overall because its scores on the induced correct rows are high (false-flag rate 0.318 at 0.5 on induced rows, against 0.170 on natural rows), which pushes the pooled threshold up. At 0.5: Qwen recall 0.429 / false-flag 0.187 (ModernBERT 0.494 / 0.197, GPT-5-mini 0.547 / 0.145).

Per class (errors of the class against all 193 correct rows; AUROC | recall at 5% / 10% false alarms | recall at 0.5), Qwen against ModernBERT v3b: Class 1 (n 30) 0.709 | 0.00 / 0.03 | 0.27 against 0.724 | 0.23 / 0.23 | 0.43; Class 2 (n 4, not readable) 0.856 against 0.861; Class 3 (18) 0.754 | 0.11 / 0.28 | 0.39 against 0.754 | 0.17 / 0.33 | 0.44; **Class 4 (19) 0.893 | 0.58 / 0.79 | 0.79 against 0.802 | 0.42 / 0.47 | 0.63**; Class 5 (20) 0.787 | 0.20 / 0.30 | 0.50 against 0.756 | 0.25 / 0.40 | 0.40; Class 6 (16, 15 of them induced) 0.724 | 0.00 / 0.00 | 0.31 against 0.780 | 0.25 / 0.38 | 0.50; Class 7 (63) 0.753 | 0.02 / 0.14 | 0.40 against 0.785 | 0.19 / 0.32 | 0.49. **No per-class paired difference excludes 0** (the largest: Class 4 +0.091, interval -0.030 to +0.208). Natural rows only, recall at 0.5, Qwen | ModernBERT | GPT-5-mini: Class 1 3/21 | 5/21 | 16/21; Class 3 6/14 | 5/14 | 9/14; **Class 4 14/16 | 10/16 | 9/16**; Class 7 23/45 | 18/45 | 17/45. GPT-5-mini is far better than both trained models on Class 1 (wrong argument value: 0.77 recall at 0.5, 0.63 at 10% false alarms), which is the class where a model that cannot read the conversation's facts is weakest. Classes 5 and 6 have 1 natural error each: nothing to read there.

Per domain (AUROC; recall at 5% / 10% false alarms; paired difference Qwen minus ModernBERT): airline Qwen 0.708 against 0.783 (-0.075, interval -0.161 to +0.000), 0.038 / 0.094 against 0.132 / 0.434; retail 0.780 against 0.763 (+0.017, interval includes 0), 0.097 / 0.222 against 0.097 / 0.361; telecom 0.828 against 0.771 (+0.057, interval -0.015 to +0.133), 0.267 / 0.400 against 0.244 / 0.289. GPT-5-mini: 0.699, 0.710, 0.734. **No per-domain difference excludes 0**; Qwen is worse in airline, better in telecom (the domain where the v3b data added the most real errors), and equal in retail.

**Step 2: ensemble, EXPLORATORY, decided after seeing the test results** (`eval/analyze_final_qwen_v3b.py ensemble`, `results/final_qwen_v3b/analysis_2_ensemble.txt`). The idea came from looking at the test numbers of both models (they are equal overall and differ by subset, step 1), so it is not a pre-registered test and **the 363 rows were used both to suggest it and to score it**. What was fixed in advance of looking at the ensemble's score: no weights (plain 50/50), no threshold (0.5 or fixed false-alarm rates), two variants only: the plain average of the two probabilities, and the average of the two rank positions over the 363 test rows (uses no labels, but is computed on the test set itself, so it cannot be applied to one row in isolation). No weight or threshold was tuned on the test set. The Spearman correlation of the two models' scores is 0.677 (0.771 on the correct rows, only 0.297 on the errors: they disagree about which errors are errors).

| 363 real rows (170 errors) | AUROC (95% CI) | recall at 5% / 10% false alarms | at 0.5: recall / false-flag |
|---|---|---|---|
| ModernBERT v3b | 0.771 (0.720-0.818) | 0.229 / 0.329 | 0.494 / 0.197 |
| Qwen2.5-1.5B | 0.765 (0.714-0.814) | 0.106 / 0.218 | 0.429 / 0.187 |
| ensemble, average | 0.780 (0.730-0.825) | 0.124 / 0.312 | 0.465 / 0.161 |
| ensemble, rank-average | 0.786 (0.736-0.831) | 0.147 / 0.282 | (no probability) |

| 269 natural rows (98 errors) | AUROC (95% CI) | recall at 5% / 10% false alarms | at 0.5 |
|---|---|---|---|
| ModernBERT v3b | 0.753 (0.699-0.807) | 0.133 / 0.286 | 0.398 / 0.187 |
| Qwen2.5-1.5B | 0.803 (0.753-0.853) | 0.204 / 0.276 | 0.469 / 0.170 |
| ensemble, average | 0.785 (0.733-0.838) | 0.133 / 0.337 | 0.480 / 0.152 |
| ensemble, rank-average | 0.791 (0.740-0.842) | 0.153 / 0.276 | |

Paired bootstrap (ensemble minus the single model; 2,000 resamples): **overall, average minus ModernBERT +0.009 (-0.018 to +0.034), rank-average +0.015 (-0.010 to +0.038): neither excludes 0**; minus Qwen +0.015 and +0.021 (both include 0). **Natural rows: average minus ModernBERT +0.032 (+0.004 to +0.060), rank-average +0.038 (+0.013 to +0.063): both exclude 0; but both are below Qwen alone** (-0.017 and -0.012, intervals include 0). Induced rows: ModernBERT 0.724, Qwen 0.533, average 0.676, rank-average 0.674 (22 correct rows).

**Reading.** The ensemble does not give a reliable gain: overall it is +0.01 to +0.02 AUROC over the better single model with intervals that include 0; on natural rows it beats ModernBERT alone only because Qwen alone is better there and the ensemble sits between the two; at the strict operating points (5% false alarms) it is worse than ModernBERT alone overall (0.124 and 0.147 against 0.229) and not better than Qwen alone on natural rows (0.133 and 0.153 against 0.204). What it does do: at 0.5 the average has the lowest false-flag rate of the three (0.161) at a recall between the two models (0.465). The two models catch different errors at their own 10%-false-alarm thresholds (ModernBERT 56 of 170, Qwen 37; **both 17, only ModernBERT 39, only Qwen 20, neither 94**): Qwen catches more of Class 4 (only-Qwen 8 against only-ModernBERT 2, both 7), ModernBERT more of Class 7 (17 against 6, both 3), Class 1 (6 against 0) and Class 6 (6 against 0). That complementarity is a reason to look at a combination again with a new test set; it is not shown to pay off on this one. The ensemble costs both models' inference (Qwen on a GPU), so it would need a clear gain to be worth running; none is shown. It is **not** in the README as a recommended model; it is in the results table marked exploratory (step 5).

**Step 3: the synthetic-real gap** (`eval/analyze_final_qwen_v3b.py gap`, `results/final_qwen_v3b/analysis_3_gap.txt`, now from the prediction files; the figures equal those of the earlier report-only pass, `eval/synth_real_gap.py`). AUROC (95% CI):

| | ModernBERT v3b | Qwen2.5-1.5B |
|---|---|---|
| **Real test, 363 rows** | 0.771 (0.720-0.818) | 0.765 (0.714-0.814) |
| natural rows (269) | 0.753 (0.699-0.807) | 0.803 (0.753-0.853) |
| induced rows (94) | 0.724 (0.606-0.832) | 0.533 (0.370-0.691) |
| **test_synth, 374 rows (synthetic Classes 2 and 6)** | 0.756 (0.710-0.803) | **1.000** (0.999-1.000) |
| **Validation, 749 rows** | 0.927 (0.908-0.946) | 0.982 (0.971-0.990) |
| validation: BFCL (398) | 0.913 (0.877-0.946) | 0.978 (0.955-0.995) |
| validation: CriticTool (148) | 0.983 (0.962-0.997) | 1.000 |
| validation: tau2 synthetic, state flips, gold (111) | 0.688 (0.588-0.775) | 0.913 (0.858-0.960) |
| validation: real-origin natural (88; 17 errors) | 0.960 (0.893-1.000) | 0.976 (0.920-1.000) |
| validation: real-origin, all (92; 20 errors) | 0.962 (0.907-0.999) | 0.978 (0.931-1.000) |
| validation real-origin minus real test | +0.192 | +0.214 |
| test_synth minus real test | -0.014 | +0.235 |

*What this supports, and what it does not.* **Supported:** (1) The larger model fits the synthetic and external errors essentially perfectly (test_synth 1.000, CriticTool 1.000, BFCL 0.978, the tau2 synthetic group 0.913) where ModernBERT does not (0.756, 0.983, 0.913, 0.688). The synthetic errors are structural (a stale value, a swallowed error) and numerous in training, so capacity pays there. (2) On the human-reviewed real test rows the two models are the same overall (0.765 against 0.771, paired difference -0.006, interval -0.052 to +0.037), although the larger one is a different, much bigger model with a longer run; on the natural rows it is ahead by +0.049 (borderline, step 1) and on the induced rows behind by 0.191. (3) **Both models score about 0.96 to 0.98 on held-out real-origin rows of the validation split and 0.77 on the real test rows; the gap is as large for the model with 10 times the parameters (+0.21) as for the small one (+0.19).** On the natural rows only (where the models differ), validation 0.960 and 0.976 against test 0.753 and 0.803: +0.21 and +0.17. Capacity moved the synthetic AUROC by +0.24 and the real-test AUROC by about 0, and it did not close the validation-test gap. **This is consistent with the limit being the real labeled data rather than the model's capacity**, and the project's own history points the same way for the one thing that was varied inside a model: ModernBERT's real-test AUROC rose with the number of real training errors, 0.667 with 16 (D-025), 0.732 with 94 (D-026, REAL_WEIGHT = 3), 0.771 with 220 and tool balancing (D-028), while a 1.5B model trained on the same 220 reaches 0.765.
**Not shown, and why this is "consistent with" and not "shown":** (a) one pair of models, one recipe each (different epochs, 3 against 2; different input handling; different pretraining), one 363-row test set; the intervals are about +-0.05. (b) "Real labeled data" is two things here, **how many** real errors there are and **how their labels are made**: the real-origin training and validation labels come from the same LLM-assisted localization pipeline (D-026, D-028: proposals from gpt-5-mini, gated by rules), while the test labels are human-reviewed (D-022). A model can learn the habits of the labeling pipeline, which would make validation easy and test hard whatever the number of examples; this experiment cannot separate quantity from label process. (c) The real-origin validation set is 92 rows with 20 errors (the interval 0.93 to 1.00 is wide, though far from 0.77); its correct rows are earlier calls of the same runs as the errors, which may make them easier to tell apart than the test's correct rows. (d) The test rows are drawn from other models' runs (gpt-4.1 and similar agents in the three domains) and from induced faults; a distribution difference between validation and test, not label noise, could also produce the gap. (e) The ModernBERT sequence (0.667, 0.732, 0.771) changed more than the count of real errors between runs (the real-weighting, the tool balancing, the policy block), so it is a trend, not a controlled dose-response. **The sentence I would stand behind:** "Making the model 10 times larger fitted the synthetic errors perfectly and did not improve the real-test AUROC overall; the gap between held-out validation rows and human-reviewed test rows is the same for both. The evidence points to the amount and the labeling of real data, not to the model's size, as the main limit; this was not tested in isolation."

**Step 4: speed and cost per call** (`eval/speed_cost.py`, `results/final_qwen_v3b/speed_cost.json`; the 363 test rows). **I re-ran the CPU measurement today to check it** (a second full pass over the 363 rows, same machine): p50 0.69 s, p95 2.29 s, mean 0.87 s, against 0.68, 2.29 and 0.86 in the first pass, so the figures reproduce within about 2%.

| scorer | where it ran | latency per call | cost per call |
|---|---|---|---|
| **ModernBERT v3b** (149M, fp32, `models/final_v3b/`) | **Mac, Apple M5 (10 cores), 4 torch threads, CPU only, one row at a time; measured here on all 363 rows** (formatting + tokenizing + forward) | **p50 0.68 s, p95 2.29 s, mean 0.86 s**, max 2.59 s; the half of the rows up to the median length (1,863 tokens) 0.31 s on average, the longer half 1.43 s; formatting and tokenizing 4.5 ms; the fp32 probabilities differ from the saved GPU ones by 0.0005 on average, 0.006 at most | no per-call charge (own hardware) |
| ModernBERT v3b | Kaggle T4, fp16, length-sorted batches | **0.037 s per row** (26.9 rows/s; the validation pass of the training run, 749 rows, 27.8 s) | free tier, not priced |
| **Qwen2.5-1.5B** (4-bit + LoRA) | Kaggle T4, fp16, batch 2, length-sorted | **0.224 s per row** (4.46 rows/s; the epoch-2 validation pass of `qwen_v3b`, 749 rows, 167.8 s, in `docs/qwen_v3b_run_log.txt`): **6 times ModernBERT's time per row on the same GPU** | free tier, not priced. CPU latency of Qwen: **not measured** (the 4-bit weights need a GPU; I did not download the 3 GB base model to run it in fp32 on the CPU; the per-token cost is roughly 10 times ModernBERT's, so several seconds per row) |
| GPT-5-mini | OpenAI API, the recorded latency of each call (Phase 0 and the judge run) | **p50 3.93 s, p95 8.37 s**, mean 4.49 s (network and queueing included) | **$0.00117** per call ($0.42 for the 363 rows; mean 3,144 input and 191 output tokens at $0.25 / $2.00 per 1M tokens, `eval/pricing.json`) |
| GPT-5-nano | same | **p50 2.88 s, p95 5.22 s**, mean 3.33 s | **$0.00026** per call ($0.094 for the 363 rows; 3,144 input and 254 output tokens at $0.05 / $0.40 per 1M) |

Cross-check against `results/phase0_baseline.json` (the Phase 0 run on 210 other rows): mini p50 3.59 s, p95 5.68 s, $0.00122 per call; nano p50 3.35 s, p95 6.34 s, $0.00026 per call: the same order and the same costs. **What this does and does not say.** The CPU row is a real single-request latency; the T4 rows are **throughput of a batch, not the latency of one request** (one request alone on a T4 would take longer per row than the amortized figure, and I have no measurement of it); the judge latencies are wall-clock times of the API calls at the time they were made, with concurrent workers, and move with the API's load; the judge cost counts input and output tokens only. **Eval-only timings of the Kaggle run `qwen_v3b_eval` were not available** (its log was not provided and the saved notebook page has no timestamps); the T4 figures are the validation passes inside the two training runs, which score the same kind of rows. Orders of magnitude: ModernBERT on a laptop CPU is about 6 times faster at the median than GPT-5-mini's API call and 4 times faster than nano's, at no per-call fee; the 363 test rows cost $0.42 with the mini; Qwen needs a GPU and is 6 times slower than ModernBERT on one, for the same overall AUROC.

**Step 5: the final README results section** (`README.md`, "Results on the final test set"; numbers from `eval/analyze_final_qwen_v3b.py readme`, `results/final_qwen_v3b/analysis_5_readme.txt`, speed and cost from step 4). One main table on the 363 real rows with ModernBERT v3b, Qwen, the average ensemble (italic, marked exploratory), GPT-5-mini, GPT-5-nano, length-only and tool-name-only: AUROC overall with CI, natural-row AUROC, induced-row AUROC (added: it is where Qwen and ModernBERT differ most, and omitting it would make the natural-row column misleading), recall at 5% and 10% false alarms, and latency / cost per call. The final_v2 row of the previous table is replaced by one sentence (0.732 overall, 0.715 natural). Below it: per-class recall at 0.5 for the five scorers with n total and natural, the false-flag rates, and the notes: the overall tie of the two models and where they differ (natural +0.049, induced -0.191, both hints); "beats GPT-5-mini" not significant for either model (+0.052 and +0.046 overall; natural +0.059 and +0.108), GPT-5-mini better at 10% false alarms and on Class 1, at $0.0012 and 4 s per call; the exploratory ensemble labeled as decided after seeing the test results, with no gain shown; the synthetic-real gap with its limits; the small test set and the optimism of choosing among runs; Classes 2 and 6 mostly induced, Class 2 with 4 rows; the length shortcut. The opening paragraph of the README and the Models section were updated to say that the earlier 0.65-0.67 figures are those of the first 102-row test set. **What I changed from the request:** the table has an induced column that was not asked for; the rank-average ensemble is in the text, not the table (one ensemble row, the one with a probability and a 0.5 threshold). **Not in the README:** the per-domain table (in D-030 step 1: no Qwen-ModernBERT difference excludes 0; airline -0.075, retail +0.017, telecom +0.057).

**Summary of D-030.** (1) Qwen2.5-1.5B on the real test set: AUROC 0.765, equal to ModernBERT v3b's 0.771 (paired difference -0.006, -0.052 to +0.037); ahead on natural rows (+0.049, borderline), behind on induced rows (-0.191); not significantly better than GPT-5-mini (+0.046), significantly better than GPT-5-nano and the length shortcut. (2) The average ensemble gives +0.009 overall over ModernBERT (interval includes 0) and is not recommended. (3) Qwen fits the synthetic rows perfectly (test_synth 1.000) and does not do better on real ones; the validation-to-test gap is about 0.2 for both models: consistent with real labeled data, not capacity, as the limit; not shown in isolation. (4) ModernBERT v3b runs at 0.68 s median on a laptop CPU, 6 times faster than Qwen on a T4 per row and 4 to 6 times faster than the judge calls, at no per-call cost; so **ModernBERT v3b remains the model to keep** (same accuracy, a fraction of the cost, no GPU), and Qwen's natural-row lead is a reason to test it again on more natural errors, not to switch. (5) What would change this: more human-reviewed natural errors for the test set (the 98 natural errors cannot separate +0.05), and a controlled test of the labeling-process question in step 3.

---

## D-031 — Portfolio packaging: license audit, inference package, writeup, model card, demo plan (2026-11-22)

No API call and no Kaggle run. The final model is ModernBERT v3b (`models/final_v3b/`, D-029). The five deliverables are done in the order license audit (3), inference package (4), writeup (1), model card (2), demo plan (5), because the writeup and the card need the licence facts and the exact input format. The earlier sections of this entry are written as each step is committed.

**Step 3 (done first): license audit, `docs/licenses.md`.** Read: `data/raw/tau2-bench/LICENSE` (MIT, Sierra Research 2025) and its `pyproject.toml` (`license = "MIT"`); `data/raw/bfcl-gorilla/LICENSE` (Apache-2.0) and its README ("Gorilla is Apache 2.0 licensed"); fetched today (the Hugging Face cache holds configs and tokenizers, not model cards): the cards of `answerdotai/ModernBERT-base` (`license: apache-2.0`), `Qwen/Qwen2.5-1.5B` (`license: apache-2.0`, `license_link` to its LICENSE, which is the Apache text), `gorilla-llm/Berkeley-Function-Calling-Leaderboard` (Apache-2.0; "all the models, and data used to train the models are released under Apache 2.0") and `chocckaka/CriticTool-Dataset` (**the card is only the line `license: apache-2.0`**; the GitHub repository shows no license, per D-019, not re-checked). Verdicts: (a) the fine-tuned weights: **probably publishable** under Apache-2.0 terms (base model license kept, changes stated); (b) the 363-row test set with τ²-bench text: **probably publishable with the MIT notice**; (c) the training data: **not as it stands**. Not settled, and flagged instead of guessed: **(1)** the OpenAI and Anthropic terms for the model output inside our data (the τ²-bench transcripts are written by gpt-4.1, gpt-4.1-mini, o4-mini and claude-3-7-sonnet agents and a gpt-4.1 user simulator; our induced runs, the synthetic negatives and the LLM-proposed labels came from gpt-5-nano / gpt-5-mini / gpt-4.1-mini): **not read**; **(2)** CriticTool: Apache-2.0 only on a card that says nothing else; **(3)** personal-looking data (names, addresses, card values) inside τ²-bench rows: not verified to be synthetic; **(4)** the git history still contains the 37 MB `induced_runs.jsonl` (the repository has no remote and was never pushed, checked); publish from a fresh history; **(5)** a search of the history of every non-`.jsonl` file for OpenAI-key patterns found none (`.jsonl` files not searched; run a scanner before publishing). The license field of the model card stays TODO, as asked.

**Step 4: inference package, `verifier/predict.py`.** `load(model_dir)` and `score(conversation, candidate_call, tool_schemas, domain, operating_point)` returning a `Verdict` (probability of "incorrect", `flagged`, the threshold used, the operating point, **the exact input text the model read** including the policy line, and the token count). It contains the notebook's formatting code (`format_input_v2` with the policy block, the evidence rule, the tightening ladder to 4,096 tokens) copied and made standalone; `verifier/data/` holds the 18 policy snippets (`policy_snippets.json`, equal to the dict in the notebook: checked), the three τ²-bench tool-schema texts (one per domain, taken from the test rows; used when `tool_schemas=None`) and `thresholds.json`. `tool_schemas` may be the dataset's text, a list of OpenAI-style or plain function descriptions (converted to the same text; the prerequisite `# Requires:` lines of the τ² schemas are only in the bundled text), or None. `turns_from_openai` converts chat messages. CLI: `python -m verifier.predict --input call.json [--operating-point fa05|fa10|default|<number>] [--show-input]`, `--example` prints an input file. README has a "Use the verifier" section with a 6-line example.

*Operating points* (`verifier/data/thresholds.json`, computed from `results/final_v3b/test_predictions.jsonl`, rule: flag when probability >= threshold): **fa05: threshold 0.895** (4.7% of the 193 correct rows flagged, recall 0.229); **fa10: threshold 0.731** (9.8% flagged, recall 0.329); default 0.5 (19.7% flagged, recall 0.494). **They are in-sample**: set and measured on the same 363 rows that also chose between models, and the induced correct rows score high (D-027), so on other traffic the share of correct calls flagged will differ; the saved scores are from the Kaggle GPU in fp16.

*Test* (`python -m verifier.check_against_saved`, CPU, torch 2.15, transformers 5.24): **(1) input text: all 363 test rows give character-for-character the text of the notebook's `format_input_v2`** (0 of 363 differ; the notebook cells are run on `data/kaggle_upload_v3b`); **(2) scores: 12 test rows** (the 6 shortest, 27 to 173 tokens, and the 6 longest, 4,061 to 4,096 tokens; telecom, airline, retail; Classes 1, 3, 5, 6, 7 and correct) **against the saved GPU probabilities: maximum difference 0.0013, mean 0.0003**; the bundled-schema path (`tool_schemas=None`) works. The CLI and the converters were run on a hand-made example.

*What the hand-made probes showed (two or three, not a study; recorded because they are the first thing a user will try):* a clean "cancel a pending order after the user confirmed the order id and reason" conversation scores 0.02; the same call with a shorter confirmation ("Yes." after "Shall I cancel it?") scores 0.88 and is flagged; the same call with a 3-character order id (`#W1`) scores 0.9999; "cancel a delivered order" (a clear policy violation), with the status in the lookup result, scores 0.015 and is **not** flagged. So the score reacts to phrasing and to the format of ids, misses a clear violation that the model's Class 7 recall (0.40 at 0.5 for Qwen, 0.49 for ModernBERT, D-030) already says it often misses, and hand-made examples are outside the training distribution. The README example is the clean one; the model card says this.

**Step 1: writeup, `docs/writeup.md`.** About 3,250 words of prose (3,670 with the tables), for technical founders and ML hiring managers; sections as asked: the problem; the taxonomy with the Class 7 redefinition and sub-reasons; the data (τ²-bench and BFCL, synthetic corruptions with paired controls, induced errors by fault injection, mind-change users and value changes, LLM-assisted localization with human review, the 363-row test set and its labeling rules); what went wrong (the Phase 0 judge result on bad heuristic labels, the length and tool-name shortcuts including `cancel_reservation`, the 11% visibility, the 16 real errors, the induced-run signature); results (the README table, operating points, per class, speed and cost); the synthetic-real gap with the careful reading of D-030; limitations and next steps; and a table mapping each topic to its source files and D-entries. **How the numbers were checked:** every figure was compared with the D-entry or result file it is attributed to while writing (the table and per-class numbers are those of the README and `results/final_qwen_v3b/analysis_*.txt`); I did not recompute them for the writeup. Choices: it says "we"; it states that the ensemble was decided after seeing the test results; it says the thresholds shipped with the model are in-sample; it says nothing is published yet and refers to `docs/licenses.md`. **Two things I wrote that go beyond the D-entries:** the sentence that the first synthetic Class 7 generator "made the opposite artifact" to the test set (D-009 says its negatives were shorter than positives while real Class 7 rows are longer) and the sentence that the shelved text normalization "would move induced scores only a little" (the D-027 ablation, which was a test of the idea on final_v2, not of the trained fix). It links to `docs/demo_plan.md`, written in step 5.

**Step 2: model card, `docs/model_card.md`** (Hugging Face format: YAML front matter with `base_model`, `pipeline_tag`, tags and a `model-index` entry with the overall test AUROC 0.771; **`license: other`, `license_name: TODO`, `license_link: TODO`**, and TODO for developer and citation, as asked). Sections: a "read this first" paragraph (not significantly better than the GPT-5-mini judge, a triage signal, not a gate); model details (architecture, files with the SHA-256 of D-029, the training run: Kaggle T4, 3 epochs, lr 2e-5, effective batch 16, 6,780 s, seed 42); intended use (pre-execution checks of tool calls in customer-service agents of the τ²-bench kind; route high scores to a human or a retry) and out-of-scope use (unattended blocking, other domains, explaining the reason, security use, treating the score as calibrated); the exact input format and how to call it (the `verifier` package, the block layout of `format_input_v2` with the policy block, the evidence and tightening rules, the three operating points with the caveat that they are in-sample); the training data (7,154 rows, 2,570 errors, 220 real, `REAL_WEIGHT = 3`, sources, no test overlap, the open question about model-generated text); evaluation with intervals (the README table plus ModernBERT-only paired differences, per class, per domain, test_synth, validation, speed); known failure modes (misses at strict settings and weaker than the judge on Class 1 and 3; shortcuts in the test set; induced rows; phrasing and ID-format sensitivity from the D-031 probes, labeled anecdotal; agent reply style; evidence cut for 26 of 363 test rows; labels and their rules; small reused test set; not tested: other domains, adversarial input, fairness). **Checked:** the checksum, thresholds (0.895 and 0.731, equal to the evaluation report), counts and intervals against D-029, D-030 and the training log; **not verified:** that the YAML passes the Hugging Face validator (`license: other` with TODO names is valid syntax, but I did not run the hub's check), and the claim "0.04 s per row on a T4" is the amortized validation pass, not a single-request latency (said so in the card).

**Step 5: demo plan, `docs/demo_plan.md` (plan only; nothing built).** A Gradio app for Hugging Face Spaces on CPU: example conversations (a real test row it gets right, one it misses, one false alarm, a hand-written clean one), editable conversation and candidate call, a domain dropdown, and a result with the probability, the decision at a selectable operating point (`fa05` 0.895, `fa10` 0.731, 0.5), **the exact text the model read with the policy line highlighted** (it comes straight from `Verdict.input_text`), the token count and a notice when the ladder cut evidence, plus a fixed note on what the score means (not calibrated, phrasing-sensitive, AUROC 0.771, not significantly better than the judge). Effort: **about 1 day** for a working version, **+0.5 to 1 day** to polish. Optional "agent with a gate": **variant A**, a replay of a recorded test-set run with per-call scores, blocked calls marked and our labels shown next to the decisions (blocking is annotation only, said on the page): **about 1 to 1.5 days** on top; **variant B**, a live τ²-bench agent whose flagged calls are blocked and returned to the agent, measured on gate on / off: **3 to 5 days**, needs keys and money (the induced runs cost about $0.008 each, D-019) and is not suitable for the free Space; at the 10% false-alarm setting it would block 1 in 3 errors and 1 in 10 good calls, so the effect may be small or negative. Recommended order: settle the license and the example policy first (the open points of `docs/licenses.md` decide whether real test conversations may be shown), then the basic Space, then variant A. **Not verified:** the free-tier specification of Spaces (2 vCPU, 16 GB is from memory) and the CPU latency on it (the 0.68 s median is from a 10-core Mac).
